Featured Effects1. Orbit Cards# CES style instruction — X01 Orbit Cards

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X01 Orbit Cards** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — Screenshots, photos or proof cards circle the subject like satellites. Perspective is simulated with scale, brightness and blur: on the far side cards shrink, dim and pass behind the speaker; on the near side they swell and sharpen. With a subject matte the cards truly pass behind; without one, a 2D arc keeps every card in front and fades it out where it would disappear, so the illusion never breaks.

### X01 Orbit Cards

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:04.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Supplied cards orbit the speaker on a tilted elliptical path, slipping behind them on the far side and swinging large and bright across the front.
- **Parameters:** Render mode: Auto (use mask if available, else 2D); Cards: 5 cards; Time per revolution: 6 s; Orbit tilt: 18 deg; Orbit radius: 40%; Direction: Clockwise (seen from above); Orbit ring guide: on
- **Steps:**
  1. PREREQUISITE CHECK: cards passing behind the speaker requires the subject to be cut out so the far-side cards can be composited between the background and the person, which needs a clean subject matte (supplied, or from an external segmentation step checked for edge flicker). If a usable matte exists, build the full version; otherwise use the 2D version — keep every card in front of the subject: shift the ellipse up so its near side passes across the chest/shoulder line (never the eyes or mouth), and on the far half of the orbit fade each card to 0% and shrink it to 0.6× instead of drawing it behind the subject. Tell the user which version was rendered.
  2. Layer order (matte version): background plate → far-side cards and far half of the ring → subject cut out by the matte → near-side cards and near half of the ring → captions. Re-sort each card every frame from its angle (sin θ < 0 = far side).
  3. Geometry: centre the ellipse on the subject's upper chest (≈ 52% of frame height); horizontal radius 40% of frame width (432px), vertical radius 133px (from the 18° tilt). Card i sits at θᵢ = 2π·i/5 + 2π·t/6s, moving clockwise: x = cx + R·cos θ, y = cy + r·sin θ.
  4. Depth cues: map d = (sin θ + 1)/2 to scale 0.62 → 1.12, brightness 0.55 → 1, blur 0.35% → 0% of frame width and shadow strength; rotate each card on Y by −cos θ × 25° so it faces along the orbit.
  5. Cards: the supplied screenshots/photos at ≈ 24% of frame width, 3% corner radius, 1px light border, soft shadow. Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims. Draw a 0.25%-wide ellipse guide at 18% opacity, split into far and near halves so each half follows the same layering as the cards.
  6. Timing: ease the angular speed in over 12 frames and out over the last 12; 6s per revolution gives ≈ 0.8 turns across 4.5s. Keep speed constant in between and verify no card crosses the face.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte, Photos, Social comment
- **Fallback:** Without a subject matte, run the 2D arc: cards travel a front-only arc around the upper body and fade out at the ends instead of passing behind. If segmentation is attempted but flickers or tears hair edges, fall back to 2D rather than shipping a broken matte. With fewer than three cards, use B02 Floating Screenshot Cards instead.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.

2. infinite Content Wall
# CES style instruction — X02 Infinite Content Wall

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X02 Infinite Content Wall** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — A seamless, tilted wall of media — screenshots, thumbnails, comments, results — streams upward and downward in alternating columns, creating a sense of volume and momentum. The speaker stays in front: cut out with a matte for the full effect, or framed in a floating inset card when no matte exists. It reads instantly as 'there is a lot of this', ideal for portfolios, testimonials or catalogues.

### X02 Infinite Content Wall

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:05.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Columns of supplied screenshots and tiles scroll endlessly in alternating directions on a tilted wall behind the speaker, filling the frame.
- **Parameters:** Render mode: Auto (use mask if available, else 2D); Columns: 4; Scroll speed: 12%; Wall tilt: 16 deg; Alternate column direction: on; Wall dim: 40%; Tile shape: Mixed (portrait + square)
- **Steps:**
  1. PREREQUISITE CHECK: placing the speaker in front of a wall that replaces their background requires a subject matte (or footage shot on a clean, keyable background), which needs a clean subject matte (supplied, or from an external segmentation step checked for edge flicker). If a usable matte exists, build the full version; otherwise use the 2D version — keep the original footage intact inside a floating inset card (≈ 64% of frame width, 4% corner radius, soft 6% shadow, centred in the lower half) and run the wall behind the card. Tell the user which version was rendered.
  2. Tiles: crop the supplied screenshots/photos to a mix of 9:16 and 1:1 tiles without distorting them. If there are fewer tiles than slots, repeat the set but never place the same tile next to itself. Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims.
  3. Wall: 4 columns with 1.5% gaps filling 130% of the frame, inside a container with CSS perspective ≈ 1944px rotated rotateX(16°) rotateZ(−9°) so the wall recedes upward.
  4. Seamless scroll: duplicate each column's tile set and translate it by exactly one set height, then wrap (modulo) — no visible jump. Base speed 12% of frame height per second (≈ 230px/s), with ±15% variation per column and odd columns moving in the opposite direction.
  5. Darken the wall by 40% plus a radial vignette so the subject stays dominant; add a soft contact shadow behind the speaker to separate planes.
  6. Bring the wall in over 15 frames (scale 1.1 → 1, opacity 0 → 1) and keep the speaker's face and any captions clear of high-contrast tiles.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte, Photos, Social comment
- **Fallback:** Without a matte, use the 2D inset-card version: the untouched footage floats in a rounded card over the scrolling wall. With fewer than 6 distinct tiles, reduce to 2–3 columns so repetition is less obvious.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.

3. Frame Break
# CES style instruction — X03 Frame Break

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X03 Frame Break** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — The footage first plays inside a device or picture frame, then a foreground element pushes past the frame edge: the part beyond the boundary is drawn in front of the bezel while the rest stays inside the screen. The broken boundary creates a playful 3D pop that stops the scroll. True frame break on live footage needs a subject matte; without one, the same pop is done with a supplied transparent cutout such as a product, sticker or still of the subject.

### X03 Frame Break

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** The speaker or a supplied cutout bursts out of a stylised phone frame — rising past the bezel so part of them sits in front of the frame.
- **Parameters:** Render mode: Auto (use mask if available, else 2D); Frame type: Phone frame; Break edge: Top edge; Break distance: 35%; Frame tilt: 8 deg; Overlap shadow: on; Easing: Back out (overshoot)
- **Steps:**
  1. PREREQUISITE CHECK: having the speaker extend past the frame edge requires their matte so the outside portion can be drawn over the bezel, which needs a clean subject matte (supplied, or from an external segmentation step checked for edge flicker). If a usable matte exists, build the full version; otherwise use the 2D version — use a supplied transparent cutout (product PNG, sticker, logo or a still of the subject already cut out) as the breaking element; the live footage stays entirely inside the screen and the cutout bursts out of its top edge. Tell the user which version was rendered.
  2. Composite a stylised, unbranded phone (≈ 62% of frame width, thick dark bezel, 12% corner radius) over a dark, blurred copy of the footage, tilted 8° (rotateX 5°, rotate −3°) with CSS perspective ≈ 2160px. The source footage plays inside the screen area.
  3. Split the breaking element into two copies sharing one transform: copy A clipped to the screen rectangle (behind the bezel), copy B (matte-cut or cutout) clipped to everything beyond the top screen edge and layered above the bezel. Together they read as one element leaving the screen.
  4. Animation: over 17 frames the element rises until 35% of its height is past the top edge, using a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)); hold, then settle back 11% at the end of the range.
  5. Cast a soft shadow from copy B onto the bezel/frame (offset 1% of frame width, blur 2.5%, 40% opacity) — this shadow is what sells the depth.
  6. Verify the boundary on every frame: no halo or missing hair where copies A and B meet, and the face is fully readable. Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte, Product image, Photos
- **Fallback:** Without a subject matte, break the frame with a supplied transparent cutout (product, logo, sticker or a still cutout photo of the subject) instead of the live footage. If no cutout asset exists either, use L04 Floating Device Frame with a punch-in rather than faking the break.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.

4. Headline Slam# CES style instruction — H01 Headline Slam

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **H01 Headline Slam** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Hooks** — The first frame carries a bold 2–5 word promise. The headline enters from a larger scale, overshoots slightly and settles in under half a second, giving the opening a physical 'impact' without covering the face. It tells a muted viewer exactly what the video delivers before the first sentence ends.

### H01 Headline Slam

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:01.80 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** An oversized headline lands in the opening second with a controlled scale overshoot and settles above the speaker.
- **Parameters:** Headline text: (not set); Duration: 1.8 s; Scale: 2.2x; Easing: Back out (overshoot); Text size: 11%; Text position: Upper third; Text colour: #F4F2EB; Accent colour: #D7FF5F; Impact micro-shake: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Create a headline overlay layer above the source footage from 00:00.00 to 00:01.80 (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Headline copy: derive a 2–5 word promise strictly from the speaker's first sentence in the transcript (English). Never introduce a claim the speaker does not make.
  3. Typeset in Space Grotesk (bold/black weight), cap height ≈ 11% of frame width, colour #F4F2EB; render the single most important word in #D7FF5F. Break into at most two balanced lines.
  4. Entrance: scale from 2.2x to 1.0 over ~13 frames using a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)); opacity 0→1 within the first 3 frames; add 1–2 frames of directional motion blur on the way in.
  5. On the landing frame apply a 3–4 frame positional shake (≤ 1.2% of frame width, decaying) to the headline layer only — never to the face.
  6. Place in the upper third of the frame. Keep the speaker's eyes and mouth unobstructed and respect the platform safe zone.
  7. Hold for the remainder of 1.8s, then exit over 6 frames (opacity → 0, scale → 0.96) as the first sentence continues.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If no transcript is available, ask for the headline text or transcribe the first 5 seconds locally before writing it. If brand fonts are missing, use Inter Black / Space Grotesk Bold.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.

5. Karaoke Follow# CES style instruction — C01 Karaoke Follow

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C01 Karaoke Follow** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Dialogue is split into short pages of a few words. The whole page is readable in advance, and a colour fill travels through it in lock-step with the voice, so the eye always knows where the speaker is. Because nothing scales or jumps, the effect feels precise rather than busy, and muted viewers can follow the rhythm of the delivery, not just the words.

### C01 Karaoke Follow

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** Short caption pages sit over the speaker and each word fills with the accent colour at the exact moment it is spoken.
- **Parameters:** Caption typeface: Bold sans (Space Grotesk / Inter Black); Text size: 7.5%; Highlight behaviour: Progressive fill (colour wipes through the word); Words on screen: 5; Spoken-word colour: #D7FF5F; Upcoming-word colour: #F4F2EB; Safe-zone offset: 18%; All caps: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a karaoke caption layer across every spoken section of the video on its own track above all footage and graphics (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Paging: group words into pages of at most 5 words and 30 characters per line (one line; two only when a page would exceed it), breaking at phrase boundaries — never between a number and its unit or inside a name. A page appears 2 frames before its first word and is replaced (hard cut, no fade) when the next page's first word starts; hold the last page at most 18 frames into silence, then clear.
  4. Highlight: every word starts in #F4F2EB and fills with #D7FF5F from left to right over its own spoken duration (render a #D7FF5F copy of the word clipped by an inset that tracks (frame − startFrame) / (endFrame − startFrame)), so the fill follows the syllables. Spoken words stay #D7FF5F; nothing scales or moves.
  5. Typeset in Space Grotesk at Black/ExtraBold weight (fallback: Inter Black), cap height ≈ 7.5% of frame width (≈ 81px), ALL CAPS with +2% tracking, with a 0.12em dark stroke or a 30% black drop shadow so both colours hold 4.5:1 contrast on bright footage.
  6. Placement: anchor the caption block's bottom edge 18% of frame height (346px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
  7. Render with Remotion: a <KaraokeCaptions /> component reads captions.json ([{ text, startMs, endMs }] grouped into pages) and derives the active word from useCurrentFrame() / 30. Verify by scrubbing three random sentences — the fill must start within ±1 frame of each audible word onset.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If no transcript is supplied and local transcription is not possible, ask the user for an SRT/VTT. With only line-level timing, render whole-line captions without word highlights rather than spacing fake word times evenly — invented timing visibly drifts out of sync.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.

6. Screenshot Punch# CES style instruction — P01 Screenshot Punch

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **P01 Screenshot Punch** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Proof** — The moment the speaker makes a claim, the supporting screenshot punches into frame: it enters slightly oversized, settles to its final width with a short overshoot and casts a layered shadow that separates it from the footage. The background dims or blurs just enough to make the capture the only thing to read. Because it lands on the exact word that references it, the screenshot reads as evidence rather than decoration.

### P01 Screenshot Punch

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** A real screenshot drops in over the speaker at a precise scale, lands with a soft overshoot and a deep shadow, holds long enough to read, then clears.
- **Parameters:** Screenshot file: (not set); Duration: 3 s; Entrance: Drop + punch (from above, overshoot); Screenshot width: 82%; Landing tilt: -2 deg; Shadow depth: 60%; Background treatment: Dim footage 45%; Easing: Back out (overshoot)
- **Steps:**
  1. Place screenshot from the project assets (the user's supplied screenshot; if none is present, stop and ask for it — do not substitute a mock-up) on an overlay layer above the source footage from 00:00.00 to 00:03.00 (1080×1920 @ 30fps). Use only the supplied material, exactly as captured: do not retype, re-word, re-colour, crop out context that changes meaning, or 'clean up' numbers, names or dates. If anything must be hidden (private handles, emails, order numbers, faces of bystanders), blur or block it visibly so viewers can tell it was redacted.
  2. Scale it to 82% of frame width (886px), preserving its aspect ratio; if it would exceed 70% of frame height, fit by height instead. Never upscale beyond 200% of its native pixels — if it would, show it smaller rather than blurry. Round corners by ≈ 2.5% of its width.
  3. Entrance over 14 frames: enter from 12% of frame height above its rest position at scale 1.12 and land at scale 1.0, using a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)), while rotating from -5.0° to a resting tilt of -2°. Opacity reaches 1 within the first 3 frames.
  4. Shadow: two stacked drop shadows — a tight contact shadow (y offset 0.6% of frame width, blur 1.2%, 31% black) and a soft ambient one (y offset 3%, blur 8%, 37% black). Add a 1px 10% white inner stroke so dark screenshots separate from dark footage.
  5. Dim the underlying footage to 55% brightness over 9 frames as the screenshot enters, restoring it on exit.
  6. Land the punch on the exact word in the transcript where the speaker references the screenshot. Hold it fully still for at least 2.3s — long enough to read every word that matters (≈ 3 words per second); extend the range rather than cutting it short.
  7. Exit over 8 frames (scale 1.0 → 0.96, opacity → 0, ease-in). Verify on a phone-sized render that the key text in the screenshot is legible at 82% width.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If no screenshot is supplied, do not recreate, mock up or 'illustrate' the evidence — omit this beat and list it as a missing asset, or ask the user for the capture. If the screenshot is too low-resolution to read, show it smaller with a crop around the relevant area (keeping enough context to stay honest) and flag it.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.

- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.2. Pattern Interupt# CES style instruction — H02 Pattern Interrupt

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **H02 Pattern Interrupt** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Hooks** — The video begins like any other, then visibly breaks: the frame freezes, inverts or snaps to an unexpected framing while a one-word title stamps across it. The deliberate 'glitch in the expected' resets the viewer's attention just as they are about to scroll.

### H02 Pattern Interrupt

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:01.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Breaks a conventional opening with a sharp contrast — a freeze, an inverted flash, a sudden reframe or a disruptive title.
- **Parameters:** Interrupt type: Freeze + inverted flash; Interrupt word: "WAIT."; Duration: 1 s; Intensity: 70%; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Let the opening play normally for 8–15 frames (interrupt at ≈ 00:00.35), then break the pattern.
  2. At the interrupt point hold the current frame (freeze) and flash a colour-inverted copy for 2 frames at 75% opacity, then return to the frozen normal frame.
  3. Overlay the interrupt word "WAIT." in Space Grotesk black weight, accent #D7FF5F, centered, for the interrupt duration (1.0s total).
  4. Resume normal playback on the next spoken syllable; the interrupt must never cut a word in half.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If the opening has no natural pause, place the interrupt on the first breath between sentences. Inverted flashes are skipped when the user requests photosensitivity-safe output.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
3. Question First
# CES style instruction — H03 Question First

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **H03 Question First** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Hooks** — Instead of a statement, the video opens on an open loop: a question the viewer wants answered. Words appear in a measured stagger, the final question mark pulses, and the overlay wipes away to the speaker who begins answering. Curiosity carries the viewer into the first sentence.

### H03 Question First

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:02.40 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A provocative question builds word by word in kinetic type over a dimmed frame, then clears to reveal the speaker.
- **Parameters:** Question: (not set); Duration: 2.4 s; Word stagger: 0.09 s; Background dim: 65%; Text size: 8.5%; Exit reveal: Wipe up; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Open on the first frame of the source with a 65% black dim overlay (or a soft blur) for 2.4s.
  2. Write the question from the speaker's own topic as stated in the transcript; it must be a question the video genuinely answers.
  3. Reveal one word at a time with a 0.09s stagger; each word rises 4% of frame height and fades in over 8 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)). Highlight the most important word in #D7FF5F.
  4. Set in Space Grotesk, cap height ≈ 8.5% of frame width, max 3 lines, centered with balanced line breaks.
  5. After the last word, pulse the question mark (scale 1→1.15→1 over 10 frames), hold ≥ 0.6s, then exit with a wipe-up transition into the speaker's first answering sentence.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** If the speaker never poses or answers a question, ask the user for the question text instead of inventing one.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.

5. Countdown Hook
# CES style instruction — H05 Countdown Hook

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **H05 Countdown Hook** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Hooks** — Large numerals count down 3–2–1 on strict beats while the frame pushes in a little further on each one. The countdown creates a built-in promise that something is about to happen and synchronises naturally to music or a spoken '3 things…' structure.

### H05 Countdown Hook

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:02.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A three-beat countdown with escalating zoom and rhythm, landing on the first line of the video.
- **Parameters:** Count from: 3; Beat interval: 0.5 s; Zoom per beat: 5%; Sync to: Fixed interval; Final word: "GO"; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Create a countdown overlay from 3 to 1, then "GO". Each numeral occupies one beat of 0.5s (15 frames).
  2. Each numeral: Space Grotesk black, height ≈ 38% of frame, colour #D7FF5F, entering at scale 1.3 → 1.0 in 5 frames and leaving with a 3-frame fade.
  3. Escalate the underlying footage: increase scale by 5% on every beat (stepped, eased over 4 frames) and reset to 100% when the final word appears.
  4. Total duration ≈ 2.00s at fixed timing. The final word lands exactly on the first spoken syllable or the main beat drop.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Music track
- **Fallback:** Without music, use the fixed interval. If the beat interval would exceed the available opening, shorten the count rather than slowing the beat.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**

6. Split Reveal# CES style instruction — H06 Split Reveal

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **H06 Split Reveal** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Hooks** — Two offset slices of the frame — or a narrow aperture — hint at the subject before the mask resolves into the full picture. The partial view creates a tiny mystery, and the resolving motion gives the eye a clear path straight to the speaker.

### H06 Split Reveal

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:01.40 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** The opening frame starts divided or obscured, then a moving mask slides the halves together to reveal the full shot.
- **Parameters:** Mask shape: Vertical split (two halves); Duration: 1.4 s; Easing: Expo out (snappy); Initial offset: 18%; Seam colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Split the first frame into left and right halves. Start the left half shifted up by 18% and the right half shifted down by 18% of frame height.
  2. Animate the mask/slices to their natural position over 1.4s using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)); the source video keeps playing underneath (do not freeze).
  3. Draw a 0.4%-wide seam in #D7FF5F along each split edge that fades out as the halves meet.
  4. Implement as masked copies of the same video layer (Remotion: nested <AbsoluteFill> with clip-path / transform), keeping audio from a single layer to avoid phasing.
  5. The reveal completes at 00:01.40.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING)
- **Fallback:** If the speaker is off-center, align the split seam with the subject's center line rather than the frame center.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.

### H09 Contrarian Hook

Captions
Karaoke Follow# CES style instruction — C01 Karaoke Follow

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C01 Karaoke Follow** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Dialogue is split into short pages of a few words. The whole page is readable in advance, and a colour fill travels through it in lock-step with the voice, so the eye always knows where the speaker is. Because nothing scales or jumps, the effect feels precise rather than busy, and muted viewers can follow the rhythm of the delivery, not just the words.

### C01 Karaoke Follow

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** Short caption pages sit over the speaker and each word fills with the accent colour at the exact moment it is spoken.
- **Parameters:** Caption typeface: Bold sans (Space Grotesk / Inter Black); Text size: 7.5%; Highlight behaviour: Progressive fill (colour wipes through the word); Words on screen: 5; Spoken-word colour: #D7FF5F; Upcoming-word colour: #F4F2EB; Safe-zone offset: 18%; All caps: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a karaoke caption layer across every spoken section of the video on its own track above all footage and graphics (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Paging: group words into pages of at most 5 words and 30 characters per line (one line; two only when a page would exceed it), breaking at phrase boundaries — never between a number and its unit or inside a name. A page appears 2 frames before its first word and is replaced (hard cut, no fade) when the next page's first word starts; hold the last page at most 18 frames into silence, then clear.
  4. Highlight: every word starts in #F4F2EB and fills with #D7FF5F from left to right over its own spoken duration (render a #D7FF5F copy of the word clipped by an inset that tracks (frame − startFrame) / (endFrame − startFrame)), so the fill follows the syllables. Spoken words stay #D7FF5F; nothing scales or moves.
  5. Typeset in Space Grotesk at Black/ExtraBold weight (fallback: Inter Black), cap height ≈ 7.5% of frame width (≈ 81px), ALL CAPS with +2% tracking, with a 0.12em dark stroke or a 30% black drop shadow so both colours hold 4.5:1 contrast on bright footage.
  6. Placement: anchor the caption block's bottom edge 18% of frame height (346px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
  7. Render with Remotion: a <KaraokeCaptions /> component reads captions.json ([{ text, startMs, endMs }] grouped into pages) and derives the active word from useCurrentFrame() / 30. Verify by scrubbing three random sentences — the fill must start within ±1 frame of each audible word onset.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If no transcript is supplied and local transcription is not possible, ask the user for an SRT/VTT. With only line-level timing, render whole-line captions without word highlights rather than spacing fake word times evenly — invented timing visibly drifts out of sync.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**

- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.

2. Pop World Captions# CES style instruction — C02 Pop Word Captions

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C02 Pop Word Captions** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Instead of a full sentence, the viewer sees only the word or two being spoken right now. Each group arrives with a restrained scale overshoot and a few percent of upward travel, outlined for contrast, and is replaced the moment the next group starts. The constant small motion keeps eyes anchored near the face and gives fast-paced delivery a punchy, rhythmic feel without becoming chaotic.

### C02 Pop Word Captions

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** One to three words at a time pop onto the speaker's chest with a small scale overshoot and a short upward drift, then hand off to the next.
- **Parameters:** Words per pop: 1 word; Pop overshoot: 1.12x; Rise distance: 2%; Easing: Back out (overshoot); Caption typeface: Bold sans (Space Grotesk / Inter Black); Text size: 9%; Emphasis colour: #D7FF5F; Safe-zone offset: 22%
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a pop-word caption layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Grouping: show exactly one word at a time (max 14 characters); merge articles and short function words ("a", "the", "to") with the following word so nothing flashes for under 4 frames. A group appears on its first word's start frame and is replaced when the next group starts; clear after 15 frames of silence.
  4. Entrance per group: scale 0.72 → 1.12 → 1.0 and translate up from +2% of frame height to rest over 9 frames using a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)); opacity 0 → 1 in the first 2 frames. No exit animation — the next group replaces it on a hard cut.
  5. Typeset in Space Grotesk at Black/ExtraBold weight (fallback: Inter Black), cap height ≈ 9% of frame width (≈ 97px), with a solid dark outline (≈ 0.14em, paint-order stroke) for contrast. Colour the single most important word of each sentence (the noun or number the sentence is about, as spoken) in #D7FF5F — at most one emphasis per sentence.
  6. Placement: anchor the word group's bottom edge 22% of frame height (422px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
  7. Implement as a Remotion <PopWords /> component reading captions.json; compute each group's local frame (frame − startFrame) and drive scale/translate with interpolate() or spring(). Check that no group is visible for fewer than 4 frames.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** Without word timing, transcribe locally with Whisper before building this style. If that is impossible, use C06 Editorial Lower Thirds with line-level timing instead — word pops on guessed timing look visibly out of sync.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
3. Typewriter Subtitles# CES style instruction — C03 Typewriter Subtitles

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C03 Typewriter Subtitles** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Classic one- or two-line subtitles, but instead of appearing whole, the characters are revealed progressively at a pace derived from the phrase's spoken duration. The caret and the steady reveal suggest thought being written out in real time, which suits reflective, narrative or documentary delivery. The full phrase always lands before the speaker moves on, so reading never lags behind the voice.

### C03 Typewriter Subtitles

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** Each subtitle types itself on character by character with a blinking caret, paced so the phrase completes as the speaker finishes it.
- **Parameters:** Reveal speed: 24; Characters per line: 32; Lines per subtitle: 2 lines; Blinking caret: on; Backdrop: Translucent box; Caption typeface: Clean sans (Inter Semibold); Text colour: #F4F2EB; Safe-zone offset: 14%
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a typewriter subtitle layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level (grouped into phrases) timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Segment the transcript into subtitles of at most 2 lines × 32 characters, splitting at punctuation or natural pauses (≥ 250ms gaps between words). Each subtitle appears on its first word's start frame and clears 9 frames after its last word ends or when the next subtitle starts, whichever is first.
  4. Reveal: show characters progressively at 24 characters/second (1.25 frames per character), but cap the reveal so the final character lands no later than the phrase's last word start — compute revealFrames = min(chars / 24 × 30, lastWordStartFrame − firstWordStartFrame + 2). Reveal whole grapheme clusters (accents, emoji) and never re-flow already visible text: pre-compute line breaks on the full string.
  5. Caret: a thin block caret (0.08em wide, #F4F2EB) follows the last revealed character and blinks at 2Hz once the phrase is complete; remove it when the subtitle clears.
  6. Typeset in Inter Semibold (fallback: Inter Semibold), cap height ≈ 4.4% of frame width, colour #F4F2EB, centered, line-height 1.25. Place it on a translucent box (#0B0C0E at 70%, radius 1.2% of width, padding 0.5em × 0.8em) sized to the full phrase from the first frame so it never resizes while typing.
  7. Placement: anchor the subtitle block's bottom edge 14% of frame height (269px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
  8. Implement as a Remotion <TypewriterSubtitles /> component reading captions.json; slice the string with Math.floor(localFrame × 24 / 30) per subtitle.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** With only line-level SRT timing, run the reveal across each SRT cue's duration (capped at the cue end). Without any transcript, transcribe locally with Whisper first; do not type out text the speaker didn't say.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
4. Highlight Sweep# CES style instruction — C04 Highlight Sweep

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C04 Highlight Sweep** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — The caption reads like a normal subtitle, but selected words get a marker-style highlight that wipes in from left to right on their spoken frame, flipping the word to a dark ink for contrast. Limiting it to one or two words per line turns the captions into an emphasis system: the viewer's eye is pulled to the word that carries the meaning, much like a highlighter on a printed page.

### C04 Highlight Sweep

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** Full caption lines stay steady while an accent-coloured block sweeps in behind one key word per line exactly as it is spoken.
- **Parameters:** Highlighted words: (not set); Highlight colour: #D7FF5F; Text on highlight: #0B0C0E; Highlight shape: Solid block; Sweep duration: 0.2 s; Max highlights per line: 1; Caption typeface: Bold sans (Space Grotesk / Inter Black); Safe-zone offset: 18%
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a highlight-sweep caption layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Choose highlight words from the transcript itself: the noun, number or verb that carries each sentence's meaning (as spoken). Never add words the speaker did not say.
  4. Captions: lines of at most 26 characters, two lines per page, pages cut on phrase boundaries; text in Space Grotesk at Black/ExtraBold weight (fallback: Inter Black), cap height ≈ 6.5% of frame width, colour #F4F2EB with a soft shadow. Highlight at most 1 word per line.
  5. Sweep: on the highlighted word's start frame, draw a solid rectangle 1.15× the word's cap height, padded 0.12em horizontally, radius 0.08em in #D7FF5F behind the word, scaling X from 0 → 1 from its left edge over 6 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)); switch the word's fill to #0B0C0E once the band passes its midpoint. Highlights persist until the page clears.
  6. Placement: anchor the caption block's bottom edge 18% of frame height (346px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
  7. Implement as a Remotion <HighlightSweep /> component: measure each word box with a hidden layout pass (or @remotion/layout-utils measureText) so the band matches the rendered glyph width exactly; render band layer under text layer inside the same line container.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If keywords can't be identified with confidence, ask the user to supply them. Without word timing, transcribe locally first; as a last resort apply highlights at the start of each line rather than mid-line on guessed timing.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.

5. Stacked Captions# CES style instruction — C05 Stacked Captions

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C05 Stacked Captions** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Phrases are broken into two or three balanced short lines and stacked like a magazine pull-quote. Lines enter in a controlled stagger, each sliding up from behind its own mask, and the most important line can be set larger in the accent colour. The block reads in one glance and gives captions a designed, typographic presence suited to statement-led content.

### C05 Stacked Captions

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** Each phrase is set as a tight stack of two or three short lines that rise into view one after another, the key line set larger.
- **Parameters:** Lines per stack: 3 lines; Line stagger: 0.12 s; Alignment: Left; Size contrast: on; Characters per line: 14; Accent colour: #D7FF5F; Caption typeface: Bold sans (Space Grotesk / Inter Black); Safe-zone offset: 20%
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a stacked caption layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level (grouped into phrases) timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Build stacks of 3 lines with at most 14 characters per line, balancing line lengths (no orphan single words, no line under 40% of the longest). A stack covers one phrase; start a new stack at punctuation or a pause ≥ 300ms.
  4. Entrance: each line sits in its own mask and slides up from 100% of its line height to rest over 11 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)); line n starts 4 frames after line n−1, and the first line starts on the phrase's first word. Exit the whole stack upward through the same masks in 6 frames as the next stack begins.
  5. Hierarchy: set the line containing the phrase's key word (as spoken) at 1.35× size in #D7FF5F; other lines in #F4F2EB at cap height ≈ 7% of frame width.
  6. Typeset in Space Grotesk at Black/ExtraBold weight (fallback: Inter Black), line-height 0.95, left-aligned to an 8% margin, with a soft shadow for contrast.
  7. Placement: anchor the stack's bottom edge 20% of frame height (384px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
  8. Implement as a Remotion <StackedCaptions /> component reading captions.json; precompute stacks and line splits once, then drive each line's translateY with interpolate() on its staggered local frame.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If word timing is missing, phrase-level SRT timing is enough for this style — animate each stack on the cue start. Without any transcript, transcribe locally first rather than writing condensed copy.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).

6. Editorial Lower Thirds# CES style instruction — C06 Editorial Lower Thirds

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C06 Editorial Lower Thirds** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — This is the magazine and documentary approach to captions: one or two lines of sentence-case text in an editorial serif or clean sans, generous margins, and a hairline rule in the accent colour. Sentences crossfade over a handful of frames with no bounce or scaling. It signals a premium, considered tone and lets the footage breathe while staying fully accessible.

### C06 Editorial Lower Thirds

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** Calm sentence-case subtitles sit low-left in a refined typeface under a thin accent rule, crossfading softly between sentences.
- **Parameters:** Typeface: Editorial serif; Alignment: Left (editorial); Accent rule: on; Crossfade: 6 frames; Characters per line: 38; Soft bottom scrim: on; Rule colour: #D7FF5F; Safe-zone offset: 14%
- **Steps:**
  1. Add an editorial subtitle layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level (grouped into phrases) timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Segment into subtitles of at most 2 lines × 38 characters, split at sentence or clause boundaries; keep sentence case and the speaker's punctuation. Minimum display 1.0s, maximum 6s (split longer sentences at a comma).
  4. Typeset in an editorial serif at regular weight (e.g. Fraunces, Source Serif or Newsreader), cap height ≈ 4.2% of frame width, line-height 1.3, colour #F4F2EB, left-aligned to an 8% margin. Above the first line draw a #D7FF5F rule 12% of frame width long and 0.35% of width thick, 1.2em above the text; it stays static across subtitles.
  5. Transitions: crossfade between subtitles over 6 frames (opacity only — no scale, slide or blur); start the fade-in on the next subtitle's first word.
  6. Add a soft bottom scrim (black 0% → 45%, covering the lowest 32% of frame) under the text so it holds 4.5:1 on bright footage without a visible box.
  7. Placement: anchor the subtitle block's bottom edge 14% of frame height (269px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** Line-level SRT timing is sufficient for this style. Without a transcript, transcribe locally with Whisper; never condense or rewrite the dialogue for elegance.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.

7. Explosive Keywords# CES style instruction — C07 Explosive Keywords

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C07 Explosive Keywords** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Most of the dialogue runs as a compact, quiet caption line. When a chosen keyword is spoken, it leaves the line and becomes an independent visual event: it scales to several times its size in the accent colour, with optional burst lines and a brief dim of the footage, then returns to its slot. Used sparingly, it turns the one word that matters into a moment the viewer remembers.

### C07 Explosive Keywords

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** A regular caption line runs until a key word bursts out of it, filling the frame at several times its size for a beat before snapping back.
- **Parameters:** Keywords: (not set); Keyword scale: 3.2x; Keyword hold: 0.6 s; Max keywords per sentence: 1; Burst lines: on; Dim footage on keyword: on; Accent colour: #D7FF5F; Caption typeface: Bold sans (Space Grotesk / Inter Black)
- **Steps:**
  1. Add an explosive-keyword caption layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Keywords: pick at most 1 per sentence from the transcript — the result, number or verb the sentence hinges on, as spoken. Never insert words that weren't said; aim for no more than one explosion every 4–5 seconds.
  4. Base captions: one line of ≤ 24 characters in Space Grotesk at Black/ExtraBold weight (fallback: Inter Black), cap height ≈ 5% of frame width, #F4F2EB, anchored per the safe zone (bottom edge 20% above the frame bottom on 9:16).
  5. Explosion: on the keyword's start frame, hide it in the line and spawn a copy that scales from 1x to 3.2x (capped so it fits 90% of frame width) while travelling to the frame's optical center over 6 frames with a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)), rotated −4°, in #D7FF5F. Hold 18 frames, then return to its slot over 5 frames. Add 10–12 radial burst lines (accent, 0.6% width stroke) that shoot outward and fade within 8 frames. Dim the footage to 65% brightness for the hold, easing back over 6 frames.
  6. Keep the speaker's eyes uncovered: if the centered keyword would overlap the face, place it in the upper third instead.
  7. Implement as a Remotion <ExplosiveKeywords /> component reading captions.json plus a keywords list; the explosion is a separate absolutely-positioned layer sequenced with <Sequence from={keywordStartFrame}>.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Sound effects, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** Without word timing, explosions cannot land on the word — transcribe locally first. If no clear keywords exist, ask the user which words matter rather than exploding filler.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.
