8. Subtitle Capsules# CES style instruction — C08 Subtitle Capsules

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C08 Subtitle Capsules** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Captions are set inside a solid, rounded container — like an app label or chat chip — that animates to the width of each new phrase. The capsule guarantees contrast on any footage and gives captions a friendly, product-like personality. An optional speaker dot turns the capsule into a light-weight speaker identifier for interviews and duets.

### C08 Subtitle Capsules

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** Each short phrase appears inside a rounded label capsule that stretches, pops or slides to fit the next phrase as the speaker talks.
- **Parameters:** Capsule colour: #F4F2EB; Text colour: #0B0C0E; Corner shape: Full pill; Phrase change: Stretch to the new width; Words per capsule: 4; Speaker dot: on; Caption typeface: Clean sans (Inter Semibold); Safe-zone offset: 20%
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a capsule caption layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level (grouped into phrases) timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Chunk dialogue into phrases of at most 4 words (≤ 26 characters, one line), cutting at natural pauses. Each capsule appears on its first word's start frame and holds until the next phrase starts; clear after 15 frames of silence.
  4. Capsule: #F4F2EB fill, fully rounded ends (radius = height / 2), padding 0.45em × 0.9em, a soft shadow (0 0.6% 2% black at 35%). Text in Inter Semibold (fallback: Inter Semibold), cap height ≈ 5% of frame width, colour #0B0C0E. Put a 0.5em speaker dot before the text; if several speakers are present, give each a distinct dot colour and keep it consistent.
  5. Phrase change: animate the capsule's width from the old phrase width to the new one over 7 frames with a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)) (center-anchored), crossfading the text over the first 4 frames. Measure text widths with @remotion/layout-utils measureText so capsule widths are exact.
  6. Placement: anchor the capsule's bottom edge 20% of frame height (384px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
  7. Implement as a Remotion <SubtitleCapsules /> component reading captions.json; precompute phrase widths, then interpolate width/position per phrase boundary.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** Line-level SRT timing works if word timing is unavailable — use cue boundaries as phrase changes. Without any transcript, transcribe locally first.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
9. Monospace Terminal Captions# CES style instruction — C09 Monospace Terminal Captions

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C09 Monospace Terminal Captions** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Captions behave like a command line: each phrase prints after a prompt symbol, word by word in sync with the voice, followed by a blinking cursor. Completed lines scroll up and dim like terminal history. The deliberate, mechanical reveal suits developer, AI and technical content and makes the captions feel native to the subject rather than decoration.

### C09 Monospace Terminal Captions

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** Dialogue prints word by word in a monospace terminal block with a prompt and blinking cursor, older lines scrolling up and dimming.
- **Parameters:** Prompt symbol: ">"; Cursor: Block █; Reveal unit: Word by word; Previous lines kept: 1 lines; Text colour: #D7FF5F; Terminal title bar: on; Faint scan lines: off; Safe-zone offset: 16%
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a terminal caption layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps).
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Lines: one phrase per line, max 28 characters (monospace makes this exact), split at pauses ≥ 250ms; prefix every line with "> " in 50% opacity. Text keeps the speaker's words verbatim in lowercase unless the user asks otherwise (proper nouns and acronyms keep their case).
  4. Reveal word by word — each word appears whole on its start frame; a █ cursor in #D7FF5F sits after the last revealed character and blinks at 1.6Hz (on 10 frames / off 9 frames at 30fps) whenever no word is being revealed.
  5. History: when a new line starts, previous lines scroll up one line-height over 4 frames and drop to 45% opacity; keep 1 previous line visible, older lines fade out.
  6. Block: monospace (JetBrains Mono / IBM Plex Mono Medium), cap height ≈ 4.2% of frame width, text #D7FF5F, on #0A0C0A at 82% with radius 1.5% of width and a 0.15% #D7FF5F border at 25% opacity. Add a slim title bar (three 1.6%-wide dots and a 'captions' label) above the text.
  7. Placement: anchor the terminal block's bottom edge 16% of frame height (307px at 1080×1920) above the bottom of the frame, horizontally centered with ≥ 8% side margins, so it stays clear of the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame). Never cover the speaker's eyes or mouth; if the face sits unusually low in a shot, lift the block above it for that shot only.
  8. Implement as a Remotion <TerminalCaptions /> component reading captions.json; derive the visible word index from useCurrentFrame() and render history lines from the same data.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** Without word timing, use phrase-level SRT cues and reveal each line's words evenly across its cue. Without a transcript, transcribe locally — never type out paraphrased 'code-like' text.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
10. Bilingual Caption Flow# CES style instruction — C10 Bilingual Caption Flow

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **C10 Bilingual Caption Flow** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Captions** — Two coordinated caption tiers run together: the primary line carries the spoken language in a strong weight, and a secondary line carries the translation in a lighter, smaller, colour-shifted style. Both tiers share timing and change together, and their combined block is positioned against a configurable safe-zone offset so neither collides with platform UI. It lets one video serve two language audiences without doubling the edit.

### C10 Bilingual Caption Flow

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Captions layer (topmost text)
- **What it does:** A bold primary caption in the spoken language is paired with a smaller, differently coloured translation line, both kept inside the safe zone.
- **Parameters:** Secondary language: "Bahasa Melayu"; Translation source: Supplied translated subtitle file; Secondary line position: Below primary; Secondary size: 62%; Primary colour: #F4F2EB; Secondary colour: #779CFF; Safe-zone offset: 18%; Language tags: off
- **Steps:**
  1. Add a two-tier caption layer across every spoken section of the video on the top track (1080×1920 @ 30fps): primary in English (the spoken language), secondary in Bahasa Melayu.
  2. Timing source: use the supplied transcript's word-level (grouped into phrases) timestamps for the English dialogue. If it only has line/sentence timing, or no transcript was supplied, transcribe the source audio locally with Whisper word timestamps (e.g. `whisper source.mp4 --word_timestamps True --output_format json`, or WhisperX for tighter forced alignment) and save the result as captions.json. Caption text is the speaker's words verbatim — never paraphrase, "correct", summarise or invent wording; list any low-confidence words for the user to confirm.
  3. Secondary text: use the supplied Bahasa Melayu subtitle file verbatim and align its cues to the primary phrases by timecode overlap. If no translated file is supplied, stop and ask for one — or for permission to translate.
  4. Segmentation: phrases of ≤ 2 primary lines × 32 characters; the secondary tier may wrap to 2 lines of ≤ 40 characters. Both tiers appear and clear together (same in/out frames), crossfading over 4 frames.
  5. Hierarchy: primary in Space Grotesk Bold, cap height ≈ 5.6% of frame width, #F4F2EB; secondary in Inter Medium italic at 62% of the primary size, #779CFF, placed below the primary with a 0.5em gap.
  6. Safe zone: the whole two-tier block's bottom edge sits 18% of frame height (346px) above the frame bottom; the block grows upward when the secondary tier wraps, so the bottom edge never moves. Keep ≥ 8% side margins and clear the TikTok / Reels / Shorts interface (description text and action buttons occupy roughly the bottom 20% and the right-hand 12% of the frame).
  7. Implement as a Remotion <BilingualCaptions /> component reading both caption JSON files; render a dark 40% scrim behind the block when both tiers exceed 3 total lines.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Transcript with timing (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Document / PDF, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If no translation is supplied and translating is not approved, render primary captions only (as C06) and list the missing secondary-language file in the report. Never machine-translate silently.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.

Layouts1. Vertical Split Screen# CES style instruction — L01 Vertical Split Screen

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L01 Vertical Split Screen** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — The full-frame shot slides apart into two regions separated by a crisp divider: typically the speaker on top and the thing they're talking about below. The split ratio is adjustable and can shift mid-section when the second region needs more room. Viewers get the face and the evidence at once, which is the most reliable way to keep reaction and context on screen together in vertical video.

### L01 Vertical Split Screen

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:10.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** The frame divides into two regions — speaker in one, a screen recording or second shot in the other — split by a divider at an adjustable ratio.
- **Parameters:** Split orientation: Stacked (top / bottom); Split ratio: 55%; Speaker region: Region A (top / left); Second region source: Screen recording; Divider thickness: 0.5%; Divider colour: #D7FF5F; Split-in duration: 0.5 s; Easing: Expo out (snappy)
- **Steps:**
  1. Between from 00:00.00 to 00:10.00, split the 1080×1920 frame horizontally into a top region A and a bottom region B: A = 55% (1056px), B = 45% (864px).
  2. Region A: the speaker, re-cropped (not squeezed) so the eyes sit on the region's upper third; region B: the supplied screen recording, cropped to fill. Scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio.
  3. Transition in: starting from the full-frame speaker shot, animate the split line from the frame edge to 55% over 15 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)), re-cropping the speaker in sync so the face never leaves its region; reverse the move when the section ends.
  4. Divider: a #D7FF5F bar 0.5% of the frame height thick (10px) centred on the split, with a subtle 0 0 1.5% glow of the same colour.
  5. If the script calls for it, let the ratio shift by up to ±12% mid-section (e.g. give region B more room while a detail is being explained), using the same easing over 12 frames — never more than once every 3 seconds.
  6. Implement in Remotion as two absolutely-positioned <AbsoluteFill> regions with overflow:hidden and <OffthreadVideo style={{objectFit:'cover'}}>; or in FFmpeg with crop + vstack/hstack after scaling each input to its region size. Keep captions on top of both regions, centred on the divider or inside the speaker region.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screen recording, B-roll footage, Second shot
- **Fallback:** If no second source is supplied, ask for one — do not fill region B with invented UI or stock footage. A screen recording can be replaced by a sequence of supplied screenshots with slow pans.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
2. Picture-in-Picture Focus# CES style instruction — L02 Picture-in-Picture Focus

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L02 Picture-in-Picture Focus** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — The evidence — a recording, demo or B-roll — becomes the main picture, and the speaker shrinks into a floating picture-in-picture window with a border and soft shadow. The window is movable: it can glide to another corner whenever important content would be hidden underneath. The audience keeps the human connection while giving the detail the space it needs.

### L02 Picture-in-Picture Focus

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:15.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** The source media fills the frame while the speaker sits in a rounded window in one corner that glides to another corner when detail appears beneath it.
- **Parameters:** PiP corner: Bottom right; PiP width: 34%; Window shape: Rounded rectangle; Inside the window: Speaker (source media fills the frame); Move away from detail: on; Border colour: #F4F2EB; Edge margin: 4%; Easing: Spring (physical)
- **Steps:**
  1. Between from 00:00.00 to 00:15.00, fill the 1080×1920 frame with the supplied source media (screen recording / B-roll) and place the speaker in a picture-in-picture window. Scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio.
  2. Window: 367×459px (34% of frame width, 4:5), corner radius 29px, 0.5% #F4F2EB border, shadow 0 1.5% 4% black at 45%. Crop the speaker so the face fills the upper 60% of the window.
  3. Initial position: bottom right corner, inset 4% from the side edges and kept clear of platform UI (on 9:16, stay above the bottom 18% and left of the right 12% action rail).
  4. Entrance: scale the window from 0.6 → 1.0 with opacity 0 → 1 over 11 frames using a physical spring (Remotion spring(): damping ≈ 12, stiffness ≈ 180, mass 1); exit with the reverse.
  5. Movement: inspect the main picture for important content (cursor, highlighted UI, text being discussed) under the window. When it would be covered, glide the window to the nearest free corner over 14 frames with a physical spring (Remotion spring(): damping ≈ 12, stiffness ≈ 180, mass 1); at most one move every 2 seconds. List the move timecodes in your report.
  6. Implement in Remotion with an <AbsoluteFill> main layer and a positioned window container (overflow hidden, borderRadius) whose left/top are interpolated between corner anchors; or in FFmpeg with scale + overlay=x:y using expressions for the move.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screen recording, B-roll footage, Second shot
- **Fallback:** Without a separate source (screen recording / B-roll), ask for one; do not pip the speaker over a duplicate of their own shot. If no clean corner exists, shrink the window to 24% width rather than covering essential content.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
3. Triple Stack# CES style instruction — L03 Triple Stack

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L03 Triple Stack** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — The vertical frame is divided into three stacked sections — for example speaker, screen and result — with a clear focal hierarchy: the section being discussed takes the largest share at full brightness while the others compress into supporting strips. As the script moves on, focus passes to the next band with a smooth resize. It packs three sources into one frame without becoming a confusing collage.

### L03 Triple Stack

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:10.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Three media bands stack vertically and the focal band grows to take most of the frame while the other two shrink and dim, shifting with the script.
- **Parameters:** Initial focal section: Top; Focal section height: 50%; Shift focus with the script: on; Gutter: 1%; Dim non-focal sections: on; Section labels: off; Focus outline colour: #D7FF5F; Easing: Ease in-out (smooth)
- **Steps:**
  1. Between from 00:00.00 to 00:10.00, divide the 1080×1920 frame into three full-width horizontal bands separated by 1% gutters (19px, background #0B0C0E): focal band 50% (960px), other bands 24.0% each (461px).
  2. Assign sources from the supplied media: typically speaker / screen recording / result shot. Ask the user if the order isn't clear from the script. Each band crops its source with object-fit: cover centred on the subject — resizing a band re-crops, it never squashes. Scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio.
  3. Focus: start with the top band focal. Pass focus to whichever band the speaker is referring to (derive the moments from the transcript; list them in your report), animating band heights over 15 frames using a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)); hold each focus ≥ 2s.
  4. Dim non-focal bands to 55% brightness and desaturate 30%; outline the focal band with a #D7FF5F inner stroke 0.4% of width that travels with the focus change.
  5. No section labels; the focus change alone carries the hierarchy.
  6. Entrance: slide the bands in from alternating sides with a 4-frame stagger over 14 frames. Implement in Remotion with three containers whose top/height are interpolated, each holding an <OffthreadVideo objectFit="cover">.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screen recording, B-roll footage, Screenshots, Second shot
- **Fallback:** With only two sources, use L01 Vertical Split Screen instead. Never fill the third band with a duplicate or invented asset.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L04 Floating Device Frame** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**Layouts** — Raw screen captures look flat and unfinished. Here the recording is placed inside a clean device frame that rises into view, settles at a slight perspective tilt and floats with a subtle drift and glass reflection, over a blurred version of the source footage or a brand gradient. The product moment instantly looks like a launch video rather than a screen share.

### L04 Floating Device Frame

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:08.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A screen recording plays inside a polished phone, tablet or browser mockup that floats in with a gentle 3D tilt over a soft background.

- **Steps:**

  2. Background: the source footage scaled 115%, Gaussian blur ≈ 4% of width, brightness 55%; add a soft contact shadow under the device (ellipse, black 40%, blur 3% of width).
  3. Entrance: rise from +40% of frame height with rotateX 25° → 6.0° and opacity 0 → 1 over 21 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)); use CSS perspective ≈ 2.5× the device width.
  4. Idle float: oscillate translateY by ±1.2% of frame height and rotateY between −10° and +10° on a slow sine (period 4s); keep motion subtle enough that UI text stays readable. Add a diagonal glass reflection (white 0% → 10% → 0% gradient) that sweeps across the screen once on entry.
  5. Exit with the reverse of the entrance over 12 frames. Implement in Remotion with a perspective container and a transformed device <div>; the recording plays via <OffthreadVideo> inside the screen mask.
  6. Verify that the smallest UI text in the recording renders ≥ 2.2% of frame width inside the frame; if not, crop the recording to the relevant region or increase device size.
- **Assets:** Screen recording (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Source video, Screenshots, Brand logo
- **Fallback:** If only screenshots are supplied, use them as stills inside the device with a slow scroll; never mock up UI that isn't in the supplied material. Without any screen material, ask for it.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.

- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.

5. Editorial Sidebar# CES style instruction — L05 Editorial Sidebar

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L05 Editorial Sidebar** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — Part of the frame is reserved for explanation: a solid editorial panel slides in from the side and the speaker's shot re-frames into the remaining space. The panel carries a short heading and two to four annotations, data points or definitions that appear one by one as they are mentioned. It works like a magazine sidebar — the speaker talks, the panel holds the takeaways.

### L05 Editorial Sidebar

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:10.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A clean text panel slides in from one side while the speaker re-centres in the remaining frame, then fills with a heading and numbered notes.
- **Parameters:** Sidebar side: Right; Sidebar width: 40%; Sidebar heading: (not set); Annotations: (not set); Numbered notes: on; Panel colour: #F4F2EB; Accent colour: #FF7A6B; Note stagger: 0.15 s
- **Steps:**
  1. Between from 00:00.00 to 00:10.00, slide a solid #F4F2EB panel 40% of frame width (432px), full height, in from the right edge over 15 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)); simultaneously re-crop the speaker into the remaining 60% so the face is centred in it (object-fit: cover — scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio.)
  2. Heading: derive a 2–5 word heading from the speaker's own framing of this section in the transcript (English); never introduce a claim they don't make.
  3. Notes: 2–4 short annotations taken from the transcript or the supplied data, each ≤ 32 characters. If the material doesn't contain clear takeaways, ask the user for them.
  4. Reveal each note as the speaker mentions it (derive times from the transcript), or 0.15s apart if they are listed together: fade + rise 2% over 9 frames. Number them 01, 02… in #FF7A6B mono. Note text in Inter, ≥ 3.6% of frame width.
  5. Keep ≥ 6% inner padding in the panel and respect the platform safe zone (on 9:16 leave the bottom 18% of the panel empty). Captions move into the speaker area while the panel is visible.
  6. Exit by sliding the panel back out over 12 frames as the speaker re-centres. Implement in Remotion with an interpolated panel translateX and an interpolated speaker crop offset.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Verified data, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If no heading or notes are supplied and the transcript lacks clear takeaways, ask the user for the panel copy. Never pad the sidebar with generic tips.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
6. Center Spotlight# CES style instruction — L06 Center Spotlight

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L06 Center Spotlight** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — The main subject is isolated in a clean central window — rounded, circular or capsule-shaped — that closes in from the full frame like an iris. Outside it, a blurred and darkened copy of the same footage keeps the environment present, with optional context labels. Attention locks on the subject while the surrounding atmosphere still tells the viewer where this is happening.

### L06 Center Spotlight

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:06.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** The shot contracts into a sharp central frame with a thin accent ring while the surrounding area becomes a blurred, darkened copy carrying context labels.
- **Parameters:** Frame shape: Rounded rectangle; Frame width: 72%; Surround treatment: Blurred + darkened copy; Surround dim: 55%; Accent ring: on; Ring colour: #D7FF5F; Context label: (not set); Easing: Expo out (snappy)
- **Steps:**
  1. Between from 00:00.00 to 00:06.00, duplicate the shot into two layers: a background copy (scaled 112%) and a sharp foreground copy masked to a central rounded rectangle 778×973px with 54px radius, centred on the subject's face (eyes on the window's upper third).
  2. Surround: Gaussian blur ≈ 3% of width and brightness 45% on the background copy. Scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio.
  3. Entrance: close the mask from the full frame to its final size over 18 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)); the foreground copy does not scale, only the mask moves, so the subject never distorts.
  4. Ring: a #D7FF5F stroke 0.4% of width hugging the mask, drawn on (opacity 0 → 1, scale 1.06 → 1.0) in the last 8 frames of the entrance.
  5. Context labels: only if the script names the place or situation — use those exact words; otherwise leave the surround clean.
  6. Exit by reopening the mask to full frame over 14 frames. Implement in Remotion with clipPath (inset(... round ...) or circle()) on the foreground <AbsoluteFill>, or in FFmpeg with a geq/alphamerge mask over a boxblur background.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Second shot, B-roll footage
- **Fallback:** If the subject moves a lot, track the window to the face (or use a wider frame size) rather than letting them leave it; if tracking isn't possible, keep the effect to stationary sections.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
7. Grid Conversation# CES style instruction — L07 Grid Conversation

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L07 Grid Conversation** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — Multiple participants — podcast guests, remote callers or reference clips — are arranged in a tidy grid with consistent gutters and corner radii. Tiles pop in one after another, then an active-speaker outline and a small level indicator follow the conversation. Viewers always see the whole panel and instantly know who is speaking, just like a well-produced video call.

### L07 Grid Conversation

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:12.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Speakers or references populate a 2×2 (or 1+2) grid tile by tile, and a highlight moves to whoever is currently talking.
- **Parameters:** Grid: 2 × 2; Gutter: 1.2%; Tile corner radius: 2%; Active-speaker highlight: on; Highlight colour: #D7FF5F; Name tags: on; Tile entry stagger: 0.12 s
- **Steps:**
  1. Between from 00:00.00 to 00:12.00, arrange 4 supplied participant feeds in a 2 × 2 grid on a #0B0C0E canvas with 1.2% gutters (13px) and 22px corner radii; outer margin equals the gutter.
  2. Crop each feed to its tile with object-fit: cover centred on the face (eyes on the tile's upper third). Scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio. Sync all feeds to a common clap/waveform before laying them out.
  3. Entrance: tiles pop in one after another, 0.12s apart (scale 0.9 → 1, opacity 0 → 1 over 9 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1))), in reading order.
  4. Active speaker: detect who is speaking from each feed's audio level (RMS above −35 dBFS for ≥ 200ms, with 300ms hold) or from speaker-labelled transcript segments; draw a #D7FF5F 0.5%-width inner outline on that tile and a small 3-bar level meter in its corner. The outline moves between tiles over 6 frames.
  5. Name tags: bottom-left of each tile, small sans (2.8% of width) on a 60% black pill — use the names/handles the user supplies; if none are given, ask rather than inventing names.
  6. Implement in Remotion with a CSS grid of <OffthreadVideo> tiles, or in FFmpeg with xstack after scaling/cropping each input to the tile size. Mix all participant audio at matched loudness.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Second shot (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** With a single camera, don't fake extra participants — use L02 Picture-in-Picture with supplied reference media instead. Missing feeds should be reported, not filled with placeholders.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.

- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.

- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).

8. Cinematic Letterbox# CES style instruction — L08 Cinematic Letterbox

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L08 Cinematic Letterbox** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — Controlled letterboxing instantly changes the register of a video from creator content to film. Bars ease in to a mathematically exact aspect ratio, the shot is re-cropped (or fitted over a blurred fill) inside the band so nothing is squashed, and the empty bars can carry titles or captions. Used for a section or the whole piece, it signals a deliberate, cinematic moment.

### L08 Cinematic Letterbox

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:08.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Black bars close in from top and bottom to a true 2.39:1, 2:1 or 1.85:1 band, cropping (never stretching) the shot into a cinematic frame.
- **Parameters:** Letterbox ratio: 2.39:1 (anamorphic scope); Reframing: Crop to the band (no scaling); Bar animation: 0.6 s; Band position: 0%; Bar colour: #000000; Use bars for titles/captions: on; Easing: Ease in-out (smooth)
- **Steps:**
  1. Between 00:00.00 and 00:08.00, letterbox the 1080×1920 frame to 2.39:1: visible band 1080×452px, bars 734px each (top bar 734px, bottom bar 734px with the 0% band offset) in #000000.
  2. Reframing: crop the source to the band without scaling; position the crop so the subject's eyes sit on the band's upper third (track per shot if the subject moves). Scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio.
  3. Bars animate in from the frame edges over 18 frames using a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)) at the start, and out the same way at the end; the crop moves in sync so the subject stays inside the closing band.
  4. Use the bars as typographic space: titles or captions for this section sit centred in the bottom bar (and labels in the top bar) in Inter, never overlapping the picture band. Text comes from the user's params or the transcript only.
  5. Implement in Remotion with two absolutely-positioned bars whose height is interpolated, over an <AbsoluteFill> crop; or in FFmpeg with crop=1080:452:0:(ih-452)/2+0,pad=1080:1920:0:(oh-ih)/2-0:color=0x000000.
  6. Verify one frame with a circle test: any round object in the source must remain perfectly round after letterboxing (no aspect distortion).
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If key content (faces, on-screen text) can't fit the band, use the 'fit' reframing with a blurred fill, or limit the letterbox to B-roll shots and keep talking-head sections full frame.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
9. Collage Canvas# CES style instruction — L09 Collage Canvas

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L09 Collage Canvas** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — An editorial scrapbook of the story: supplied media cards — photos, the speaker, a screenshot, a short note — land one by one on a textured canvas with small rotations, tape and soft shadows, organised around a single headline. A gentle parallax drift keeps the composition alive. It turns a set of references into one designed page that viewers can take in at a glance.

### L09 Collage Canvas

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:06.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Photos, a speaker cut-out, a screenshot and a note card fly onto a paper canvas and settle at slight angles around a bold headline.
- **Parameters:** Media cards: 4 cards; Rotation jitter: 6 deg; Canvas: Warm paper; Headline: (not set); Tape & stickers: on; Card stagger: 0.22 s; Accent colour: #D7FF5F; Parallax drift: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Between from 00:00.00 to 00:06.00, build a full-frame collage on a warm off-white paper texture (#EDE6D8 with subtle fibre noise), 1080×1920.
  2. Cards: 4 media cards made only from supplied assets (photos, screenshots, a speaker cut-out if a subject mask exists). Each card keeps its native aspect ratio with a 2% white border and a soft drop shadow — scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio. Arrange on a loose 2-column grid with deliberate overlaps, rotations within ±6°, and keep ≥ 1 card's key detail fully uncovered.
  3. Headline: a 2–4 word title taken from the speaker's own words for this section; ask the user if none fits.
  4. Assembly: headline first, then cards land one by one 0.22s apart, each flying in from just outside the nearest edge (translate 30–40% of frame, rotation +8° extra) and settling with a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)) over 14 frames. Add translucent tape strips or a small sticker on 1–2 cards as they land.
  5. Hold with a slow parallax drift: each card translates 0.5–1.5% in alternating directions (back cards less) over the hold; exit by fading the whole canvas over 9 frames.
  6. Implement in Remotion with one absolutely-positioned card component per asset driven by spring() with staggered delays; ensure the total assembly finishes ≥ 1.5s before the section ends so the composition can be read.
- **Assets:** Photos (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Source video, Subject mask / matte, Screenshots, Brand logo
- **Fallback:** With fewer than three supplied visuals, reduce the card count or use B02 Floating Screenshot Cards; never pad the collage with stock or generated imagery.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
10. Adaptive Proof Panel# CES style instruction — L10 Adaptive Proof Panel

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **L10 Adaptive Proof Panel** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Layouts** — The frame allocates space dynamically between the talking head and the supporting evidence. When the speaker references proof, their shot contracts into a rounded band and a labelled evidence panel rises into the freed space; if a second piece of evidence follows, the panel grows and the speaker band shrinks further. When the proof section ends, the speaker reclaims the full frame — the layout always reflects what matters right now.

### L10 Adaptive Proof Panel

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:08.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** When evidence appears the speaker's shot shrinks into a band and a proof panel takes the rest, resizing as more evidence arrives, then hands the frame back.
- **Parameters:** Evidence share: 62%; Speaker placement: Top band; Resize duration: 0.45 s; Evidence panel: Floating card; Source label: (not set); Highlight the key detail: on; Accent: #D7FF5F; Evidence file: (not set)
- **Steps:**
  1. Between from 00:00.00 to 00:08.00, run the talking head full-frame until the speaker first references the evidence (find the moment in the transcript; list it in your report).
  2. Evidence: use only the real screenshots/documents the user supplied for this claim. If none are supplied, stop and ask — never mock up evidence.
  3. Reallocation: over 14 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)), contract the speaker into a rounded top band (32% of frame height, 3% margins, radius 3% of width, face re-centred by re-cropping) and raise the evidence panel into the remaining 62% (1190px).
  4. Panel: a floating card with a 2.5% radius and soft shadow, evidence fitted with object-fit: contain on #16181C. Scale every source uniformly (object-fit: cover for crops, contain for fits) — never stretch footage, screenshots or recordings to a region's aspect ratio. Label it with the source taken from the file name or the user's description in small mono caps plus "SUPPLIED". Outline the specific detail the speaker cites with a #D7FF5F rounded rectangle that draws on 6 frames after the panel lands.
  5. If a second piece of evidence follows within the same section, grow the panel by up to 12% of frame height (speaker band shrinks accordingly) and stack the new item in with the same easing; never go below 20% height for the speaker band.
  6. When the speaker moves on, reverse the reallocation so the talking head returns to full frame. Implement in Remotion with interpolated container rects (top/height) for speaker and panel; keep captions in the speaker band or above the panel, never over the evidence.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Social comment, Document / PDF, Verified data, Screen recording
- **Fallback:** Without supplied evidence, do not use this layout — keep the speaker full-frame and note in the report which claim lacked proof.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
























Proof1. Screenshot Punch# CES style instruction — P01 Screenshot Punch

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **P01 Screenshot Punch** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Proof** — The moment the speaker makes a claim, the supporting screenshot punches into frame: it enters slightly oversized, settles to its final width with a short overshoot and casts a layered shadow that separates it from the footage. The background dims or blurs just enough to make the capture the only thing to read. Because it lands on the exact word that references it, the screenshot reads as evidence rather than decoration.

### P01 Screenshot Punch

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** A real screenshot drops in over the speaker at a precise scale, lands with a soft overshoot and a deep shadow, holds long enough to read, then clears.
- **Parameters:** Screenshot file: (not set); Duration: 3 s; Entrance: Drop + punch (from above, overshoot); Screenshot width: 82%; Landing tilt: -2 deg; Shadow depth: 60%; Background treatment: Dim footage 45%; Easing: Back out (overshoot)
- **Steps:**
  1. Place screenshot from the project assets (the user's supplied screenshot; if none is present, stop and ask for it — do not substitute a mock-up) on an overlay layer above the source footage from 00:00.00 to 00:03.00 (1080×1920 @ 30fps). Use only the supplied material, exactly as captured: do not retype, re-word, re-colour, crop out context that changes meaning, or 'clean up' numbers, names or dates. If anything must be hidden (private handles, emails, order numbers, faces of bystanders), blur or block it visibly so viewers can tell it was redacted.
  2. Scale it to 82% of frame width (886px), preserving its aspect ratio; if it would exceed 70% of frame height, fit by height instead. Never upscale beyond 200% of its native pixels — if it would, show it smaller rather than blurry. Round corners by ≈ 2.5% of its width.
  3. Entrance over 14 frames: enter from 12% of frame height above its rest position at scale 1.12 and land at scale 1.0, using a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)), while rotating from -5.0° to a resting tilt of -2°. Opacity reaches 1 within the first 3 frames.
  4. Shadow: two stacked drop shadows — a tight contact shadow (y offset 0.6% of frame width, blur 1.2%, 31% black) and a soft ambient one (y offset 3%, blur 8%, 37% black). Add a 1px 10% white inner stroke so dark screenshots separate from dark footage.
  5. Dim the underlying footage to 55% brightness over 9 frames as the screenshot enters, restoring it on exit.
  6. Land the punch on the exact word in the transcript where the speaker references the screenshot. Hold it fully still for at least 2.3s — long enough to read every word that matters (≈ 3 words per second); extend the range rather than cutting it short.
  7. Exit over 8 frames (scale 1.0 → 0.96, opacity → 0, ease-in). Verify on a phone-sized render that the key text in the screenshot is legible at 82% width.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If no screenshot is supplied, do not recreate, mock up or 'illustrate' the evidence — omit this beat and list it as a missing asset, or ask the user for the capture. If the screenshot is too low-resolution to read, show it smaller with a crop around the relevant area (keeping enough context to stay honest) and flag it.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
2. Comment Spotlight# CES style instruction — P02 Comment Spotlight

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **P02 Comment Spotlight** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Proof** — A genuine viewer comment or testimonial is isolated on its own card while everything around it recedes. After a beat for the viewer to start reading, a highlighter sweep or underline lands on the single phrase that matters, steering the eye without altering the words. It turns social proof into a readable moment instead of a blurry screenshot flashed past.

### P02 Comment Spotlight

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** One real comment is lifted out of the feed onto a clean card, the rest of the frame dims, and the key phrase is marked with a highlighter sweep.
- **Parameters:** Comment screenshot: (not set); Exact comment text (optional): (not set); Phrase to highlight: (not set); Highlight style: Highlighter sweep; Handle treatment: Show handle (permission given); Background dim: 60%; Duration: 3.5 s; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Use the real comment from the project assets (the user's supplied comment screenshot; if none is present, stop and ask for it — do not substitute a mock-up) from 00:00.00 to 00:03.50. Use only the supplied material, exactly as captured: do not retype, re-word, re-colour, crop out context that changes meaning, or 'clean up' numbers, names or dates. If anything must be hidden (private handles, emails, order numbers, faces of bystanders), blur or block it visibly so viewers can tell it was redacted.
  2. Present the supplied comment capture itself on the card (crop to the comment's bounding box with ≥ 2% padding). Do not re-type it; if it is unreadable, ask the user for the exact text rather than guessing.
  3. Card: 907px wide, centered in the upper half, 3% corner radius, soft shadow; it lifts out with scale 0.94 → 1.0 and translateY 3% → 0 over 12 frames (a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1))). Dim the footage behind by 60%.
  4. Show the commenter's handle and avatar as captured — only because the user confirmed permission. Keep the platform's date/like count if visible; they are part of the context.
  5. After 18 frames, mark the single most relevant phrase (the one the speaker responds to, taken word-for-word from the comment) with a highlighter sweep (#D7FF5F at 45% opacity, multiply blend, left → right over 12 frames). Never highlight words that are not in the comment.
  6. Hold the full card for at least 2.5s (estimate from the comment length at ≈ 3 words/second + 0.8s), then exit with a 8-frame fade while the footage brightens back.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Social comment (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screenshots, Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** Without a real comment (screenshot or exact text with permission), omit the beat — never write a sample comment, invent a handle or paraphrase feedback as if it were a quote. If the speaker reads a comment aloud, you may caption their spoken words as speech, not as a comment card.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
3. Metrics Count-Up# CES style instruction — P03 Metrics Count-Up

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **P03 Metrics Count-Up** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Proof** — A single real metric animates from a starting value that gives it context — zero, last month's figure, or a baseline — to its exact final value, decelerating as it approaches so the last digits are readable. Tabular figures keep the number from jittering, a prefix/suffix gives it units, and a small source line shows where the number came from. The motion makes growth feel tangible while the label keeps it honest.

### P03 Metrics Count-Up

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:02.80 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** A verified number ticks up from a meaningful starting value to its exact final figure in large tabular numerals, with its source label underneath.
- **Parameters:** Verified final value: (not set); Start value: "0"; Prefix: (not set); Suffix: (not set); Decimal places: 0; Count duration: 1.6 s; Source label: (not set); Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. The final value is not set. Ask the user for the exact verified number and its source. Only if the speaker states an exact figure in the transcript may you use it — verbatim — with the source label "As stated by the speaker". Never estimate, extrapolate or invent a number.
  2. Count from 0 to the verified final value over 1.6s (48 frames) with an expo-out curve so the last 20% of the change takes ~50% of the time and the final digits are readable. Starting at zero is fine for totals; for growth claims prefer the real prior value.
  3. Typeset in Space Grotesk heavy weight with tabular numerals (font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums) so the width never jitters; cap height ≈ 16% of frame width, colour #D7FF5F. Format intermediate frames to 0 decimal places with thousands separators appropriate for English.
  4. In Remotion, compute the displayed value per frame as interpolate(frame, [start, start + 48], [0, finalValue], { easing: Easing.out(Easing.exp), extrapolateRight: "clamp" }), then format; the last frame must render the exact final value.
  5. Add a small source line under the number naming where the figure comes from (e.g. the analytics tool and date range from the user's data). If the source is unknown, ask — do not show an unsourced statistic as fact.
  6. Hold the final value fully static for ≥ 1.2s after the count finishes, then fade out over 8 frames. Land the final digit on the word where the speaker says the number.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Verified data (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screenshots, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If no verified value or source is supplied, skip the counter and ask for the data. If the user can share a dashboard screenshot instead, use P01 Screenshot Punch with the real capture. Never animate a number that you calculated, rounded up or approximated from vague speech.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
4. Before and After Wipe# CES style instruction — P04 Before and After Wipe

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **P04 Before and After Wipe** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Proof** — The before image fills the frame; a thin divider with a handle sweeps across it and everything behind the line becomes the after image, at identical framing. The wipe can travel in any direction and settle at a split position so both states stay visible side by side. Because the two images are pixel-aligned, the eye sees only what actually changed.

### P04 Before and After Wipe

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Two aligned real visuals share the frame while a clean divider line sweeps across, revealing the after state over the before, then rests on a split.
- **Parameters:** Before/after files: (not set); Wipe direction: Left → right (vertical divider); Resting divider position: 50%; Wipe duration: 1.4 s; Easing: Ease in-out (smooth); Before label: "Before"; After label: "After"; Divider colour: #F4F2EB
- **Steps:**
  1. Load the before/after pair from the project assets (the user's supplied before/after pair; if none is present, stop and ask for it — do not substitute a mock-up) and show it from 00:00.00 to 00:03.50. Both images must be the user's real captures. Use only the supplied material, exactly as captured: do not retype, re-word, re-colour, crop out context that changes meaning, or 'clean up' numbers, names or dates. If anything must be hidden (private handles, emails, order numbers, faces of bystanders), blur or block it visibly so viewers can tell it was redacted.
  2. Align them first: same resolution, framing and crop (match by feature points if needed). Scale both identically to cover 1080×1920. Never apply a grade, sharpen or exposure change to only one side — any processing must be applied to both.
  3. Stack AFTER above BEFORE and reveal AFTER with a clip mask whose edge travels left to right, from 0% to 50% of the frame width over 1.4s (42 frames) using a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)). It rests at 50%, leaving both states visible.
  4. Draw the divider as a vertical #F4F2EB line 0.4% of frame width thick with a 5%-wide circular handle (two small chevrons) at its midpoint, plus a soft shadow so it reads on light and dark images.
  5. Label each side — "Before" on the before side and "After" on the after side — in Inter bold, ≈ 3% of frame width, on small pill backgrounds in the corners; each label fades as its side shrinks below 15% of the frame.
  6. Hold the resting split for at least 1.5s before the range ends so viewers can compare details; if the speaker walks through differences, keep it on screen for the whole explanation.
- **Assets:** Before & after pair (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Source video, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If only one image exists, or the two are not aligned (different angles/zoom), do not fake the missing state or morph one into the other. Use a simple side-by-side or sequential cut with clear labels instead, and flag that a true wipe needs matching framing.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.

- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.

  5. Keep the capture's date/URL area visible when present — it is part of the evidence. Hold the result for 2.5s (longer if the highlighted line exceeds 8 words), then exit with a 8-frame fade.

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **P06 Receipt Stack** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Proof** — Instead of flashing a collage, authentic proof cards — messages, payment confirmations, reviews, emails — arrive one at a time with a slight rotation and settle onto a growing stack. Each card gets its own readable hold before the next covers it, and a small counter shows how many there are. Quantity reads as weight of evidence while every item stays individually legible.

### P06 Receipt Stack

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:05.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Several real proof captures deal onto the frame one by one, each fully readable before the next lands, building a tidy stack of receipts.
- **Parameters:** Proof captures folder: (not set); Cards in stack: 3 cards; Minimum hold per card: 1.4 s; Arrangement: Fanned stack (rotated); Rotation spread: 6 deg; Show count (e.g. 3 / 5): on; Auto-redact personal data: on; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Take the 3 real captures from the project assets (the user's supplied proof captures; if none is present, stop and ask for it — do not substitute a mock-up) (in the user's order) and build a stack overlay from 00:00.00 to 00:05.00. If fewer than 3 are supplied, use only what exists — never pad the stack with duplicates or mock-ups. Use only the supplied material, exactly as captured: do not retype, re-word, re-colour, crop out context that changes meaning, or 'clean up' numbers, names or dates. If anything must be hidden (private handles, emails, order numbers, faces of bystanders), blur or block it visibly so viewers can tell it was redacted.
  2. Before animating, redact personal data on every card (names of private individuals, emails, phone numbers, order IDs, card digits, addresses) with visible frosted blocks; leave amounts, dates and the platform context untouched.
  3. Each card: scaled to 799px width (max 58% of frame height), 2.5% corner radius, white 1px inner stroke and soft drop shadow. Cards deal in from below-right, travelling ≈ 30% of frame height with a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)) over 12 frames.
  4. Fan them: alternate rest rotations within ±6° and offset each by 1.5% of frame width so earlier cards peek out underneath.
  5. Stagger: the next card may only arrive after the current one has been fully visible for ≥ 1.4s, or longer when it contains more text (≈ 3 words/second + 0.8s). Total ≈ 6.4s for 3 cards. Dim older cards to 70% brightness as new ones land.
  6. Show a small counter ("1 / 3" → "3 / 3") in #D7FF5F mono type at the stack's top-left corner, incrementing on each landing.
  7. After the last card's hold, sweep the whole stack out together (translateY −8%, opacity → 0 over 9 frames).
- **Assets:** Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Social comment, Document / PDF, Source video, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** With only one real capture, use P01 Screenshot Punch instead. If captures contain data the user cannot share, redact it or drop that card — never replace it with a recreated or 'example' receipt.

10. App UI Breakdown# CES style instruction — P10 App UI Breakdown

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **P10 App UI Breakdown** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Proof** — Starting from the real app screen, a smooth push-in travels to the single element being discussed — a button, toggle or panel. Viewers learn where the feature lives and what it does without hunting through a busy interface.

### P10 App UI Breakdown

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:04.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** The camera zooms from a real full-screen capture into one interface element, dims the rest, and a callout line labels what that element actually does.
- **Parameters:** App screenshot or frame: (not set); UI element to focus: (not set); Callout text: (not set); Zoom factor: 2.4x; Callout side: Auto (most empty side); Dim other UI: 55%; Hold on element: 2 s; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Use the real interface capture from the project assets (the user's supplied app screenshot or screen-recording frame; if none is present, stop and ask for it — do not substitute a mock-up) at full frame from 00:00.00 to 00:04.00. If a screen recording is supplied, take the frame where the element is visible in its real state. Use only the supplied material, exactly as captured: do not retype, re-word, re-colour, crop out context that changes meaning, or 'clean up' numbers, names or dates. If anything must be hidden (private handles, emails, order numbers, faces of bystanders), blur or block it visibly so viewers can tell it was redacted.
  2. The focus element is not set. Use the element the speaker names in the transcript at this moment; if unclear, ask.
  3. Push in over 21 frames to 2.4x, centering the element (offset to leave room for the callout), with a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)). Resample from the native-resolution capture; if 2.4x would exceed 150% of native pixels, cap the zoom so text stays crisp.
  4. During the push, dim everything outside the element's padded box by 55% and draw a #D7FF5F focus ring (0.4% of frame width, 1.2% radius, 1.5% padding) around it.
  5. Add a callout on the side with the most empty space with a short label (≤ 8 words) describing the element's function — taken from the UI's own label or the speaker's explanation, never an invented capability.
  6. Hold on the element 2.0s (≥ 3 words/second for the callout), then pull back out to 1x over 15 frames while the ring and callout fade.
- **Assets:** Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screen recording, Source video, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** Without a real capture of the interface, do not build a mock UI and present it as the product. Ask for a screenshot or recording; until then, explain the feature with V07 Callout Bubble on the speaker, clearly without showing an interface.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.
