- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.










Visual Beats1. Kinetic Section Title# CES style instruction — V01 Kinetic Section Title

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **V01 Kinetic Section Title** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Visual Beats** — When the script moves to a new idea, a 1–4 word title rises up through an invisible mask — each line sliding out from behind a hard edge — with a small chapter index and a thin rule for structure. It signals 'new section' the way a magazine heading does, giving viewers a mental bookmark and a breath between ideas.

### V01 Kinetic Section Title

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:01.60 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** A short chapter title rises out of a hidden mask line by line with a small chapter number above it, holds for a beat, then slides away.
- **Parameters:** Section title: (not set); Chapter label: "01"; Reveal: Rise through mask; Duration: 1.6 s; Text size: 10%; Backdrop: Over footage with scrim; Easing: Expo out (snappy); Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Write a 1–4 word section title from the speaker's own wording at this chapter change in the transcript (English); do not add claims.
  2. Add a 45% black gradient scrim behind the text from 00:00.00 to 00:01.60; above the title show the chapter label "01" in Inter mono-style caps at ≈ 2.8% of frame width with a 10%-wide #D7FF5F rule beside it.
  3. Title in Space Grotesk bold, cap height ≈ 10% of frame width, centered vertically. Each line rises 110% of its own height from behind a clip mask (overflow hidden on a line wrapper) over 14 frames, lines 2 frames apart, using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)).
  4. Hold until 1.0s, then exit by sliding lines up through the same mask over 9 frames (ease-in), revealing the footage again.
  5. Keep the title within the title-safe area (10% inset) and above the platform caption band.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If no title is supplied and the transcript has no clear chapter phrasing, ask for the section name rather than inventing one. Without brand fonts use Space Grotesk Bold.

2. Number Burst# CES style instruction — V02 Number Burst

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **V02 Number Burst** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Visual Beats** — Each number the speaker mentions — a ranking position, a step count, a figure — explodes into frame with an overshooting scale pop and a ring of short accent strokes. Entries are deliberately staggered so each lands on its own beat and sticks for a moment. The energy makes lists and rankings feel like a countdown show while keeping each value clearly legible.

### V02 Number Burst

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:02.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Numbers or ranking positions from the script burst onto the frame one after another with a staggered scale pop and radiating accent sparks.
- **Parameters:** Numbers / rankings (one per line): (not set); Mode: Ranking positions (#3 → #1); Stagger: 0.35 s; Burst accent: Radiating sparks; Text size: 14%; Hold after last: 1 s; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. No values are set. Take every number or ranking position verbatim from the transcript (the ranking order the speaker announces). Never invent, round or re-order numbers; if the speaker gives none, ask the user.
  2. Each value: Space Grotesk black, cap height ≈ 14% of frame width, colour #D7FF5F, entering with scale 0 → 1.18 → 1.0 over 10 frames (a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1))) and a 3-frame opacity fade.
  3. Stagger entries by 0.35s (11 frames) — or, better, sync each to the word where the speaker says it — across 3 values from 00:00.00 to 00:02.50.
  4. On each landing, emit 8 short #D7FF5F strokes radiating from the number's center (length 4% → 0 of frame width, travelling 10% outward over 9 frames, ease-out) — purely decorative, no extra text.
  5. When a new value lands, shrink previous ones to 60% scale and 50% opacity (or move them into a row) so the newest is always dominant. Hold the final state 1.0s, then fade all over 8 frames.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Verified data, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If the script contains no explicit numbers, skip this beat — do not create rankings or figures to fill it. Without SFX the visual pop works alone.

**Visual Beats** — Each point in a spoken list cascades onto the frame on its own beat: a numbered or ticked marker lands first, then the item text slides in beside it. The active item is full brightness while earlier items dim, so viewers always know which point is being discussed and can still see the structure so far.

### V04 List Cascade

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:05.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** List items slide in one after another with numbered markers; the current item stays bright while earlier ones step back, keeping a clear hierarchy.

- **Steps:**

  3. Each item: marker pops (scale 0 → 1 over 6 frames), then the text slides in from the left (8% of frame width) with opacity 0 → 1 over 11 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)). Items start 0.8s apart, or on the word where each is spoken.
  4. When a new item lands, dim previous items to 45% opacity; the active item stays 100% with its marker in the accent colour.
  5. Each item must be visible ≥ 1.9s before the list clears (≈ 3 words/second). After the last item, hold the complete list 1.2s, then fade out bottom → top with a 2-frame stagger.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If the speaker's list is implicit, ask the user to confirm the items. For lists over 6 items, split into two cascades rather than shrinking the type.

5. Comparison Collision# CES style instruction — V05 Comparison Collision

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **V05 Comparison Collision** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Visual Beats** — Option A enters from one side, option B from the other; they meet at center with a short impact and bounce apart into an aligned two-column layout with a VS marker between them. The physical collision dramatises the contrast, and the settled alignment makes the comparison easy to scan — same size, same baseline, each with its own label and colour.

### V05 Comparison Collision

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Two option cards fly in from opposite edges, collide in the middle with a small impact, and settle side by side under a VS divider for comparison.
- **Parameters:** Option A: (not set); Option B: (not set); Collision: Slam + bounce apart; Settled layout: Side by side; Option A colour: #FF7A6B; Option B colour: #779CFF; Impact shake: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Option labels are not fully set. Use the two things the speaker contrasts, named in their own words from the transcript; any attributes shown under each must also come from the script or supplied assets — never invented pros/cons.
  2. Build two equal cards (42% wide × 34% tall each, 3% radius) from 00:00.00 to 00:03.00. If product images or screenshots are supplied, place them in the cards; otherwise use the label only.
  3. Animate A from off-frame left and B from off-frame right, accelerating (ease-in) to meet at center in 11 frames, then rebound apart to their settled columns over 12 frames with a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)).
  4. On contact: a 4-frame positional shake of the card layer (≤ 1% of frame width) and a brief white flash line along the contact edge (2 frames, 60%).
  5. Once settled, pop a "VS" badge (Space Grotesk black, ≈ 7% of frame width) into the gap with scale 0 → 1.1 → 1. Hold the aligned comparison ≥ 1.5s (longer while the speaker compares), then slide both cards out.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Product image, Screenshots, Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** If the script only names one option, use V03 Keyword Spotlight instead. Never add a competitor or a claim about either option that the speaker did not make.

**Visual Beats** — A small bubble grows from a point on a visible object — a product on the desk, a tool in hand, the speaker themselves — with its tail anchored to that point. The pointer makes the connection explicit, and the short text names or explains the object. If the object moves, the anchor tracks it, so the label always belongs to the right thing.

### V07 Callout Bubble

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:02.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)

  4. Entrance: scale from 0 at the anchor point (transform-origin = tail tip) to 1.0 over 9 frames with a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)); text fades in 2 frames later.
  5. Track the anchor point frame by frame (point tracking or manual keyframes every 4–6 frames, smoothed) so the tail stays attached if the object or camera moves.
  6. Hold ≥ 2.3s, then shrink back into the anchor over 6 frames.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Subject mask / matte
- **Fallback:** If the object moves too much to track reliably, freeze the frame briefly (M04) or place the label as a lower-third instead of attaching it.

8. Shape Morph# CES style instruction — V08 Shape Morph

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **V08 Shape Morph** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Visual Beats** — A single solid shape transforms through matched states: circle to rounded square to triangle and back, interpolating its outline point by point while rotating slightly. Because the morph is continuous, it reads as one idea becoming another — useful as a transition accent, a background motif behind text or a visual metaphor for change.

### V08 Shape Morph

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:02.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** A bold geometric shape smoothly morphs between compatible forms — circle, square, triangle — rotating as it goes, to mark a change of idea.
- **Parameters:** Shape sequence: Circle → square → triangle; Morph duration: 0.6 s; Hold per shape: 0.4 s; Rotation per morph: 90 deg; Outline only: off; Shape size: 50%; Easing: Ease in-out (smooth); Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Create a shape layer from 00:00.00 to 00:02.50: circle → square → triangle, 50% of frame width (540px), centered unless composed with text.
  2. Represent every state as a closed path with the SAME number of points (resample each shape to 64 points, starting at 12 o'clock, clockwise) so interpolation never flips or tangles. In Remotion use @remotion/paths interpolatePath() or flubber's interpolate(); in After-Effects-style tools use matched vertex counts.
  3. Morph each pair over 0.60s (18 frames) using a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)), holding each state 0.40s, while rotating +90° per morph on the same curve.
  4. Render as a solid #D7FF5F fill with a subtle 10% darker inner shadow for depth; no outline.
  5. Enter by scaling from 0 → 1 over 9 frames and exit by scaling to 0 (or expanding past the frame edges as a transition) over 9 frames. Keep the shape clear of faces unless used as a full transition.
- **Assets:** — · optional: Source video, Brand logo
- **Fallback:** If path morphing is unavailable, approximate with a single element animating border-radius (circle ↔ square) and a clip-path polygon cross-fade for the triangle.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.

**Visual Beats** — Real numbers become a simple, honest chart: bars rise from a zero baseline, a line draws through its points or a donut fills to its share, one series at a time. The value the speaker is talking about is highlighted in the accent colour while the rest stays neutral, and a source line keeps it accountable. Motion reveals the shape of the data in the order it should be read.

### V09 Data Visualization Beat

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:04.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)

  3. Bars grow from the baseline (axis starts at 0), staggered 9 frames apart, each over 22 frames with a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)); value labels fade in as each bar completes.

  5. Hold the completed chart ≥ 2s (longer with more than 5 data points) so viewers can read the labels, then fade out over 9 frames.
- **Assets:** Verified data (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Source video, Brand fonts

**Visual Beats** — Rather than showing the whole sentence at once, the statement is revealed in staged chunks in sync with the speaker, each new chunk appearing as earlier ones settle into a muted tone. The final words — the point — land with more weight and the accent colour. Pacing the reveal makes viewers read along at speaking speed and saves the emphasis for the end.

### V10 Statement Reveal

  4. Earlier chunks settle to 55% opacity in regular weight; the emphasis words arrive last in bold and #D7FF5F, scaling 1.08 → 1.0 over 6 frames.
  5. Hold the complete sentence ≥ 3.3s after the last chunk, then fade out over 8 frames.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** Without word timing, pace at the chosen words-per-second and nudge chunks to audible phrase breaks. Never rewrite the speaker's sentence; trim only with an ellipsis if the user approves.

Animated B-Roll1. Browser Flythrough# CES style instruction — B01 Browser Flythrough

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **B01 Browser Flythrough** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Animated B-Roll** — Instead of a jittery screen recording, a tall static capture of the page is moved by a virtual camera: it scrolls to each focal section, eases to a stop and pushes in so the relevant headline or button fills the frame. A clean browser chrome and an optional guide cursor tell viewers they are looking at a real site, while the controlled pacing gives them time to read. Every pixel comes from the supplied capture — nothing is redrawn.

### B01 Browser Flythrough

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:05.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A supplied full-page website capture glides inside a browser frame, pausing on chosen sections with smooth scroll moves and gentle push-ins.
- **Parameters:** Full-page capture: (not set); Focal stops: 3; Hold per stop: 0.9 s; Push-in at stops: 1.4x; Browser frame: Dark browser chrome; Address bar text: (not set); Guide cursor: on; Easing: Ease in-out (smooth)
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a full-frame website cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:05.00 (1080×1920 @ 30fps). Source: the supplied full-page capture (a tall PNG of the whole page). Build only from the supplied assets — do not invent details, text, UI, numbers or features that are not present in them. Never retype, redraw or restyle page elements.
  2. Wrap the page in a minimal dark browser frame inset 6% from the frame edges: 2.5% corner radius, three muted window dots and an address bar reading the real domain of the captured page (taken from the supplied website URL); leave the bar blank if it is unknown — never type a made-up URL.
  3. Plan 3 focal stops down the page, chosen from sections the speaker actually mentions during this range (e.g. the hero headline, the feature they name, the pricing block). Order them top to bottom so the camera never scrolls backwards.
  4. Animate a virtual camera over the static capture (Remotion: translate the image inside an overflow-hidden viewport): each scroll move takes ≈ 0.77s (23 frames) with a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)); at each stop hold 0.9s (27 frames) and push in to 1.40× centred on the focal element. Scroll first, then push — never both at once. If 1.40× would upscale beyond the capture's native pixels, lower the push-in instead of accepting blur.
  5. Add a small neutral guide cursor (≈ 3% of frame width, soft shadow) that glides to each focal element 6 frames before the hold and taps once (scale 1 → 0.85 → 1 over 6 frames). It only points; it never shows interactions the capture does not contain.
  6. Add 3–4 frames of vertical motion blur during scroll moves only. Enter from the speaker with a 6-frame settle (scale 1.04 → 1) and return on a clean cut. Verify every stop is legible on a phone: the smallest text you rely on must be ≥ 2.5% of frame height after the push-in.
- **Assets:** Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Website capture, Screen recording
- **Fallback:** If only a URL is supplied, capture the page with a headless browser (e.g. Playwright full-page screenshot at 2× pixel ratio, 390–430px mobile width or 1440px desktop) and confirm with the user that it is current. If neither a capture nor a URL exists, ask for one — never mock up a website. With only a screen recording, apply the same crops and holds to its frames but keep its own timing.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.

2. Floating Screenshot Cards# CES style instruction — B02 Floating Screenshot Cards

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **B02 Floating Screenshot Cards** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Animated B-Roll** — Several screenshots are arranged like a loose stack in space, tilted on a shared angle with real depth separation. On each beat one card glides forward, straightens and sharpens while the rest drift back and blur, so viewers always know which image matters. It turns a folder of static screenshots into a guided, cinematic tour.

### B02 Floating Screenshot Cards

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:06.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Supplied screenshots float as tilted cards at different depths; the camera brings one card forward at a time while the others soften behind.
- **Parameters:** Cards: 4 cards; Stack tilt: 14 deg; Depth spread: 40%; Hold per focused card: 1.2 s; Depth-of-field blur: on; Accent colour: #D7FF5F; Easing: Expo out (snappy)
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a 3D card cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:06.00. Lay out 4 supplied screenshots as cards in one shared 3D space: CSS perspective ≈ 1728px, every card rotated 14° on Y and 7° on X, spaced 40% of card width apart in Z and offset diagonally so each card stays partly visible.
  2. Use the screenshots exactly as supplied (native resolution, original crop): 2.5% corner radius, 1px light inner border, soft long shadow (blur ≈ 6% of frame width at 40% opacity). Build only from the supplied assets — do not invent details, text, UI, numbers or features that are not present in them. If a card is missing, use fewer cards rather than fabricating a screen.
  3. Advance the focus in the order the speaker mentions each screen: the focused card moves forward in Z, rotates to 0°, and scales to ≈ 78% of frame width over 14 frames with a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)), then holds 1.2s (36 frames).
  4. Non-focused cards drift back, dim to 55% brightness and blur by depth (≈ 0.4% of frame width per layer behind the focus). The focused card is always perfectly sharp.
  5. Outline the focused card with a 0.4%-wide #D7FF5F hairline that fades in after it lands. Let the whole stack drift continuously (±1.5° Y rotation, ±2% translation) so it never looks frozen.
  6. Timing check: 4 cards × (1.2s hold + ~0.5s move) ≈ 6.8s against a 6.0s range — too long, so drop the least-referenced cards or shorten the hold.
- **Assets:** Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screen recording
- **Fallback:** With only one screenshot use P01 Screenshot Punch instead. If the renderer cannot do 3D transforms, fake the depth in 2D with per-layer scale (0.8–1.0), diagonal offset, brightness and blur.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.

It presents the app as a finished product rather than a raw recording, while the screen content stays exactly as captured.

### B03 Device Showcase

### B04 Explainer Icon Flow

  5. Connectors: SVG paths with pathLength=1, drawn by animating stroke-dashoffset 1 → 0 over 13 frames just before the next node lands. After each connector is drawn, send a small #D7FF5F pulse dot along it once to show direction.
  6. Hold the completed flow ≥ 1s before cutting back, and keep everything inside an 8% safe margin.

5. Parallax Photo Depth# CES style instruction — B05 Parallax Photo Depth

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **B05 Parallax Photo Depth** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Animated B-Roll** — Pre-separated layers of a photo are stacked and moved at speeds proportional to their distance, producing the 2.5D 'camera drift' feel of documentary stills. When no separated layers exist, the style falls back to an honest single-plane push and drift rather than faking a depth split. Movement stays subtle so the photograph still reads as a photograph.

### B05 Parallax Photo Depth

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:04.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A still photo gains gentle depth: supplied foreground, subject and background layers drift at different speeds, or a careful 2D push is used instead.
- **Parameters:** Layer source: Supplied separated layers; Depth layers: 3; Parallax strength: 50%; Direction: Right; Overall push-in: 1.08x; Atmospheric haze: on; Easing: Ease in-out (smooth)
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to the photo cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:04.00, filling 1080×1920. Build only from the supplied assets — do not invent details, text, UI, numbers or features that are not present in them. Never generate or hallucinate image content.
  2. Stack the 3 supplied layers (transparent PNGs separated from one photo, farthest first). If only the flat photo and a subject matte exist, use exactly two layers: background plate and matted subject.
  3. Move each layer right at a speed proportional to its nearness: the farthest layer travels ≈ 0.6% of frame width over the range, the nearest ≈ 2.6%, the rest interpolated linearly. Apply an overall push from 1.0 to 1.08× with a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)).
  4. Overscan every layer by ≥ 13% so no edge is ever revealed. If moving a near layer exposes a hole in the layer behind it, reduce the parallax until no hole shows or ask the user for a clean plate — do not inpaint new detail.
  5. Add a subtle atmospheric haze: lift the farthest layer by ~6% brightness and −8% contrast with a soft gradient, nothing stylised.
  6. Ease in and out over 12 frames so the motion begins and ends gently, and check the edges frame by frame at the extremes of the move.
- **Assets:** Photos (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte
- **Fallback:** Without separated layers or a subject matte, use the conservative 2D push-and-drift only. If the user wants true parallax, ask for layered exports (e.g. from the photo editor) or a clean background plate; never auto-generate missing background.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
6. Product Cutout Reveal# CES style instruction — B06 Product Cutout Reveal

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **B06 Product Cutout Reveal** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Animated B-Roll** — The product image, cut out on transparency, enters on a calm graphic set — rising from below, gliding in or emerging from depth — and settles with a small tilt correction. A single light sweep passes across the product and a contact shadow grounds it, giving a studio packshot feel. Only the supplied product is shown; no features, labels or claims are added.

### B06 Product Cutout Reveal

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A supplied product cutout rises onto a clean graphic backdrop with soft directional motion, a light sweep across its surface and a grounded shadow.
- **Parameters:** Product cutout: (not set); Backdrop: Soft gradient; Background colour: #1A1C22; Entry direction: Rise from below; Light sweep: on; Settle tilt: 4 deg; Accent colour: #D7FF5F; Easing: Expo out (snappy)
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a product reveal from 00:00.00 to 00:03.50 on a soft vertical gradient from #1A1C22 to a 12% lighter tint, with a faint #D7FF5F glow (≤ 15% opacity) behind the product. Product: the supplied product image. Build only from the supplied assets — do not invent details, text, UI, numbers or features that are not present in them.
  2. Check the cutout first: it must be a clean transparent PNG with no halo or leftover background. If it is a JPG on a background, run background removal and show the user the result before using it.
  3. Place the product at ≈ 58% of frame width, optically centred slightly above the middle. Entry: rise from 18% of frame height below its rest position over 21 frames with a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)), starting 4° off-axis and settling to 0° in the last 8 frames.
  4. Ground it with a contact shadow (soft ellipse, multiply, 35% opacity) that grows and sharpens as the product lands.
  5. 6 frames after landing, sweep one 20° diagonal white highlight across the product (screen blend, ≤ 30% opacity, 24 frames), masked to the product's own alpha so it never spills onto the backdrop.
  6. Hold the settled product for the remainder of the range with a ≤ 1% idle float. Add no text, badges or claims unless the user supplied them.
- **Assets:** Product image (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Brand logo, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If no transparent cutout exists, frame the original product photo as a rounded card on the same backdrop instead of attempting a rough cutout. Never redraw or generate a product image.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.

7. Animated Map Journey# CES style instruction — B07 Animated Map Journey

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **B07 Animated Map Journey** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Animated B-Roll** — A minimal map shows only the region that matters. A route line draws from one supplied location to the next, the camera tracks the leading edge, and a pin and label pop in at each stop. Geometry comes from the user's coordinates or a properly licensed map source with attribution — never guessed locations — so the journey is both clear and accurate.

### B07 Animated Map Journey

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:06.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A route line draws between the supplied locations on a clean map while the camera follows it, and each stop's pin and name pop in on arrival.
- **Parameters:** Stop names: (not set); Route style: Road-following line; Camera follows route: on; Follow zoom: 1.6x; Route colour: #D7FF5F; Hold at each stop: 0.7 s; Map look: Dark minimal
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a map cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:06.00. Take the stops and their order from the supplied map-locations file (or the places the speaker names); if fewer than two locations are known, ask the user instead of guessing.
  2. Geometry must be real: use the supplied coordinates/GPX, or geocode the given names with a properly licensed source (e.g. OpenStreetMap/Nominatim, Natural Earth coastlines) and confirm ambiguous matches with the user. Never invent coordinates, borders or roads, and show the source attribution (e.g. "© OpenStreetMap contributors") small in a corner.
  3. Style a dark minimal map (near-black water, charcoal land, faint roads, no POI clutter) cropped to the bounding box of all stops plus 12% padding.
  4. Draw the route in #D7FF5F (stroke ≈ 0.7% of frame width, round caps) by animating stroke-dashoffset: follow the real road/path geometry from the supplied GPX or a routing service (e.g. OSRM). Each leg takes ≈ 1.95s (59 frames), proportional to its distance, with an ease-in-out.
  5. Camera: start on the full route overview, then push to 1.6× and track the leading point of the line, easing so it never moves faster than ~25% of frame width per second; pull back to the overview on the final stop.
  6. At each stop, pop a pin (scale 0 → 1 over 8 frames, back-out) and its label in Inter semibold, then hold 0.7s (21 frames) before the next leg starts.
- **Assets:** Map locations (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Verified data
- **Fallback:** If no locations or coordinates are supplied, ask for them. If licensed map tiles are not available, render coastlines and borders from open vector data (e.g. Natural Earth) as a flat minimal map rather than screenshotting a commercial map service.

8. Abstract Motion Canvas# CES style instruction — B08 Abstract Motion Canvas

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **B08 Abstract Motion Canvas** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Animated B-Roll** — When there is no footage for an idea, a restrained geometric animation can carry it: rings expand for growth, nodes link for connection, a pair orbits for balance, streams of lines for flow. The shapes move with deliberate timing tied to the speech, giving the viewer a visual metaphor rather than a decoration. It uses only basic shapes, so it never misrepresents real people, places or products.

### B08 Abstract Motion Canvas

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:04.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Circles, grids, lines and simple geometry animate in sync to visualise an abstract idea — growth, connection, balance or flow — without literal imagery.
- **Parameters:** Visual concept: Connection (nodes and paths); Palette: Ink + accent; Element density: 8; Animation speed: 1x; Line weight: 0.5%; Background colour: #0B0C0E; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a full-frame abstract canvas from 00:00.00 to 00:04.00 on a #0B0C0E background with a faint 8%-opacity dot grid (spacing 6% of frame width).
  2. Concept "connection": 8 nodes appear on a loose grid; paths draw between neighbours via stroke-dashoffset (15 frames each) until a connected network forms, then one path pulses in the accent.
  3. Build all shapes as SVG in Remotion with strokes of 0.5% of frame width (5px); palette: ink #F4F2EB lines with #D7FF5F for the one element that matters. Use only primitive geometry — no icons, logos, people or places.
  4. Tie the main transformation to the phrase the speaker uses for this idea (transcript word timing); the secondary motion loops quietly at 1.00× speed.
  5. Add no text unless the user supplies it. Keep an 8% safe margin, ease every move (no linear starts), and fade the canvas out over 8 frames before cutting back.
- **Assets:** — · optional: Transcript with timing, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If the idea being discussed is unclear from the transcript, ask the user which concept to visualise rather than choosing a misleading metaphor. A plain slow-moving dot grid is an acceptable neutral fallback.

9. Document Flyover# CES style instruction — B09 Document Flyover

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **B09 Document Flyover** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Animated B-Roll** — A real PDF page or document scan is presented as a physical sheet, then the camera moves in to the specific passages the speaker refers to. Each passage is marked with a highlight, box or underline while the rest of the page softly dims, so viewers can read the actual source wording. It turns 'it says so in the report' into visible, checkable evidence.

### B09 Document Flyover

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:05.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A supplied document page tilts into view, then a guided crop glides between the exact lines that matter while a marker highlights each one.
- **Parameters:** Document page: (not set); Highlighted passages: 2; Highlight style: Marker sweep; Highlight colour: #D7FF5F; Crop zoom: 2.2x; Paper tilt intro: on; Hold per passage: 1.2 s
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a document cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:05.00. Source: the supplied document — rasterise the page at ≥ 300 dpi (e.g. pdftoppm -r 300) so it stays sharp at 2.2×. Build only from the supplied assets — do not invent details, text, UI, numbers or features that are not present in them.
  2. Intro: the page enters as a sheet tilted rotateX 18° / rotate −4° and settles flat over 18 frames, with a soft paper shadow on a dark desk-like backdrop.
  3. Choose 2 passages that the speaker actually quotes or references during this range; locate them by text search in the PDF (e.g. pdftotext -bbox) so the crop targets the real coordinates. Never highlight wording that is not on the page.
  4. Guided crop: push from the full page to 2.2× on passage 1, then glide (15 frames, ease-in-out) to each following passage, holding 1.2s (36 frames) on each. Dim the area outside the current passage to 60% brightness.
  5. Mark each passage with a #D7FF5F marker band (multiply blend, 55% opacity, slightly taller than the text line) that wipes left → right over 11 frames, starting as the crop arrives.
  6. Before export, check for personal data (names, signatures, account numbers) visible in the crop and blur it unless the user confirms it may be shown.
- **Assets:** Document / PDF (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screenshots
- **Fallback:** If only a photo of the document exists, deskew and flatten it first; if the passage cannot be located reliably, ask the user for the page and line. Never retype a document as a mock page.

10. Interface Component Breakdown# CES style instruction — B10 Interface Component Breakdown

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **B10 Interface Component Breakdown** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Animated B-Roll** — The interface is shown as an exploded view: its real layers lift apart along the depth axis in an isometric or low side angle, each tagged with a short label and leader line, then collapse back into the finished screen. Viewers understand how the parts relate — what sits on what — in a few seconds. Layers are cut only along genuine component boundaries from the supplied design or screenshot.

### B10 Interface Component Breakdown

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:05.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A supplied UI screen separates into labelled layers — background, containers, content, controls — that hover apart in 3D, then snap back together.
- **Parameters:** Layers: 4; Explode distance: 60%; View angle: Isometric; Layer labels: on; Layer names: (not set); Accent colour: #D7FF5F; Easing: Ease in-out (smooth)
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to an exploded-UI cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:05.00. Prepare 4 layers from the supplied screen: use the user's layered design export if available; otherwise crop rectangular regions of the screenshot along genuine component boundaries (background, containers/cards, content, controls). Build only from the supplied assets — do not invent details, text, UI, numbers or features that are not present in them. Leave the area under a lifted element as it appears in the screenshot — never repaint hidden pixels.
  2. Start flat and full-frame, then rotate the stack into an isometric view (rotateX 55°, rotateZ −35°) over 18 frames with a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)).
  3. Separate the layers along depth: layer i rises to i × 60% of the screen's height (in Z) over 21 frames, bottom layer first, 2-frame stagger. Give each layer a soft shadow on the one below and a 1px light edge.
  4. Label each layer with a short tag in Inter (mono feel, ≈ 3.2% of frame width) connected by a thin #D7FF5F leader line: derive the names from what the speaker calls each part in the transcript, or ask the user — don't guess component names.
  5. Hold the exploded state ≥ 1.8s, then collapse back (reverse order, 15 frames) and rotate to the flat screen before cutting back.
- **Assets:** Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screen recording
- **Fallback:** If the screenshot cannot be cleanly separated (overlapping translucent elements), explode only the 2–3 layers with clear boundaries, or use a flat front stagger with outlines drawn around each region instead.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.

Signature Effects
Orbit Cards# CES style instruction — X01 Orbit Cards

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X01 Orbit Cards** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — Screenshots, photos or proof cards circle the subject like satellites. Perspective is simulated with scale, brightness and blur: on the far side cards shrink, dim and pass behind the speaker; on the near side they swell and sharpen. With a subject matte the cards truly pass behind; without one, a 2D arc keeps every card in front and fades it out where it would disappear, so the illusion never breaks.

### X01 Orbit Cards

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:04.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Supplied cards orbit the speaker on a tilted elliptical path, slipping behind them on the far side and swinging large and bright across the front.
- **Parameters:** Render mode: Auto (use mask if available, else 2D); Cards: 5 cards; Time per revolution: 6 s; Orbit tilt: 18 deg; Orbit radius: 40%; Direction: Clockwise (seen from above); Orbit ring guide: on
- **Steps:**
  1. PREREQUISITE CHECK: cards passing behind the speaker requires the subject to be cut out so the far-side cards can be composited between the background and the person, which needs a clean subject matte (supplied, or from an external segmentation step checked for edge flicker). If a usable matte exists, build the full version; otherwise use the 2D version — keep every card in front of the subject: shift the ellipse up so its near side passes across the chest/shoulder line (never the eyes or mouth), and on the far half of the orbit fade each card to 0% and shrink it to 0.6× instead of drawing it behind the subject. Tell the user which version was rendered.
  2. Layer order (matte version): background plate → far-side cards and far half of the ring → subject cut out by the matte → near-side cards and near half of the ring → captions. Re-sort each card every frame from its angle (sin θ < 0 = far side).
  3. Geometry: centre the ellipse on the subject's upper chest (≈ 52% of frame height); horizontal radius 40% of frame width (432px), vertical radius 133px (from the 18° tilt). Card i sits at θᵢ = 2π·i/5 + 2π·t/6s, moving clockwise: x = cx + R·cos θ, y = cy + r·sin θ.
  4. Depth cues: map d = (sin θ + 1)/2 to scale 0.62 → 1.12, brightness 0.55 → 1, blur 0.35% → 0% of frame width and shadow strength; rotate each card on Y by −cos θ × 25° so it faces along the orbit.
  5. Cards: the supplied screenshots/photos at ≈ 24% of frame width, 3% corner radius, 1px light border, soft shadow. Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims. Draw a 0.25%-wide ellipse guide at 18% opacity, split into far and near halves so each half follows the same layering as the cards.
  6. Timing: ease the angular speed in over 12 frames and out over the last 12; 6s per revolution gives ≈ 0.8 turns across 4.5s. Keep speed constant in between and verify no card crosses the face.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte, Photos, Social comment
- **Fallback:** Without a subject matte, run the 2D arc: cards travel a front-only arc around the upper body and fade out at the ends instead of passing behind. If segmentation is attempted but flickers or tears hair edges, fall back to 2D rather than shipping a broken matte. With fewer than three cards, use B02 Floating Screenshot Cards instead.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
2. Infinite Content Wall
# CES style instruction — X02 Infinite Content Wall

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X02 Infinite Content Wall** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — A seamless, tilted wall of media — screenshots, thumbnails, comments, results — streams upward and downward in alternating columns, creating a sense of volume and momentum. The speaker stays in front: cut out with a matte for the full effect, or framed in a floating inset card when no matte exists. It reads instantly as 'there is a lot of this', ideal for portfolios, testimonials or catalogues.

### X02 Infinite Content Wall

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:05.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Columns of supplied screenshots and tiles scroll endlessly in alternating directions on a tilted wall behind the speaker, filling the frame.
- **Parameters:** Render mode: Auto (use mask if available, else 2D); Columns: 4; Scroll speed: 12%; Wall tilt: 16 deg; Alternate column direction: on; Wall dim: 40%; Tile shape: Mixed (portrait + square)
- **Steps:**
  1. PREREQUISITE CHECK: placing the speaker in front of a wall that replaces their background requires a subject matte (or footage shot on a clean, keyable background), which needs a clean subject matte (supplied, or from an external segmentation step checked for edge flicker). If a usable matte exists, build the full version; otherwise use the 2D version — keep the original footage intact inside a floating inset card (≈ 64% of frame width, 4% corner radius, soft 6% shadow, centred in the lower half) and run the wall behind the card. Tell the user which version was rendered.
  2. Tiles: crop the supplied screenshots/photos to a mix of 9:16 and 1:1 tiles without distorting them. If there are fewer tiles than slots, repeat the set but never place the same tile next to itself. Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims.
  3. Wall: 4 columns with 1.5% gaps filling 130% of the frame, inside a container with CSS perspective ≈ 1944px rotated rotateX(16°) rotateZ(−9°) so the wall recedes upward.
  4. Seamless scroll: duplicate each column's tile set and translate it by exactly one set height, then wrap (modulo) — no visible jump. Base speed 12% of frame height per second (≈ 230px/s), with ±15% variation per column and odd columns moving in the opposite direction.
  5. Darken the wall by 40% plus a radial vignette so the subject stays dominant; add a soft contact shadow behind the speaker to separate planes.
  6. Bring the wall in over 15 frames (scale 1.1 → 1, opacity 0 → 1) and keep the speaker's face and any captions clear of high-contrast tiles.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte, Photos, Social comment
- **Fallback:** Without a matte, use the 2D inset-card version: the untouched footage floats in a rounded card over the scrolling wall. With fewer than 6 distinct tiles, reduce to 2–3 columns so repetition is less obvious.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.

3. Frame Break# CES style instruction — X03 Frame Break

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X03 Frame Break** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — The footage first plays inside a device or picture frame, then a foreground element pushes past the frame edge: the part beyond the boundary is drawn in front of the bezel while the rest stays inside the screen. The broken boundary creates a playful 3D pop that stops the scroll. True frame break on live footage needs a subject matte; without one, the same pop is done with a supplied transparent cutout such as a product, sticker or still of the subject.

### X03 Frame Break

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** The speaker or a supplied cutout bursts out of a stylised phone frame — rising past the bezel so part of them sits in front of the frame.
- **Parameters:** Render mode: Auto (use mask if available, else 2D); Frame type: Phone frame; Break edge: Top edge; Break distance: 35%; Frame tilt: 8 deg; Overlap shadow: on; Easing: Back out (overshoot)
- **Steps:**
  1. PREREQUISITE CHECK: having the speaker extend past the frame edge requires their matte so the outside portion can be drawn over the bezel, which needs a clean subject matte (supplied, or from an external segmentation step checked for edge flicker). If a usable matte exists, build the full version; otherwise use the 2D version — use a supplied transparent cutout (product PNG, sticker, logo or a still of the subject already cut out) as the breaking element; the live footage stays entirely inside the screen and the cutout bursts out of its top edge. Tell the user which version was rendered.
  2. Composite a stylised, unbranded phone (≈ 62% of frame width, thick dark bezel, 12% corner radius) over a dark, blurred copy of the footage, tilted 8° (rotateX 5°, rotate −3°) with CSS perspective ≈ 2160px. The source footage plays inside the screen area.
  3. Split the breaking element into two copies sharing one transform: copy A clipped to the screen rectangle (behind the bezel), copy B (matte-cut or cutout) clipped to everything beyond the top screen edge and layered above the bezel. Together they read as one element leaving the screen.
  4. Animation: over 17 frames the element rises until 35% of its height is past the top edge, using a back-out curve with slight overshoot (cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1)); hold, then settle back 11% at the end of the range.
  5. Cast a soft shadow from copy B onto the bezel/frame (offset 1% of frame width, blur 2.5%, 40% opacity) — this shadow is what sells the depth.
  6. Verify the boundary on every frame: no halo or missing hair where copies A and B meet, and the face is fully readable. Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte, Product image, Photos
- **Fallback:** Without a subject matte, break the frame with a supplied transparent cutout (product, logo, sticker or a still cutout photo of the subject) instead of the live footage. If no cutout asset exists either, use L04 Floating Device Frame with a punch-in rather than faking the break.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
4. UI World# CES style instruction — X04 UI World

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X04 UI World** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — The speaker is placed inside an environment made of their own product's screens: panels hang at different depths, drift and turn slightly, with a perspective floor grid suggesting space. Panels behind the subject require a matte; in 2D mode all panels float around the edges in front of the footage. The result feels like standing inside the software being discussed.

### X04 UI World

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:05.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Floating interface panels built from supplied screens surround the speaker at several depths, drifting in 3D space as the camera slowly pushes through.
- **Parameters:** Render mode: Auto (use mask if available, else 2D); Panels: 6; Depth layers: 3; Panel drift: 40%; Camera push-in: 1.12x; Perspective floor grid: on; Accent colour: #779CFF
- **Steps:**
  1. PREREQUISITE CHECK: panels hanging behind the speaker requires a subject matte (or footage shot on a clean, keyable background), which needs a clean subject matte (supplied, or from an external segmentation step checked for edge flicker). If a usable matte exists, build the full version; otherwise use the 2D version — place every panel in front of the footage around the frame edges (left, right, top corners), keeping a clear oval around the face and upper body; nothing is drawn behind the subject. Tell the user which version was rendered.
  2. Panels: 6 crops of the supplied screenshots/recordings (≈ 22–34% of frame width), 2.5% radius, 1px #779CFF edge at 50% opacity and a soft outer glow (≤ 20%). Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims.
  3. Depth: distribute panels across 3 depth layers in a CSS 3D scene (perspective ≈ 1620px): far panels at translateZ −40% of frame width, 60% brightness, 0.3% blur; near panels at +15%, full brightness and sharp. Each panel turns 8–20° on Y to face the subject.
  4. Drift: every panel floats on its own slow sine path (±2.2% of frame width, ±4° rotation, period 3–5s, phases offset) so the world feels alive but never busy.
  5. Camera: push the whole world (panels + matted subject) from 1.0 to 1.12× over the range with a smooth ease-in-out; near panels scale ≈ 1.4× faster than far ones to create parallax.
  6. Add a perspective floor grid (#779CFF lines at 18% opacity, fading to 0 toward the horizon at ≈ 62% of frame height) under the subject.
  7. Enter by fading and pushing panels in from depth over 18 frames, staggered by 2 frames, and keep captions on a clear band free of panels.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte, Screen recording
- **Fallback:** Without a matte, use the 2D layout: all panels float in front of the footage around the edges with a clear zone for the face. If only one or two screenshots exist, use B02 Floating Screenshot Cards instead of repeating the same screen many times.

5. Infinite Zoom Tunnel# CES style instruction — X05 Infinite Zoom Tunnel

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X05 Infinite Zoom Tunnel** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — Each image contains the next one, framed at its centre, so a constant exponential zoom carries the viewer through layer after layer. Because the scale grows geometrically, the motion feels steady rather than slowing down, and when the last image nests the first the tunnel loops forever. It is a hypnotic way to move through a series of moments, products or places.

### X05 Infinite Zoom Tunnel

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:04.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Supplied images nest inside one another like frames within frames, and the camera zooms continuously through them in a seamless looping tunnel.
- **Parameters:** Nested layers: 5; Time per layer: 0.9 s; Frame shape: Rounded rectangle; Direction: Zoom in; Nesting ratio: 2.4x; Radial motion blur: on; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a zoom-tunnel cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:04.00. Order the 5 supplied images (each ≥ 1080×1920 after cropping — every layer is seen full-frame at some point). Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims.
  2. Nest them: image i+1 sits centred inside image i at 1/2.4 of its size, inside a rounded-rectangle (4% radius) window with a 0.5%-wide #D7FF5F border and a soft inner shadow. If an image already contains a real screen, window or frame, corner-pin the next image into it instead.
  3. Zoom exponentially, not linearly: layer i's scale = 2.4^(i − t/0.9s), anchored at the frame centre; ≈ 27 frames per layer, ≈ 4.4 layers across the range. Linear scaling would visibly decelerate.
  4. Loop seamlessly: make the last image's window contain the first image, so after 5 layers the composition repeats exactly; drop layers whose scale exceeds 5.8× or falls below 1/5.8× to save render time.
  5. Add a subtle radial zoom blur (2–3% of frame width) to the outermost layer only, fading to sharp at the centre, so the inner image stays readable.
  6. Ease into full speed over 9 frames and out at the end; avoid any brightness pulsing as layers pass.
- **Assets:** Photos (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screenshots, B-roll footage
- **Fallback:** With fewer than 3 images, create a simpler two-layer zoom-through transition (M01-style punch from one image into the next). If images are low resolution, increase the nesting ratio less and reduce layer time so no layer is shown enlarged past its native pixels.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
6. Magnetic Mosaic# CES style instruction — X06 Magnetic Mosaic

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X06 Magnetic Mosaic** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — Tiles float loose across the frame with random rotations, then — in a staggered wave from the centre outward — fly to their grid slots with a springy snap. When the last one lands, the seams close and the pieces become a single picture, either the slices of one supplied image or a curated grid of separate assets. The sense of order emerging from chaos makes it a satisfying reveal.

### X06 Magnetic Mosaic

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:04.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Scattered media tiles drift and spin, then snap magnetically into a grid that assembles one larger supplied image or composition.
- **Parameters:** Grid: 4 × 4; Mosaic content: Slices of one supplied image; Scatter spread: 60%; Snap stagger: 0.05 s; Easing: Spring (physical); Close gaps on completion: on; Accent colour: #D7FF5F
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a mosaic cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:04.00 with a 4×4 grid (16 tiles) occupying ≈ 86% of frame width, centred.
  2. Slice the single supplied image into 16 equal tiles (each tile keeps its exact region — background-position offsets in Remotion), so the completed grid reproduces the original image exactly.
  3. Scatter: start each tile at a random position within 60% of frame size around its slot, rotated ±40°, scaled 0.6–0.9, 0.3% blur, with a slow drift (seeded random so renders are deterministic).
  4. Snap: tiles fly to their slots in a wave from the centre outward, 0.05s (2 frames) apart, each over 14 frames with a physical spring (Remotion spring(): damping ≈ 12, stiffness ≈ 180, mass 1); rotation and blur reach 0 on landing.
  5. After the last tile lands, close the 1% gutters over 9 frames and remove corner radii so the tiles fuse into one image, with a single soft #D7FF5F edge glint.
  6. Hold the completed composition ≥ 1s before cutting back.
- **Assets:** Photos (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screenshots, Brand logo
- **Fallback:** With a single low-resolution image, use a 3×3 grid so tiles are not shown enlarged. Without any images, ask for them — never generate mosaic content.

### X07 Reality Portal

  5. Then expand the portal until it exceeds the frame (≈ 15 frames, ease-in), completing the cut to the second shot; the rim fades out as it leaves the frame edges.
  6. Cue the opening to a spoken beat (e.g. "imagine…", "now look at…") and keep captions above the portal layer.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Second shot (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte, B-roll footage, Sound effects

8. Clone Echo# CES style instruction — X08 Clone Echo

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X08 Clone Echo** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — Copies of the matted subject, each delayed by a few frames and progressively more transparent and tinted, follow the live subject, so a single gesture leaves a visible trail of time. It turns a key movement or statement into a striking visual beat. Isolated echoes require a reliable subject matte; without one, a whole-frame time echo (blended delayed frames) gives an honest, softer version of the look.

### X08 Clone Echo

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:02.50 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** The speaker is duplicated into staggered, translucent echoes that trail their movement in time, fanning out like a stroboscopic multiple exposure.
- **Parameters:** Render mode: Auto (use mask if available, else 2D); Echo copies: 4; Delay between echoes: 4 frames; Opacity falloff: 30%; Echo spread: Time trail (follows motion); Tint echoes: on; Echo tint: #779CFF
- **Steps:**
  1. PREREQUISITE CHECK: isolated subject echoes over a clean background require a reliable subject matte, which needs a clean subject matte (supplied, or from an external segmentation step checked for edge flicker). If a usable matte exists, build the full version; otherwise use the 2D version — build a whole-frame time echo instead: blend 4 delayed copies of the full frame (lighten or screen blend) so movement leaves soft trails while static background stays unchanged; no fake cut-outs. Tell the user which version was rendered.
  2. Create 4 echo layers from 00:00.00 to 00:02.50: echo i shows the subject delayed by i × 4 frames (133ms per step) — in Remotion, render the matted subject <OffthreadVideo> with startFrom offset −i×4.
  3. Opacity per echo: 70%, 49%, 34%, 24% (falloff 30% each). Stack them behind the live subject, oldest at the back.
  4. Spread: each echo uses the subject's own position from its delay (pure time trail), so echoes only separate when the subject moves.
  5. Tint echoes toward #779CFF (colour overlay / hue shift, stronger on older echoes) and add a 0.5% soft glow so they read as light, not as duplicate people.
  6. Use only during a clear movement or gesture; ramp echoes in over 5 frames and collapse them back into the subject over 8 frames at the end of the range.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Subject mask / matte
- **Fallback:** Without a reliable matte, use the whole-frame time echo (blended delayed frames) — it smears moving areas but leaves the static background intact. If the subject barely moves during the range, choose a moment with clear motion or skip the effect.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **X09 Cinematic Carousel** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Signature Effects** — Panels are mounted around a virtual cylinder and the carousel rotates one step per beat, so each panel in turn becomes the large, bright focal card while the rest fall back into perspective and shadow. A low camera tilt and floor reflection give it a cinematic, premium feel. It presents a set of screenshots, photos or clips as a curated showcase rather than a slideshow.

### X09 Cinematic Carousel

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:06.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** Supplied media panels ride a 3D carousel; each turn swings a new card to the front, where it grows dominant while the others recede into shadow.
- **Parameters:** Panels: 6 cards; Time per panel: 0.9 s; Focal card scale: 1.18x; Camera tilt: 8 deg; Side panel dim: 55%; Floor reflection: on; Easing: Expo out (snappy)
- **Steps:**
  1. Cut to a carousel cutaway from 00:00.00 to 00:06.00. Mount 6 supplied panels (≈ 46% of frame width, 3% radius, 1px light edge) around a vertical cylinder: panel i at rotateY(60.0° × i) translateZ(786px), inside a preserve-3d group with CSS perspective ≈ 2376px. Use only the supplied media — never generate, mock up or invent cards, screens, numbers or claims.
  2. Tilt the camera: rotateX(−8°) on the group so the viewer looks slightly down onto the ring; hide back faces (backface-visibility: hidden).
  3. Rotate one step (60.0°) every 0.9s: 14-frame move with a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)), then hold. 6 steps = 5.4s for one full turn. Follow the order in which the speaker mentions each item.
  4. Focal card: the panel facing the camera scales to 1.18×, full brightness and gains a soft spotlight; side panels dim by 55% and blur slightly (0.2–0.4% of frame width) by distance.
  5. Add a floor reflection: a vertically flipped copy of the ring beneath it at 18% opacity, fading out over 25% of frame height, on a dark gradient floor.
  6. Ease the first step in from a stopped carousel and land the final step on the panel the speaker ends on.
- **Assets:** Screenshots (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Photos, B-roll footage, Screen recording
- **Fallback:** With fewer than three panels, use B02 Floating Screenshot Cards. If 3D transforms are unavailable, fake the carousel in 2D: a horizontal row where the centre card is large and bright and side cards are scaled 0.7×, offset and dimmed.

### X10 Blueprint Universe

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:06.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V1 (main picture)
- **What it does:** A plain visual transforms into a blueprint world: grid lines spread, outlines trace each part and animated paths connect the labelled components.

- **Steps:**
  1. Open from 00:00.00 to 00:06.00 on the supplied visual (screenshot, photo or diagram) full-frame, then over 24 frames flood the background to #0F2C5C, desaturate the visual and trace its main shapes as #BFD8FF outlines (stroke-dashoffset), crossfading the image away to leave the line drawing.

  3. Components: take the parts and their relationships strictly from what the speaker explains in the transcript; if the structure is unclear, ask the user for the component list. Never add parts the speaker doesn't mention.
  4. Connections: SVG paths with right-angle routing between related components, drawn via stroke-dashoffset over 1.2s (36 frames) each, in the order the speaker explains them; then send a small #BFD8FF pulse along each path to show direction of flow.
  5. Annotations (technical): add dimension lines with arrowheads, a few numbered callouts and corner registration marks. Dimension marks are decorative — never print measurements or figures that weren't supplied.
  6. Camera: drift slowly across the drawing (≈ 4% of frame width per second) with a 1.0 → 1.1× push, landing centred on the component being discussed at the end.
- **Assets:** Transcript with timing (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Screenshots, Photos, Document / PDF, Brand fonts
- **Fallback:** If no source visual is supplied, start directly on the blueprint field. If the transcript doesn't describe a clear system, ask for the component list rather than inventing an architecture.

Motion Pace
Snap Zoom# CES style instruction — M01 Snap Zoom

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M01 Snap Zoom** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — A short, fast scale-in (typically 115–135%) lands on a meaningful beat — a stressed word, a punchline or a musical hit. The move takes only 3–6 frames with a touch of motion blur, so it reads as a deliberate camera 'punch' rather than a slow zoom. Used sparingly, it tells the viewer 'this line matters' without any graphics.

### M01 Snap Zoom

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (1.00 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** The frame punches in on the speaker's face in a few frames exactly on a stressed word or hit, holds, then releases.
- **Parameters:** Scale: 1.25x; Zoom-in length: 5 frames; Hold: 0.8 s; Release: Ease back out; Beat source: Stressed word (transcript timing); Beat timecode: (not set); Easing: Expo out (snappy); Motion blur on the move: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply a snap zoom to the source video layer at 00:07.50 (window ends 00:08.50). Beat: use word-level transcript timing — place the effect on the first frame of the stressed word nearest 00:07.50. If only sentence timing exists, transcribe locally with word timestamps (e.g. Whisper word_timestamps=True) first.
  2. Scale the footage from 1.00 to 1.25x over 5 frames (167ms at 30fps) using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)). Set the transform origin on the speaker's face (eye-line centre), not the frame centre — in Remotion: transformOrigin at the face box, scale = interpolate(frame, [beat, beat + 5], [1, 1.25], { extrapolateLeft: "clamp", extrapolateRight: "clamp", easing }).
  3. Add motion blur during the 5 zoom frames only: wrap the layer in @remotion/motion-blur <CameraMotionBlur shutterAngle={180} samples={8}> (or in FFmpeg, tmix=frames=3 on those frames). No blur on the held frames.
  4. Hold the 1.25x framing for 0.8s (24 frames). Then ease back to 1.00x over 10 frames with a smooth ease-in-out.
  5. Resolution headroom: probe the source (ffprobe -show_streams). The crop must keep ≥ 864×1536 real source pixels for a 1.25x zoom into a 1080×1920 output. If the source cannot supply that, cap the zoom at sourceWidth ÷ 1080 × 1.1 and report it — never upscale into visible softness.
  6. Keep eyes inside the upper third after the zoom and check that the face does not hit the platform UI safe zone. Use at most one snap zoom per ~4 seconds so it keeps its meaning.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Music track
- **Fallback:** If the source lacks resolution headroom, reduce the zoom to what the pixels allow (often 1.10–1.15x for 1080p into 1080p) and add a light unsharp mask instead of upscaling. Without word timing, ask the user to point to the beat.
