2. Whip Transition# CES style instruction — M02 Whip Transition

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M02 Whip Transition** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — The outgoing shot accelerates sideways with heavy directional motion blur; at peak velocity the cut happens and the incoming shot decelerates into place from the same direction. Because the eye is following the motion, the cut becomes invisible and the change of scene feels energetic and continuous — a classic camera 'whip pan' recreated in the edit.

### M02 Whip Transition

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.33 (0.35 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Shot A whips out of frame in a streak of directional blur and shot B whips in from the same direction, hiding the cut mid-motion.
- **Parameters:** Direction: Left; Transition length: 10 frames; Motion blur strength: 70%; Shutter angle: 270 deg; Easing: Ease in-out (smooth); Settle overshoot: on
- **Steps:**
  1. At the cut boundary 00:07.33, build a 10-frame whip (333ms): 5 frames of shot A exiting and 5 frames of shot B entering, with the actual cut on the fastest frame.
  2. Shot A: translate left by ~648px (60% of frame width) accelerating with an ease-in; shot B: start 648px off the opposite side and decelerate to rest using a smooth ease-in-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1)). Both move in the SAME direction (left).
  3. Motion blur: horizontal only, shutter angle 270° — in Remotion use <CameraMotionBlur shutterAngle={270} samples={9}> around both layers; in FFmpeg approximate with a horizontal directional blur (e.g. dblur=angle=0:radius=18) peaking on the cut frame and falling to zero at both ends.
  4. Let shot B overshoot its resting position by ~2% of frame width, settling back within 4 frames.
  5. Overscale both shots to 1.04x during the whip so frame edges never reveal black; return to 1.00x within 3 frames after landing.
  6. Verify the cut does not split a spoken word: if needed, shift the boundary up to 4 frames to the nearest pause and keep dialogue audio continuous under the whip.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Second shot, B-roll footage, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If both shots are static and very similar, a whip can feel unmotivated — use a simple hard cut or a mask swipe (M09) instead. If motion blur rendering is too slow, use a 3-frame tmix blend around the cut.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.

- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

3. Slow Cinematic Push# CES style instruction — M03 Slow Cinematic Push

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M03 Slow Cinematic Push** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — A slow, almost subliminal scale-in (around 103–110% over 4–10 seconds) toward the subject's eye-line. Viewers rarely notice the move itself, but they feel the growing closeness and importance — the same technique dolly-ins use in film to underline an emotional or serious passage.

### M03 Slow Cinematic Push

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:06.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** The shot creeps in by a few percent over several seconds toward the speaker's eyes, raising intensity without a visible move.
- **Parameters:** Scale: 1.08x; Duration: 6 s; Push toward: Speaker's eye-line; Lateral drift: 0.5%; Easing: Linear; Restart after each cut: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply a slow push-in to the source video layer at 00:00.00 (window ends 00:06.00), lasting 6.0s (180 frames).
  2. Scale from 1.000 to 1.080x across the whole range using linear interpolation, with transform origin at the speaker's eye-line (detect the face box once and keep the origin fixed). That is 1.33% per second — slow enough that it is felt rather than seen.
  3. Add a 0.5% (5px) lateral drift in one direction over the same range for a dolly feel; never reverse direction mid-shot.
  4. Render with sub-pixel precision (Remotion transforms are fine; in FFmpeg use zoompan with d=1 and a high-resolution intermediate, or scale=2160:-1 before zoompan) so the push does not shimmer or step.
  5. If the range contains cuts, restart the push from 1.000x on every new shot.
  6. Resolution headroom: probe the source (ffprobe -show_streams). The crop must keep ≥ 1000×1778 real source pixels for a 1.08x zoom into a 1080×1920 output. If the source cannot supply that, cap the zoom at sourceWidth ÷ 1080 × 1.1 and report it — never upscale into visible softness.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING)
- **Fallback:** On low-resolution sources keep the push at ≤ 1.04x and lengthen it, or skip it — a soft, stepping push looks worse than none.

4. Freeze Beat# CES style instruction — M04 Freeze Beat

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M04 Freeze Beat** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — At a major emphasis point the picture holds on a single frame for under a second, often with a two-frame flash and a tiny scale bump. The sudden stillness in a moving video makes the viewer look harder at that exact moment — a face, a gesture, an object — before motion resumes. Unlike a full 'freeze and declare' hook, it is a quick punctuation mark that can be reused through the edit.

### M04 Freeze Beat

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (0.80 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Playback stops dead on a decisive frame for a beat — with a short flash and a slight punch — then resumes exactly where it left off.
- **Parameters:** Freeze length: 0.8 s; Freeze frame timecode: (not set); Frozen-frame treatment: Flash + slight punch; Flash length: 2 frames; Punch-in on freeze: 1.06x; Audio during freeze: Pause the dialogue with the picture
- **Steps:**
  1. Choose the freeze frame at or just after 00:07.50: the sharpest frame of the key gesture or expression (no motion blur, eyes open). Report the timecode you chose.
  2. Hold that single frame for 0.8s (24 frames). In Remotion wrap the footage in <Freeze frame={freezeFrame}> inside a <Sequence durationInFrames={24}>, then continue the source from the very next frame with startFrom offset by the held duration; in FFmpeg use trim + tpad=stop_mode=clone:stop_duration=0.80 and concatenate.
  3. On the first frozen frame overlay a white solid at 60% for 2 frame(s) (67ms) and step the scale to 1.06x instantly; return to 1.00x on resume.
  4. Pause the dialogue audio for exactly the same 24 frames (insert silence or room tone) so lip-sync is preserved after resume.
  5. Use no more than one freeze beat per ~8 seconds and never on a frame with closed eyes or mid-blink.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If no frame is sharp enough (heavy motion blur), freeze 1–3 frames later on a steadier frame, or use a snap zoom (M01) instead of a freeze.

5. Rhythm Cuts# CES style instruction — M05 Rhythm Cuts

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M05 Rhythm Cuts** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — Instead of cutting wherever is convenient, every cut is snapped to a documented beat marker — kicks and downbeats of the supplied music, or the natural phrase breaks in speech. The edit gains a pulse the viewer feels physically, and montage sections stop feeling random. Beat markers are measured from the audio, never guessed.

### M05 Rhythm Cuts

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Edit points and shot changes land on detected music beats or natural speech phrasing, so the footage cuts in time with the track.
- **Parameters:** Beat source: Music beats (supplied track); Cut every: Every 2 beats; Tempo hint: 120 bpm; Cut lead: 1 frames; Minimum shot length: 0.6 s; Never cut mid-word: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Build beat markers from the supplied music: run beat tracking (librosa.beat.beat_track / aubio tempo) and keep every detected beat. Save markers.json with seconds and frame numbers at 30fps. Never fabricate a grid without analysing the track.
  2. Sanity-check: the detected tempo should be near 120 bpm (or half/double it). At that tempo, cutting every 2 beat(s) means a shot change roughly every 1.00s (30 frames).
  3. Across the edit, snap each cut to the marker list, placing the cut 1 frame(s) before the marker (picture leads the beat). Skip markers that would create a shot shorter than 0.6s.
  4. Never cut in the middle of a spoken word: if a marker falls inside a word, move the cut to the nearest word boundary within ±3 frames, or skip that marker.
  5. Implement as a list of <Sequence from={cutFrame}> segments (Remotion) or an FFmpeg concat list with exact frame-accurate trim points; keep the original dialogue audio continuous under the picture cuts.
  6. Verify by exporting a low-res preview with a 1-frame white tick burned in on each marker and confirm cuts coincide.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Music track, Transcript with timing, B-roll footage
- **Fallback:** No music and no transcript? Ask for one of them, or derive phrasing markers from silence detection (ffmpeg silencedetect=noise=-35dB:d=0.18). Never invent a beat grid from a guessed BPM.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**

6. Speed Ramp# CES style instruction — M06 Speed Ramp

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M06 Speed Ramp** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — Time remapping eases the footage between speeds — for example 1.0x → 0.25x on the decisive moment → 2x to skip the follow-through — instead of switching abruptly. The viewer gets to savour the important instant while the edit still feels fast overall. It needs smooth source motion and, for real slow motion, a high-frame-rate or interpolated source.

### M06 Speed Ramp

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Playback glides from normal speed into slow motion on the key moment, then accelerates past real time before settling back.
- **Parameters:** Ramp profile: 1x → slow → fast → 1x; Slow speed: 0.25x; Fast speed: 2x; Ramp length: 10 frames; Slow-motion frames: Frame blending; Audio during ramp: Mute source, let music carry
- **Steps:**
  1. Probe the source first: ffprobe -show_entries stream=r_frame_rate,avg_frame_rate. If r_frame_rate ≠ avg_frame_rate the file is variable-frame-rate — convert to constant 30fps (ffmpeg -vf fps=30 -c:v libx264 -crf 14) BEFORE any time remapping, otherwise ramps stutter and drift out of sync.
  2. Remap time over the range at 00:00.00 (window ends 00:03.00) with the speed curve 1.0x → 0.25x → 2x → 1.0x. Each speed change eases over 10 frames (333ms) with a smooth ease-in-out — no instant jumps. Place the slowest point exactly on the key action frame.
  3. Implementation: in Remotion compute the source frame as the running integral of the speed curve (sum speed(f) per output frame) and pass it to <OffthreadVideo> via a <Freeze frame={sourceFrame}> per frame, or pre-render; in FFmpeg split the range into segments and use setpts=PTS/0.25 (slow) and setpts=PTS/2 (fast), with short intermediate segments stepping through the ramp.
  4. For the 0.25x section blend adjacent frames (ffmpeg framerate=fps=30:interp_start=0:interp_end=255 or tblend=all_mode=average) to avoid visible stepping; real high-frame-rate source frames always take priority.
  5. Mute the source audio across the ramp and let the music bed carry it; restore dialogue in sync at the first 1.0x frame.
  6. Check: total duration change is reported, no frame is dropped at segment joins, and motion reads smooth at the slowest point.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Music track, Sound effects
- **Fallback:** With 24–30fps source and no interpolation available, keep the slow speed at ≥ 0.5x or use a freeze beat (M04) instead; never present stepped frames as smooth slow motion.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
7. Subtle Camera Shake# CES style instruction — M07 Subtle Camera Shake

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M07 Subtle Camera Shake** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — A short burst of controlled positional and rotational jitter — usually under 1% of frame width and half a degree — decaying over a third of a second around an impact moment. It adds physical weight to a reveal, a landing or a strong statement. The frame is slightly overscaled so edges never show, and the noise is deterministic so renders are repeatable.

### M07 Subtle Camera Shake

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (0.40 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** The whole frame jolts with a few decaying micro-offsets and tiny rotations at an impact, as if the camera felt the hit.
- **Parameters:** Position amplitude: 0.8%; Rotation amplitude: 0.4 deg; Shake length: 12 frames; Jitter frequency (per s): 18; Decay: Exponential (sharp hit); Motion blur on jitter: off
- **Steps:**
  1. Shake the source video layer at 00:07.50 (window ends 00:07.90) for 12 frames (400ms), starting on the exact impact frame (the visual contact or the transient of the paired impact sound).
  2. Offsets: x and y = noise(seed, t × 18) × 0.8% of frame width (peak 9px); rotation = noise(seed+1, t × 18) × 0.4°. Use deterministic noise (Remotion @remotion/noise noise2D) so every render is identical.
  3. Envelope: multiply all offsets by exp(−5 × progress) so the first 2–3 frames carry most of the energy, ending exactly at zero.
  4. Overscale the layer to 1.026x for the shake duration (ease in/out over 2 frames) so no frame edge or black border is ever revealed.
  5. No motion blur — crisp jitter reads as an impact.
  6. Apply the shake to the footage layer only; captions and graphics stay steady unless they are part of the impact.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Sound effects
- **Fallback:** If the footage is already handheld and shaky, reduce amplitude by half or use a single 2-frame offset instead; never stack shake on shake.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
8. Seamless Match Out# CES style instruction — M08 Seamless Match Cut

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M08 Seamless Match Cut** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — Two supplied shots are aligned so that something in the last frame of A — a round object, a horizon line, a movement direction — matches its counterpart in the first frame of B in position, size and angle. The eye stays locked on the shape while the world around it changes, creating a clever, satisfying transition. It only works with a genuine second shot that contains a matching element.

### M08 Seamless Match Cut

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.35 (0.30 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** Shot A cuts to shot B where a shared shape, line or motion sits in exactly the same place, so the change of scene feels continuous.
- **Parameters:** Match on: Shared shape (e.g. round object → sun); Matching element: (not set); Second shot: (not set); Alignment tolerance: 3%; Dissolve bridge: 0 frames; Carry camera move across: on
- **Steps:**
  1. This technique requires a real second shot. Ask the user for shot B (asset kind "second-shot") before editing — never fabricate or generate one.
  2. Find the matching element yourself: inspect the last 2 seconds of A and the first 2 seconds of B for a shared shape (similar outline and size); report the pair you chose and the frames used.
  3. Measure the element's centre, size and angle in both frames. Transform shot B (translate, scale ≤ 1.15x within resolution headroom, rotate ≤ 3°) so the mismatch is within 3% of frame width (32px).
  4. Join with a hard cut on the exact frame — no dissolve.
  5. Continue the same camera move (push or drift direction and speed) for at least 12 frames after the cut so motion is continuous across it.
  6. Colour-match B to A for the 10 frames around the cut (exposure and white balance) and preview at full speed — the cut should feel like one shot transforming.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Second shot (⚠ MISSING) · optional: B-roll footage
- **Fallback:** If no second shot is supplied or no element genuinely matches, do not fake one — use a whip transition (M02) or a clean cut, and tell the user what kind of shot B would enable a match cut.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.

9. Mask Swipe# CES style instruction — M09 Mask Swipe

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M09 Mask Swipe** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — A geometric mask — a straight edge, split, circle or set of staggered bars — animates across the frame and reveals the next shot behind it, often with a thin accent line riding the edge. It is a clean, graphic transition that fits editorial and tech content, gives the viewer a clear direction of travel, and needs no special footage.

### M09 Mask Swipe

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.25 (0.50 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** A hard-edged mask sweeps across the frame in one direction, wiping away the current shot to reveal the next one beneath it.
- **Parameters:** Mask shape: Straight edge; Direction: Left; Swipe length: 14 frames; Edge softness: 0%; Accent line on the edge: on; Accent colour: #D7FF5F; Easing: Expo out (snappy)
- **Steps:**
  1. At the cut 00:07.25, overlap the outgoing shot (top) and incoming shot (below) for 14 frames (467ms).
  2. Animate a straight-edged rectangle mask whose leading edge travels left across the full frame over those 14 frames using a fast expo-out curve (cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1)); the mask removes the outgoing shot and reveals the incoming one.
  3. Remotion: drive clipPath (inset() for linear/split/bars, circle() for iris) with interpolate(frame, [0, 14], [0, 100]); FFmpeg: use xfade=transition=wipeleft:duration=0.47.
  4. Keep the mask edge crisp (no feather).
  5. Draw a 6px #D7FF5F line riding the mask's leading edge with a subtle glow; it appears with the mask and disappears on the final frame.
  6. Do not let the wipe edge cross the speaker's face mid-word; time it over a pause or start it after the sentence ends.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Second shot, B-roll footage
- **Fallback:** Mask swipes need no special assets. If the incoming shot is missing, wipe to a solid brand-colour card or the next sequence beat instead of an invented visual.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

10. Stutter Motion# CES style instruction — M10 Stutter Motion

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **M10 Stutter Motion** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Motion & Pace** — A short burst of time manipulation — the last few frames repeat two or three times, or the motion steps at a low frame rate — accents a beat the way a DJ stutter accents a drop. It reads as intentional because it is short, rhythmic and locked to a beat, then playback returns to normal immediately.

### M10 Stutter Motion

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (0.50 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** V2+ (overlay above the picture)
- **What it does:** On a chosen beat the footage briefly repeats a few frames or drops into choppy stepped motion, like a deliberate skip, then runs normally again.
- **Parameters:** Stutter pattern: Repeat the last frames; Repeats: 3; Frames per group: 3 frames; Step rate: 8 fps; Beat source: Waveform peak / music hit; Audio: Keep original audio running
- **Steps:**
  1. Place the stutter at 00:07.50 (window ends 00:08.00). Beat: find the strongest transient in the dialogue/music waveform inside the instance window (e.g. ffmpeg -af "astats=metadata=1:reset=1" or librosa onset_detect) and snap the effect start to that frame. Report the frame you chose.
  2. Take the 3 frames that lead into the beat and play them 3 times in a row (9 frames, 300ms), then continue from the frame after the group. Remotion: sourceFrame = groupStart + ((frame − stutterStart) % 3) during the stutter; FFmpeg: loop=loop=2:size=3:start=<groupStartFrame>.
  3. Accent the first frame of each repeat with a 1-frame +6% brightness bump so the rhythm is visible.
  4. Keep the original audio running continuously underneath; trim the picture after the stutter so lip-sync is restored by the next word.
  5. Keep the whole stutter to about 300ms (never longer than ~600ms) and use it at most once per ~10 seconds.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Music track, Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** If there is no clear beat, use a single freeze beat (M04) or skip the effect; a random stutter reads as a playback error.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.

# CES style instruction — S01 Impact Hit

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S01 Impact Hit** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — A licensed impact (boom, thud or punchy hit) is placed so its transient lines up with the frame where a big title, number or reveal lands. Mixed below the dialogue with a fast fade, it gives visual moments physical weight. Used only on genuinely major beats, it trains the viewer to notice what matters.

### S01 Impact Hit

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (0.80 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** A short, punchy impact sound lands on the exact frame a major graphic or reveal hits the screen, mixed under the voice.
- **Parameters:** Impact sound file: (not set); Level: -12 dB; Sync offset (ms): -20; Character: Punchy hit; Tail fade (ms): 250; Dialogue-safe music dip: -3 dB
- **Steps:**
  1. ASSET REQUEST: no sound effect was supplied. Ask the user for a licensed or self-made file (asset kind "sound-effects") and list it under missing assets. Never download copyrighted audio. Until it arrives: leave the moment silent (optional silence-based fallback) rather than synthesising a fake impact.
  2. Place the impact at 00:07.50 so its transient peak sits -20ms (-1 frames at 30fps) relative to the frame where the graphic lands. Align on the waveform peak, not the file start — trim any pre-roll silence first.
  3. Level: peak -12 dBFS on the SFX bus (≈ -18 to -16 LUFS short-term), always at least 4 dB under the dialogue's short-term loudness when they overlap. High-pass at 40 Hz; a gentle +2 dB shelf at 3 kHz keeps the attack audible on phone speakers.
  4. Fade the tail out over 250ms with an exponential curve; cut anything after the fade. Dip the music bed by -3 dB for the length of the hit (10ms attack, 200ms release).
  5. Remotion: <Sequence from={hitFrame + (-20 / 1000) * fps}><Audio src={staticFile("…")} volume={0.25} /></Sequence>; FFmpeg: adelay + volume=-12dB + afade=t=out, mixed with amix=normalize=0.
  6. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed. Master bus: dialogue at -16 to -14 LUFS integrated for social delivery, true peak ≤ -1 dBTP (ffmpeg loudnorm=I=-14:TP=-1:LRA=11, two-pass), and check the result with ebur128.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Sound effects (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Music track
- **Fallback:** If no licensed impact sound is supplied, request one and render the moment without it (silence-based fallback) — or use the S05 silence drop to create emphasis with no new audio.

- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.

2. Cinematic Whoosh# CES style instruction — S02 Cinematic Whoosh

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S02 Cinematic Whoosh** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — A licensed whoosh or swish is fitted to a substantial directional transition — a whip, swipe or fly-through — so its loudest point sits on the fastest frame. Panning it with the motion and filtering out the low end keeps it airy and out of the voice's way. The result makes transitions feel fast and expensive rather than abrupt.

### S02 Cinematic Whoosh

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (0.50 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** A whoosh swells into a big transition and peaks on the cut, panned in the direction of the on-screen motion.
- **Parameters:** Whoosh sound file: (not set); Level: -16 dB; Align peak to: The cut frame; Whoosh length (ms): 450; Stereo movement: Follow the on-screen direction; High-pass filter (Hz): 150
- **Steps:**
  1. ASSET REQUEST: no sound effect was supplied. Ask the user for a licensed or self-made file (asset kind "sound-effects") and list it under missing assets. Never download copyrighted audio. Until it arrives: leave the transition dry (silence-based fallback) — never improvise noise sweeps that pretend to be a designed whoosh.
  2. Fit the whoosh to the transition at 00:07.50: trim or time-stretch (pitch preserved) to ~450ms (14 frames) and place it so its loudest point lands on the cut frame. The swell should start ~270ms before that point.
  3. Level: peak -16 dBFS; it must sit ≥ 6 dB under dialogue if the transition overlaps speech. Apply a 150 Hz high-pass (12 dB/oct) so it stays airy.
  4. Automate stereo pan with the on-screen motion: start ~60% toward the side the motion comes from and sweep to ~60% on the exit side across the whoosh (FFmpeg: split into short segments with pan= gains, or apulsator-free manual automation in Remotion via two <Audio> elements with crossfaded volume). Check it collapses cleanly to mono.
  5. Fade in 30ms and out 80ms to avoid clicks; no reverb tail longer than 300ms.
  6. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed. Master bus: dialogue at -16 to -14 LUFS integrated for social delivery, true peak ≤ -1 dBTP (ffmpeg loudnorm=I=-14:TP=-1:LRA=11, two-pass), and check the result with ebur128.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Sound effects (⚠ MISSING)
- **Fallback:** Without a licensed whoosh, request one and render the transition dry; the visual transition still works. Never generate a white-noise sweep and present it as a designed sound.

3. Tension Riser# CES style instruction — S03 Tension Riser

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S03 Tension Riser** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — A licensed riser — rising noise, strings or synth — fades up from near-silence over a few seconds, with a filter opening as it climbs, and stops exactly as the reveal lands (often into an impact or silence). The growing sound creates anticipation the viewer feels physically, so the reveal arrives as a release.

### S03 Tension Riser

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:03.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** A rising swell climbs in pitch and volume for a few seconds and cuts off right on the frame of an important reveal.
- **Parameters:** Riser sound file: (not set); Rise length: 3 s; Start level: -36 dB; Peak level: -12 dB; Rise curve: Exponential (late surge); Ending: Hard cut on the reveal; Low-pass filter sweep: on
- **Steps:**
  1. ASSET REQUEST: no sound effect was supplied. Ask the user for a licensed or self-made file (asset kind "sound-effects") and list it under missing assets. Never download copyrighted audio. Until it arrives: skip the riser and let the reveal land on a short silence drop instead.
  2. Place the riser so it ends exactly on 00:03.00; it starts 3.0s earlier (00:00.00). Trim the file's own ending so the loudest point is the last frame before the reveal.
  3. Automate gain from -36 dB to -12 dB peak on an exponential curve (most of the rise in the final third). Keep the riser ≥ 4 dB under dialogue while speech is present; it may peak above the music bed only in the final 300ms.
  4. Sweep a low-pass filter from 400 Hz to 12 kHz across the rise (Remotion: pre-render; FFmpeg: segment-wise lowpass=f=… with crossfades, or sox/rubberband automation).
  5. Hard-stop the riser on the reveal frame with a 5ms fade to avoid a click.
  6. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed. Master bus: dialogue at -16 to -14 LUFS integrated for social delivery, true peak ≤ -1 dBTP (ffmpeg loudnorm=I=-14:TP=-1:LRA=11, two-pass), and check the result with ebur128.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Sound effects (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Music track
- **Fallback:** Without a licensed riser, request one; meanwhile build anticipation visually and use a silence drop (S05) on the reveal. Do not synthesise a pseudo-riser and present it as finished sound design.

4. Keyboard Ticks# CES style instruction — S04 Keyboard Ticks

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S04 Keyboard Ticks** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — When text types onto the screen — a search query, terminal line or typewriter caption — subtle keystroke samples trigger on the same frames as the characters. Randomised pitch and level stop it sounding like a machine gun, and a rate cap keeps fast typing from becoming noise. It makes typing graphics feel tactile and real.

### S04 Keyboard Ticks

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (1.50 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** Soft, varied key clicks play in sync with each character of an on-screen typing animation, quiet enough to sit under the voice.
- **Parameters:** Key-click samples: (not set); Level: -26 dB; Tick on: Every character; Humanise: 30%; Max ticks per second: 16; Sync offset (ms): 0
- **Steps:**
  1. ASSET REQUEST: no sound effect was supplied. Ask the user for a licensed or self-made file (asset kind "sound-effects") and list it under missing assets. Never download copyrighted audio. Until it arrives: render the typing animation silently (silence-based fallback).
  2. Read the typing animation's per-character reveal frames at 00:07.50 (from the graphic's own timing data, not by guesswork) and trigger one tick per visible character (skip spaces) at 0ms (0 frames) relative to the frame it appears.
  3. Cap the rate at 16 ticks per second (min 63ms apart); when typing is faster, drop ticks rather than overlap them.
  4. Humanise: rotate between at least 3 different samples and vary pitch by ±1.2 semitones and level by ±1.5 dB per tick (seeded random so renders repeat).
  5. Level: peak -26 dBFS — clearly below dialogue (≥ 10 dB under speech). High-pass at 200 Hz; pan subtly (±15%) if the text is off-centre.
  6. Remotion: map each reveal frame to a <Sequence from={frame}><Audio …/></Sequence>; FFmpeg: generate an adelay list and amix=normalize=0. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Sound effects (⚠ MISSING)
- **Fallback:** Without licensed key-click samples, request them and keep the typing silent. Recording a few real keystrokes on the user's own keyboard is a valid self-made alternative.

5. Silence Drop# CES style instruction — S05 Silence Drop

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S05 Silence Drop** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — Right before a key statement or reveal, the supporting audio — music bed, ambience and effects — drops by 30–40 dB in a few frames, leaving only room tone. The sudden absence of sound makes viewers lean in, and the line that follows feels heavier. The voice itself is never altered; only the layers around it disappear and return.

### S05 Silence Drop

- **When:** ≈ 00:00.00 → 00:01.20 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** The music and effects fall away to near-silence for a breath just before a major line, so the statement lands in a quiet room.
- **Parameters:** Silence length: 0.6 s; Drop depth: -36 dB; What drops: Music and effects only; Fade-out (ms): 80; Return: Hard return on the statement; Keep room tone: on
- **Steps:**
  1. The statement begins at 00:01.20; start the drop 0.6s before it (00:00.60).
  2. Automate the music bed and SFX buses down by -36 dB over 80ms (2 frames) with an equal-power curve.
  3. Keep natural room tone at about -60 dBFS throughout the drop (use a clean stretch of the source's own background noise) so it never sounds like a dropout.
  4. Return the supporting audio to its previous level in 1 frame exactly on the statement's first word.
  5. Dialogue is untouched by the drop. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed.
  6. Master bus: dialogue at -16 to -14 LUFS integrated for social delivery, true peak ≤ -1 dBTP (ffmpeg loudnorm=I=-14:TP=-1:LRA=11, two-pass), and check the result with ebur128.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Music track, Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** Silence drops need no new assets. If the video has no music or effects to drop, ask whether a short dialogue pause is acceptable, or skip the technique.

6. Beat Synchronization# CES style instruction — S06 Beat Synchronization

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S06 Beat Synchronization** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — The music track is run through beat and downbeat detection to produce a frame-accurate beat map. Edits and animation keyframes are then placed on those markers — downbeats for big moments, off-beats for small accents — and the music is trimmed to start and end on bar lines. The edit and the soundtrack feel like one piece instead of a video with a song pasted under it.

### S06 Beat Synchronization

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** The supplied music is analysed for beats and downbeats, and cuts, zooms and graphic entrances are snapped to that measured beat map.
- **Parameters:** Music track: (not set); Tempo hint: 0 bpm; Sync to the beat: Cuts + zoom/graphic accents; Beat division: Every beat; Sync tolerance: 1 frames; Music level under voice: -22 dB
- **Steps:**
  1. ASSET REQUEST: no music track was supplied. Ask the user for a licensed or self-made file (asset kind "music") and list it under missing assets. Never download copyrighted audio. Until it arrives: build the edit on speech phrasing only and leave a clearly marked empty music lane.
  2. Analyse the track: beat + downbeat detection (librosa.beat.beat_track with onset envelope, or madmom for downbeats); export beats.json with time (s) and frame at 30fps. Report the detected tempo. Never invent a grid without analysis.
  3. Use every beat, with downbeats marked as strong as sync points.
  4. Place cuts on strong beats and zoom/graphic accent keyframes on the remaining beats, within ±1 frame(s) of a marker, never mid-word.
  5. Trim the music to start on a downbeat and end on a bar line (cut at a zero crossing with a 10ms fade). Mix the bed at -22 LUFS short-term under speech and up to -16 LUFS in speech-free passages.
  6. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed. Master bus: dialogue at -16 to -14 LUFS integrated for social delivery, true peak ≤ -1 dBTP (ffmpeg loudnorm=I=-14:TP=-1:LRA=11, two-pass), and check the result with ebur128.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Music track (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** No music supplied: request a licensed or self-owned track. Until then, sync to speech phrasing from the transcript and leave the music lane empty — never download a copyrighted song.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — Restrained foley — a soft tap when a button is pressed, a paper slide when a card moves, a gentle click on a toggle — is matched to visible interactions in UI animations or object movement. Kept far below the voice and slightly varied, it makes graphics feel physical without ever drawing attention to itself.

### S07 Subtle Foley Accents

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (2.00 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** Quiet taps, clicks and soft slides play on the frames where on-screen buttons are pressed or objects move, adding tactile realism.
- **Parameters:** Foley sample set: (not set); Level: -28 dB; Accent: Both; Sync offset (ms): -10; Variation: 25%; Pan to on-screen position: on
- **Steps:**
  1. ASSET REQUEST: no sound effect was supplied. Ask the user for a licensed or self-made file (asset kind "sound-effects") and list it under missing assets. Never download copyrighted audio.   4. Level: peak -28 dBFS, at least 12 dB under dialogue; high-pass at 150 Hz. Vary pitch ±1.0 semitones and alternate samples so repeats never sound identical.
  5. Pan each accent to the horizontal position of its on-screen event (left edge = −40%, right edge = +40%); keep it mono-compatible.
  6. Skip accents that would land within 120ms of a stressed word, and never stack more than 3 per second. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed.

8. Dynamic Voice Ducking# CES style instruction — S08 Dynamic Voice Ducking

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S08 Dynamic Voice Ducking** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — A sidechain or transcript-driven gain automation lowers the music by a set amount while dialogue is active, with a fast attack and a slower, natural release. The music can be full and present between sentences without ever fighting the voice. It is the single most important mix move for talking videos with a music bed.

### S08 Dynamic Voice Ducking

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** The music bed automatically dips whenever someone speaks and swells back in the pauses, keeping every word clear.
- **Parameters:** Method: Sidechain compressor (voice keys the music); Duck amount: -12 dB; Attack (ms): 30; Release (ms): 400; Look-ahead (ms): 40; Dialogue target (LUFS): -14
- **Steps:**
  1. Across the whole video, duck the supplied music bed under dialogue by -12 dB. The music is user-supplied or licensed only; if none is supplied, request it and skip this step.
  2. Use the dialogue as the sidechain key: ffmpeg -filter_complex "[music][voice]sidechaincompress=threshold=0.03:ratio=6:attack=30:release=400:makeup=1[ducked]" — tune threshold so the music reaches about -12 dB of gain reduction on normal speech.
  3. Bring dialogue to -14 LUFS integrated with gentle gain only. Between sentences the music may rise to about -20 LUFS short-term; under speech it should sit around -26 LUFS short-term.
  4. Listen to every transition: no pumping on short words, no music swell inside a single sentence (raise release or merge gaps if it pumps).
  5. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed. Final pass: true peak ≤ -1 dBTP (loudnorm two-pass) and confirm with ebur128.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Music track (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Transcript with timing
- **Fallback:** With no music bed there is nothing to duck — skip the style. If the voice track cannot be separated from the music, use transcript-based keyframes instead of a sidechain.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.

9. Transition Bass Pulse# CES style instruction — S09 Transition Bass Pulse

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S09 Transition Bass Pulse** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — A brief low-frequency accent — a 40–60 Hz pulse with a fast decay — lands on a chapter or section transition. It gives the change a physical 'gear shift' without the brightness of an impact, and on headphones or good speakers it is felt as much as heard. A self-generated sine pulse is an acceptable source when no licensed sample is supplied.

### S09 Transition Bass Pulse

- **When:** ≈ 00:07.50 (0.60 s) (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** A single short sub-bass thump marks a chapter change, felt more than heard, with the music briefly ducking around it.
- **Parameters:** Pulse source: Supplied sub-bass sample; Sub-bass sample: (not set); Pulse frequency (Hz): 50; Level: -14 dB; Decay (ms): 600; Sync offset: 0 frames
- **Steps:**
  1. ASSET REQUEST: no sound effect was supplied. Ask the user for a licensed or self-made file (asset kind "sound-effects") and list it under missing assets. Never download copyrighted audio. Until it arrives: switch Pulse source to "Generate a sine pulse locally", or leave the transition without a pulse.
  2. Place the pulse at 00:07.50 on the chapter transition frame exactly. The pulse's attack must be under 10ms.
  3. Level: peak -14 dBFS. Low-pass at 125 Hz so it carries no midrange, keep it mono, and duck the music bed by 4 dB for 600ms around it so low ends do not stack.
  4. Phone-speaker check: small speakers cannot reproduce 50 Hz — add a faint 2nd harmonic (100 Hz, -12 dB relative) or a soft transient click so the accent is still perceptible.
  5. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed. Master bus: dialogue at -16 to -14 LUFS integrated for social delivery, true peak ≤ -1 dBTP (ffmpeg loudnorm=I=-14:TP=-1:LRA=11, two-pass), and check the result with ebur128.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Sound effects, Music track
- **Fallback:** No sample supplied: generate a simple sine pulse locally (self-made audio is acceptable), or skip the accent. Never download a branded or copyrighted sub-drop.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.

10. Outro Audio Resolve# CES style instruction — S10 Outro Audio Resolve

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **S10 Outro Audio Resolve** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Sound** — Instead of the song being chopped off or faded at an arbitrary point, the music is back-timed so its natural ending (or a clean bar-line edit into the ending) coincides with the outro, and the final note decays smoothly into silence over the end card. It makes the video feel finished and deliberate, and the quiet tail gives the call to action room to breathe.

### S10 Outro Audio Resolve

- **When:** ≈ 00:12.00 → 00:15.00 (≈ derived — adjust to the nearest natural beat in the footage)
- **Layer / track:** A2+ (under the original voice on A1)
- **What it does:** The music is edited to land on its final chord as the last frame arrives, then rings out in a controlled tail to silence.
- **Parameters:** Music track: (not set); Ending: Back-time to the song's real ending; Tail length: 3 s; Fade shape: Exponential (natural decay); Gap after last word: 0.5 s; Silence at end: 12 frames
- **Steps:**
  1. ASSET REQUEST: no music track was supplied. Ask the user for a licensed or self-made file (asset kind "music") and list it under missing assets. Never download copyrighted audio. Until it arrives: end on the natural room tone of the last shot with a 1s fade — never substitute a downloaded track.
  2. Back-time the track: align the song's own final chord so it hits 0.5s after the last spoken word, then edit earlier in the song (on a bar line, matching beats) so the music reaches that ending naturally.
  3. Shape the tail over 3.0s (90 frames) with an exponential fade (FFmpeg afade=t=out:curve=exp) so it reaches silence by 00:15.00 minus 12 frames.
  4. End with 12 frames (400ms) of digital silence before the last video frame so no platform clips the tail; the picture end card stays up through the tail.
  5. Keep the music under any CTA voice-over by at least 8 dB. Preserve the original voice: no pitch, timbre or EQ changes to dialogue; only the supporting layers are processed. Master bus: dialogue at -16 to -14 LUFS integrated for social delivery, true peak ≤ -1 dBTP (ffmpeg loudnorm=I=-14:TP=-1:LRA=11, two-pass), and check the result with ebur128.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING), Music track (⚠ MISSING) · optional: Sound effects
- **Fallback:** No music supplied: request a licensed or self-owned track, or close on natural room tone with a gentle 1s fade. Never pull music from the internet.

Colour & Finish
Clean Creator Grade# CES style instruction — F01 Clean Creator Grade

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F01 Clean Creator Grade** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — A transparent, honest grade: neutral whites, skin sitting at 55–70 IRE on the waveform and on the vectorscope skin line, blacks just above zero and highlights rolled off before they clip. Contrast and saturation are nudged only enough to remove the flat 'straight out of camera' look. Viewers never notice the grade — they simply see a clean, well-shot video, which is exactly why it suits talking-head creator content.

### F01 Clean Creator Grade

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Neutralises white balance, places skin at a healthy exposure, adds a gentle S-curve and a touch of saturation so every shot looks true-to-life and consistent.
- **Parameters:** Grade strength (mix): 100%; White balance shift (K, + warmer): 0; Exposure trim: 0; Contrast: 8%; Saturation: 105%; Highlight roll-off: Soft (peak ≈ 94 IRE); Protect skin tones: on; Look LUT (.cube, optional): (not set)
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Clean Creator grade as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation. Pipeline order: source footage → primary balance → this grade → captions, titles, logos and UI graphics composited on top. Do not run brand graphics through the grade; #D7FF5F and the caption whites must render exactly as specified. Screen recordings and supplied screenshots get at most 30% of the grade strength so text and interface colours stay faithful.
  2. Primary balance per shot first: sample a neutral reference (white wall, shirt, paper) and trim colorbalance midtones until R≈G≈B on it; set exposure so the brightest skin sits at 65–72 IRE and the face average at 55–65 IRE; black point at 1–2 IRE (lift −1%, never crush shadow detail); gamma neutral; gain only until highlights reach the roll-off. Keep the result neutral (no Kelvin shift).
  3. Look (strength 100%): contrast +8% as a gentle S-curve pivoting at 50%, saturation 105% applied to greens, cyans, blues and magentas only (reds/yellows untouched so skin stays put), highlight roll-off: soft shoulder starting at 80% input, peak whites ≈ 94 IRE. FFmpeg chain: -vf "eq=contrast=1.08:saturation=1:gamma=1,curves=master='0/0.012 0.5/0.5 0.8/0.78 1/0.94',huesaturation=saturation=0.05:colors=g+c+b+m".
  4. Protect skin: sample the median skin colour from a well-exposed cheek/forehead area (hex, e.g. 0xC08A6E — use the real sample) and build a hue-qualified matte around the ~20–30° skin hue range: [pre]colorkey=color=<skinHex>:similarity=0.16:blend=0.10,alphaextract,negate,gblur=sigma=4[skinMatte]; [balanced][skinMatte]alphamerge,colorchannelmixer=aa=0.7[skin]; [graded][skin]overlay=format=auto. This keeps faces on the vectorscope skin line (≈123°) while the look shifts everything else. Check the matte is soft-edged and does not catch wood, sand or orange props; tighten similarity if it does.
  5. No LUT supplied — build the look from the numeric controls below (they are the complete recipe). Never download or embed third-party LUTs; if the user later supplies a .cube file, apply it with lut3d=file=…:interp=tetrahedral before these trims.
  6. Remotion approximation (motion-graphics plates, previews, or when FFmpeg is not in the pipeline): wrap each footage <OffthreadVideo> in an <AbsoluteFill style={{ filter: "brightness(1) contrast(1.08) saturate(1.05)" }}>. CSS filters run in 8-bit display space, so for the final master prefer rendering the FFmpeg-graded footage and using that file in Remotion.
  7. Verify before export: grab one frame per shot (ffmpeg -ss <t> -i graded.mp4 -frames:v 1 shotN.png) and inspect ffmpeg -i shotN.png -vf "split[a][b];[a]waveform=mode=column:graticule=green:flags=numbers[w];[b]vectorscope=mode=color3:graticule=green[v];[w][v]hstack" scope.png. Whites must peak ≤ 94 IRE, blacks ≥ 1 IRE, the waveform should show no flat-topped clipping, and the vectorscope skin trace must sit on the skin line. Shots must match each other within ±2 IRE in skin exposure and ±3° in skin hue; fix outliers with a per-shot exposure/white-balance trim before the look, never by changing the look itself. Export at 1080×1920, 30fps, 10-bit or high-bitrate 8-bit with -pix_fmt yuv420p to avoid banding in gradients and skies.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: LUT file
- **Fallback:** If shots were filmed under mixed lighting that cannot be neutralised with one balance, correct each shot's white balance individually first, then apply this grade uniformly. If the footage is already well balanced Rec.709, apply only the contrast/saturation trims at 50% strength.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
Warm Editorial
# CES style instruction — F02 Warm Editorial

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F02 Warm Editorial** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — Warm Editorial shifts the white balance toward golden-hour light, pushes highlights toward cream rather than pure white and gives shadows a gentle chocolate or sepia undertone. Contrast stays soft and saturation slightly restrained so the warmth reads as elegant, not orange. Skin is held on the skin line so faces look healthy and polished, like a lifestyle magazine spread.

### F02 Warm Editorial

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Warms the balance by a few hundred Kelvin, tints highlights cream, softens contrast slightly and keeps skin polished — a magazine-like, inviting finish.
- **Parameters:** Grade strength (mix): 90%; Warmth (K): 600; Cream highlights: 45%; Contrast: 5%; Saturation: 96%; Shadow undertone: Chocolate (warm brown); Protect skin tones: on; Look LUT (.cube, optional): (not set)
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Warm Editorial grade as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation.
  2. Balance each shot neutral first (whites R≈G≈B, skin highlights 65–72 IRE), then warm the whole image by ≈600 K (FFmpeg colortemperature=temperature=5900:mix=0.9:pl=1). Lift 0%, gamma +2% (slightly open midtones), gain −2% so the warmth does not push highlights into clipping.
  3. Cream highlights at 41%: colorbalance highlights rh=0.02:gh=0.01:bh=-0.03:pl=1 so whites land near #FFF4E2 (never pure blue-white); shadows get a chocolate undertone with colorbalance rs=0.05:gs=0.01:bs=-0.04:pl=1. Contrast +4% with a soft shoulder curves=master='0/0.02 0.5/0.51 0.85/0.83 1/0.95' (peak whites ≈ 95 IRE), saturation 96%. FFmpeg chain: -vf "colortemperature=temperature=5900:mix=0.9:pl=1,colorbalance=rs=0.05:gs=0.01:bs=-0.04:rh=0.02:gh=0.01:bh=-0.03:pl=1,eq=contrast=1.045:saturation=0.964:gamma=1.02,curves=master='0/0.02 0.5/0.51 0.85/0.83 1/0.95'".
  4. Protect skin: sample the median skin colour from a well-exposed cheek/forehead area (hex, e.g. 0xC08A6E — use the real sample) and build a hue-qualified matte around the ~20–30° skin hue range: [pre]colorkey=color=<skinHex>:similarity=0.16:blend=0.10,alphaextract,negate,gblur=sigma=4[skinMatte]; [balanced][skinMatte]alphamerge,colorchannelmixer=aa=0.6[skin]; [graded][skin]overlay=format=auto. This keeps faces on the vectorscope skin line (≈123°) while the look shifts everything else. Check the matte is soft-edged and does not catch wood, sand or orange props; tighten similarity if it does.
  5. No LUT supplied — build the look from the numeric controls below (they are the complete recipe). Never download or embed third-party LUTs; if the user later supplies a .cube file, apply it with lut3d=file=…:interp=tetrahedral before these trims.
  6. Remotion approximation (motion-graphics plates, previews, or when FFmpeg is not in the pipeline): wrap each footage <OffthreadVideo> in an <AbsoluteFill style={{ filter: "sepia(0.12) contrast(1.045) saturate(0.964) brightness(1.03)" }}>, then layer <AbsoluteFill style={{ background: "linear-gradient(180deg, #FFE2B0, transparent 65%)", mixBlendMode: "soft-light", opacity: 0.41 }} /> for cream highlights. CSS filters run in 8-bit display space, so for the final master prefer rendering the FFmpeg-graded footage and using that file in Remotion.
  7. Verify before export: grab one frame per shot (ffmpeg -ss <t> -i graded.mp4 -frames:v 1 shotN.png) and inspect ffmpeg -i shotN.png -vf "split[a][b];[a]waveform=mode=column:graticule=green:flags=numbers[w];[b]vectorscope=mode=color3:graticule=green[v];[w][v]hstack" scope.png. On the RGB parade the highlights should show red slightly above green above blue (warm, not orange); skin stays on the skin line and must not drift toward red/orange by more than 5°. Shots must match each other within ±2 IRE in skin exposure and ±3° in skin hue; fix outliers with a per-shot exposure/white-balance trim before the look, never by changing the look itself. Export at 1080×1920, 30fps, 10-bit or high-bitrate 8-bit with -pix_fmt yuv420p to avoid banding in gradients and skies.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: LUT file
- **Fallback:** If the footage already has strong warm tungsten light, reduce warmth to 0–200 K and rely on the cream highlights alone. Mixed daylight/tungsten shots should be neutralised individually before the look.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
3. Cool Technology# CES style instruction — F03 Cool Technology

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F03 Cool Technology** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — Cool Technology moves the white balance a few hundred Kelvin toward daylight-blue, refines blues and cyans, and adds crisp contrast so edges and interfaces feel precise. Electronic accents — monitors, LED strips, neon — are kept saturated but below clipping, and can be reinforced with a subtle rim glow. Skin is qualified out of the cool shift so faces stay human instead of turning grey-blue.

### F03 Cool Technology

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Shifts whites neutral-to-cool, deepens blues, tightens contrast and keeps screen glows and LED accents controlled — a precise, modern tech finish with natural skin.
- **Parameters:** Grade strength (mix): 90%; Cool shift (K): -500; Tint (− green / + magenta): -2; Contrast: 14%; Saturation: 92%; Electronic accent colour: #38D6FF; Accent rim glow: on; Protect skin tones: on
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Cool Technology grade as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation. Pipeline order: source footage → primary balance → this grade → captions, titles, logos and UI graphics composited on top. Do not run brand graphics through the grade; #D7FF5F and the caption whites must render exactly as specified. Screen recordings and supplied screenshots get at most 30% of the grade strength so text and interface colours stay faithful.
  2. Balance each shot neutral, then cool the image by ≈500 K (colortemperature=temperature=7000:mix=0.9:pl=1) and apply tint -2 (colorbalance midtones gm=0.02). Lift −2% with a slight blue offset in shadows (colorbalance bs=0.05:rs=-0.03), gamma 0, gain held so whites read neutral-cool (#F2F6FF) at ≤ 96 IRE.
  3. Contrast +13% (curves=master='0/0.01 0.25/0.21 0.5/0.5 0.75/0.79 1/0.96'), overall saturation 93%, then boost only blues/cyans: huesaturation=saturation=0.11:colors=c+b. FFmpeg chain: -vf "colortemperature=temperature=7000:mix=0.9:pl=1,colorbalance=rs=-0.03:bs=0.05:gm=0.02:pl=1,eq=contrast=1.126:saturation=0.928,curves=master='0/0.01 0.25/0.21 0.5/0.5 0.75/0.79 1/0.96',huesaturation=saturation=0.11:colors=c+b".
  4. Electronic accents: keep screens, LEDs and neon in the #38D6FF family saturated but below 98% on every channel. Reinforce existing accent light with an edge glow only where it already exists in the shot — a soft #38D6FF rim (blurred subject-edge mask or a screen-blended radial at 15–25% opacity) on the side facing the light source. Never add glow to shots without a visible source.
  5. Protect skin: sample the median skin colour from a well-exposed cheek/forehead area (hex, e.g. 0xC08A6E — use the real sample) and build a hue-qualified matte around the ~20–30° skin hue range: [pre]colorkey=color=<skinHex>:similarity=0.16:blend=0.10,alphaextract,negate,gblur=sigma=4[skinMatte]; [balanced][skinMatte]alphamerge,colorchannelmixer=aa=0.85[skin]; [graded][skin]overlay=format=auto. This keeps faces on the vectorscope skin line (≈123°) while the look shifts everything else. Check the matte is soft-edged and does not catch wood, sand or orange props; tighten similarity if it does.
  6. Remotion approximation (motion-graphics plates, previews, or when FFmpeg is not in the pipeline): wrap each footage <OffthreadVideo> in an <AbsoluteFill style={{ filter: "contrast(1.126) saturate(0.928) hue-rotate(-1deg)" }}>, then add <AbsoluteFill style={{ background: "#3D6DFF", mixBlendMode: "soft-light", opacity: 0.18 }} /> (with a CSS mask-image radial hole over faces when skin protection is on). CSS filters run in 8-bit display space, so for the final master prefer rendering the FFmpeg-graded footage and using that file in Remotion.
  7. Verify before export: grab one frame per shot (ffmpeg -ss <t> -i graded.mp4 -frames:v 1 shotN.png) and inspect ffmpeg -i shotN.png -vf "split[a][b];[a]waveform=mode=column:graticule=green:flags=numbers[w];[b]vectorscope=mode=color3:graticule=green[v];[w][v]hstack" scope.png. RGB parade: highlights with blue ≥ green ≥ red by 2–4%; no channel flat-topping on screens or LEDs; skin trace still on the skin line. Shots must match each other within ±2 IRE in skin exposure and ±3° in skin hue; fix outliers with a per-shot exposure/white-balance trim before the look, never by changing the look itself. Export at 1080×1920, 30fps, 10-bit or high-bitrate 8-bit with -pix_fmt yuv420p to avoid banding in gradients and skies.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: LUT file, Screen recording
- **Fallback:** If the footage was shot under warm tungsten light and the cool shift turns skin grey even with protection, halve the Kelvin shift and push the coolness into the shadows only (colorbalance shadows) instead of the whole image.

- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.

4. Cinematic Teal Shadows# CES style instruction — F04 Cinematic Teal Shadows

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F04 Cinematic Teal Shadows** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — The classic cinematic split: a selective teal tint lives in the shadows below a luma pivot while highlights and faces lean slightly warm. Because the shift is restricted by luminance and skin is protected with a hue qualifier, faces stay natural and pop forward against cooler surroundings. The result reads as 'film' without the over-processed orange-and-teal cliché.

### F04 Cinematic Teal Shadows

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Pushes only the shadows and darker midtones toward teal, warms highlights slightly and qualifies skin out of the shift, giving a filmic teal-and-warm separation.
- **Parameters:** Grade strength (mix): 85%; Shadow tint: #0F7C82; Shadow tint amount: 45%; Shadow range (luma pivot): 35%; Highlight warmth: 25%; Contrast: 12%; Protect skin tones: on; Look LUT (.cube, optional): (not set)
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Cinematic Teal Shadows grade as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation.
  2. Primary balance first (neutral whites, skin 55–70 IRE, black point 1–2 IRE). Then contrast +10% via curves=master='0/0.015 0.24/0.22 0.5/0.5 0.8/0.82 1/0.96' — a slightly deeper toe with a soft highlight shoulder. Highlights: warm by 21% — colorbalance rh=0.02:gh=0:bh=-0.02:pl=1 — so faces and practical lights separate from the teal shadows; keep overall saturation at 100–105% and do not boost orange.
  3. Shadow tint #0F7C82 at 38%: restrict it to luma below 35% (full tint at 0–21%, fading to zero by 35%). FFmpeg: colorbalance=rs=-0.08:gs=0:bs=0:pl=1 (shadow band only); in Remotion/compositing, use a luma-keyed copy (lumakey or a curves-derived matte below 35% luma) tinted #0F7C82 in soft-light/colour blend at 38% opacity.
  4. Protect skin: sample the median skin colour from a well-exposed cheek/forehead area (hex, e.g. 0xC08A6E — use the real sample) and build a hue-qualified matte around the ~20–30° skin hue range: [pre]colorkey=color=<skinHex>:similarity=0.16:blend=0.10,alphaextract,negate,gblur=sigma=4[skinMatte]; [balanced][skinMatte]alphamerge,colorchannelmixer=aa=0.9[skin]; [graded][skin]overlay=format=auto. This keeps faces on the vectorscope skin line (≈123°) while the look shifts everything else. Check the matte is soft-edged and does not catch wood, sand or orange props; tighten similarity if it does.
  5. No LUT supplied — build the look from the numeric controls below (they are the complete recipe). Never download or embed third-party LUTs; if the user later supplies a .cube file, apply it with lut3d=file=…:interp=tetrahedral before these trims.
  6. Remotion approximation (motion-graphics plates, previews, or when FFmpeg is not in the pipeline): wrap each footage <OffthreadVideo> in an <AbsoluteFill style={{ filter: "contrast(1.102) saturate(1.04)" }}>, then add an <AbsoluteFill style={{ background: "linear-gradient(180deg, transparent 44%, #0F7C82)", mixBlendMode: "soft-light", opacity: 0.38, maskImage: radial-gradient over the face (transparent at the face, opaque elsewhere) }} />. CSS filters run in 8-bit display space, so for the final master prefer rendering the FFmpeg-graded footage and using that file in Remotion.
  7. Verify before export: grab one frame per shot (ffmpeg -ss <t> -i graded.mp4 -frames:v 1 shotN.png) and inspect ffmpeg -i shotN.png -vf "split[a][b];[a]waveform=mode=column:graticule=green:flags=numbers[w];[b]vectorscope=mode=color3:graticule=green[v];[w][v]hstack" scope.png. Vectorscope: shadows cluster toward cyan/teal, highlights toward orange, skin trace exactly on the skin line. Shadows must not go fully cyan — keep shadow chroma under ~30% of the scope radius. Shots must match each other within ±2 IRE in skin exposure and ±3° in skin hue; fix outliers with a per-shot exposure/white-balance trim before the look, never by changing the look itself. Export at 1080×1920, 30fps, 10-bit or high-bitrate 8-bit with -pix_fmt yuv420p to avoid banding in gradients and skies.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: LUT file
- **Fallback:** If faces are lit dimly and fall under the luma pivot, raise the pivot or relight via an exposure power window on the face before the tint; if skin protection cannot be keyed cleanly (orange walls, wood), lower shadow amount to 25% and skip the highlight warmth.
