5. Modern Monochrome# CES style instruction — F05 Modern Monochrome

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F05 Modern Monochrome** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — Instead of simply desaturating, Modern Monochrome uses a channel mix — like a red, yellow or green filter on black-and-white film — to decide which colours become light or dark, so skin, sky and clothing separate into distinct grey values. A restrained S-curve keeps true blacks and bright whites while protecting mid-grey detail, and an optional subtle tone (warm or cool) gives the black and white a modern, intentional finish.

### F05 Modern Monochrome

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Converts footage to black and white through a chosen channel-mix filter, then sets a firm but readable contrast curve with clean blacks and separated mid-greys.
- **Parameters:** B&W filter (channel mix): Red filter (bright skin, dark skies); Contrast: 25%; Black point lift: 2%; Tone: Neutral; Vignette: 20%; Look LUT (.cube, optional): (not set)
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Modern Monochrome grade as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation.
  2. Balance exposure per shot first (face 55–68 IRE) — monochrome exposes mismatches more than colour does. Convert with a red channel mix: R 0.6 / G 0.35 / B 0.05 (sums to 1.0, so exposure is preserved): colorchannelmixer=rr=0.6:rg=0.35:rb=0.05:gr=0.6:gg=0.35:gb=0.05:br=0.6:bg=0.35:bb=0.05.
  3. Contrast +25% as an S-curve that keeps mid-grey separation: curves=master='0/0.02 0.2/0.14 0.5/0.5 0.8/0.86 1/0.97'. Black point at 2.0% (never below 0), whites ≤ 97 IRE; check that hair against background and shirt against wall land at least 10 IRE apart.
  4. Tone: keep perfectly neutral — verify the vectorscope is a dot in the centre. Vignette 20%: vignette=angle=0.5:mode=forward, centred on the subject. FFmpeg chain: -vf "colorchannelmixer=rr=0.6:rg=0.35:rb=0.05:gr=0.6:gg=0.35:gb=0.05:br=0.6:bg=0.35:bb=0.05,curves=master='0/0.02 0.2/0.14 0.5/0.5 0.8/0.86 1/0.97',vignette=angle=0.5". No LUT required for this look.
  5. Remotion approximation (motion-graphics plates, previews, or when FFmpeg is not in the pipeline): wrap each footage <OffthreadVideo> in an <AbsoluteFill style={{ filter: "saturate(1.4) hue-rotate(120deg) grayscale(1) contrast(1.25)" }}>, then note that hue-rotate before grayscale only approximates the channel mix — use the FFmpeg colorchannelmixer for the master. CSS filters run in 8-bit display space, so for the final master prefer rendering the FFmpeg-graded footage and using that file in Remotion.
  6. Keep captions, logos and brand graphics in colour on top only if the user wants a single accent; otherwise render them in white/black so the frame stays monochrome. Pipeline order: source footage → primary balance → this grade → captions, titles, logos and UI graphics composited on top. Do not run brand graphics through the grade; #D7FF5F and the caption whites must render exactly as specified. Screen recordings and supplied screenshots get at most 30% of the grade strength so text and interface colours stay faithful.
  7. Verify before export: grab one frame per shot (ffmpeg -ss <t> -i graded.mp4 -frames:v 1 shotN.png) and inspect ffmpeg -i shotN.png -vf "split[a][b];[a]waveform=mode=column:graticule=green:flags=numbers[w];[b]vectorscope=mode=color3:graticule=green[v];[w][v]hstack" scope.png. The vectorscope must be a centred dot (or a tiny offset for toned looks); the waveform should span 2.0–97 IRE with visible separation bands in the midtones. Shots must match each other within ±2 IRE in skin exposure and ±3° in skin hue; fix outliers with a per-shot exposure/white-balance trim before the look, never by changing the look itself. Export at 1080×1920, 30fps, 10-bit or high-bitrate 8-bit with -pix_fmt yuv420p to avoid banding in gradients and skies.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: LUT file
- **Fallback:** If skin and background sit at the same grey after conversion, switch the channel-mix filter (red ↔ blue) before adding contrast; for very flat footage raise contrast in two stages rather than crushing blacks.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.

6. Bold Pop Colour# CES style instruction — F06 Bold Pop Colour

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F06 Bold Pop Colour** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — Bold Pop Colour is a deliberate, high-energy look: backgrounds, wardrobe and props become vivid, one accent hue is pushed further so the frame has a signature colour, and contrast is punchy. Vibrance raises muted colours more than already-saturated ones, a soft-clip guard keeps any channel from flat-topping, and the skin qualifier stops faces from turning orange — so the energy comes from the world around the subject, not from a sunburnt presenter.

### F06 Bold Pop Colour

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Drives saturation and vibrance hard on props and backgrounds, pushes a chosen accent hue, adds punchy contrast — while guarding highlights and skin from clipping or going orange.
- **Parameters:** Grade strength (mix): 90%; Saturation: 145%; Vibrance: 35%; Contrast: 15%; Pop accent hue: #FFC21A; Highlight clip guard: on; Protect skin tones: on; Look LUT (.cube, optional): (not set)
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Bold Pop Colour grade as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation.
  2. Primary balance first (neutral whites, skin 60–70 IRE). Then contrast +14% (curves=master='0/0 0.25/0.2 0.5/0.5 0.75/0.8 1/0.98'), saturation 141% on non-skin hues (huesaturation=saturation=0.41:colors=g+c+b+m), and vibrance 32% (vibrance=intensity=0.32) so muted colours catch up with vivid ones.
  3. Accent push: qualify the hue family of #FFC21A (±15° around its hue, saturation > 25%) and raise its saturation a further 15% and luminance +3% — FFmpeg: huesaturation with colors set to the matching primary/secondary (e.g. y for yellow, m for pink) at saturation=0.15:lightness=0.03. Push only backgrounds and props; never the subject's face. Clip guard: after all boosts, soft-clip every channel at 98% (curves=all='0/0 0.9/0.9 1/0.98') and check the RGB parade — no channel may flat-top on saturated reds, neon or skies. Pull saturation back on that hue rather than accepting clipping.
  4. Protect skin: sample the median skin colour from a well-exposed cheek/forehead area (hex, e.g. 0xC08A6E — use the real sample) and build a hue-qualified matte around the ~20–30° skin hue range: [pre]colorkey=color=<skinHex>:similarity=0.16:blend=0.10,alphaextract,negate,gblur=sigma=4[skinMatte]; [balanced][skinMatte]alphamerge,colorchannelmixer=aa=0.9[skin]; [graded][skin]overlay=format=auto. This keeps faces on the vectorscope skin line (≈123°) while the look shifts everything else. Check the matte is soft-edged and does not catch wood, sand or orange props; tighten similarity if it does.
  5. No LUT supplied — build the look from the numeric controls below (they are the complete recipe). Never download or embed third-party LUTs; if the user later supplies a .cube file, apply it with lut3d=file=…:interp=tetrahedral before these trims.
  6. Remotion approximation (motion-graphics plates, previews, or when FFmpeg is not in the pipeline): wrap each footage <OffthreadVideo> in an <AbsoluteFill style={{ filter: "contrast(1.135) saturate(1.405) brightness(1.03)" }}>, then push the accent with a hue-qualified mask or a <AbsoluteFill style={{ background: "#FFC21A", mixBlendMode: "color", opacity: 0.25 }}/> restricted to the background matte (needs a subject mask, otherwise skip). CSS filters run in 8-bit display space, so for the final master prefer rendering the FFmpeg-graded footage and using that file in Remotion.
  7. Verify before export: grab one frame per shot (ffmpeg -ss <t> -i graded.mp4 -frames:v 1 shotN.png) and inspect ffmpeg -i shotN.png -vf "split[a][b];[a]waveform=mode=column:graticule=green:flags=numbers[w];[b]vectorscope=mode=color3:graticule=green[v];[w][v]hstack" scope.png. Vectorscope traces may reach ~75% of the radius on props but must stay inside the 100% target boxes; skin stays on the skin line with no orange push. Shots must match each other within ±2 IRE in skin exposure and ±3° in skin hue; fix outliers with a per-shot exposure/white-balance trim before the look, never by changing the look itself. Export at 1080×1920, 30fps, 10-bit or high-bitrate 8-bit with -pix_fmt yuv420p to avoid banding in gradients and skies.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: LUT file
- **Fallback:** If the set is mostly beige or grey there is little colour to pop — apply at 50% strength and let the accent colour come from graphics instead. If skin cannot be keyed cleanly (warm walls), lower saturation to 125% rather than risk orange faces.

7. Soft Pastel Film# CES style instruction — F07 Soft Pastel Film

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F07 Soft Pastel Film** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — Soft Pastel Film gently fades the image: blacks lift to a tinted floor instead of pure black, contrast and saturation drop so colours become powdery pastels, and a split tone puts a cool lavender in the shadows and a peachy warmth in the highlights. It feels calm and contemporary, like expired film scanned bright — but skin and detail are preserved, so it still looks intentional rather than washed out.

### F07 Soft Pastel Film

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Lifts the blacks into a soft tinted floor, lowers contrast and saturation and tints shadows and highlights with pastel hues — a quiet, airy, contemporary finish.
- **Parameters:** Grade strength (mix): 90%; Black lift (fade): 12%; Saturation: 72%; Contrast: -15%; Shadow tint: #B9A8E0; Highlight tint: #FFD3C2; Look LUT (.cube, optional): (not set)
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Soft Pastel Film grade as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation.
  2. Primary balance first (skin 60–72 IRE — pastel looks should be exposed half a stop brighter than usual). Then fade: lift black point to 11% (curves=master='0/0.108 0.5/0.52 1/0.938') so the darkest pixel sits near 11 IRE and whites peak ≈ 94 IRE.
  3. Contrast -13% and saturation 75%: eq=contrast=0.865:saturation=0.748. Split tone: shadows toward #B9A8E0 (colorbalance rs=-0.01:gs=-0.02:bs=0.03), highlights toward #FFD3C2 (rh=0.06:gh=0.02:bh=0), pl=1 to preserve lightness. FFmpeg chain: -vf "curves=master='0/0.108 0.5/0.52 1/0.938',eq=contrast=0.865:saturation=0.748,colorbalance=rs=-0.01:gs=-0.02:bs=0.03:rh=0.06:gh=0.02:bh=0:pl=1".
  4. Skin: after desaturation faces can look pale — hue-qualify the ~20–30° skin range (colorkey matte on a sampled skin hex, or huesaturation=saturation=0.15:colors=r+y as a quick approximation) to restore skin saturation to ≈ 90% of the original, and keep the skin trace on the vectorscope skin line (≈123°).
  5. No LUT supplied — build the look from the numeric controls below (they are the complete recipe). Never download or embed third-party LUTs; if the user later supplies a .cube file, apply it with lut3d=file=…:interp=tetrahedral before these trims.
  6. Remotion approximation (motion-graphics plates, previews, or when FFmpeg is not in the pipeline): wrap each footage <OffthreadVideo> in an <AbsoluteFill style={{ filter: "brightness(1.06) contrast(0.865) saturate(0.748)" }}>, then add <AbsoluteFill style={{ background: "#B9A8E0", mixBlendMode: "screen", opacity: 0.24 }} /> for the lifted tinted blacks and a "#FFD3C2" soft-light gradient from the top for the highlight tint. CSS filters run in 8-bit display space, so for the final master prefer rendering the FFmpeg-graded footage and using that file in Remotion.
  7. Verify before export: grab one frame per shot (ffmpeg -ss <t> -i graded.mp4 -frames:v 1 shotN.png) and inspect ffmpeg -i shotN.png -vf "split[a][b];[a]waveform=mode=column:graticule=green:flags=numbers[w];[b]vectorscope=mode=color3:graticule=green[v];[w][v]hstack" scope.png. Waveform floor must sit at ≈11 IRE (no true black), with no banding in the lifted shadows (use 10-bit or add 1–2% grain if banding appears). Shots must match each other within ±2 IRE in skin exposure and ±3° in skin hue; fix outliers with a per-shot exposure/white-balance trim before the look, never by changing the look itself. Export at 1080×1920, 30fps, 10-bit or high-bitrate 8-bit with -pix_fmt yuv420p to avoid banding in gradients and skies.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: LUT file
- **Fallback:** If the footage is heavily compressed, lifted blacks will reveal macro-blocking — reduce the black lift to 5–6% and add fine grain (F08) to mask it.

8. Film Grain & Bloom# CES style instruction — F08 Film Grain & Bloom

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F08 Film Grain & Bloom** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — A texture pass that sits on top of any base grade. Organic, per-frame grain (luma-weighted so it is strongest in the midtones) breaks up digital smoothness and compression banding, and a thresholded bloom lets windows, practical lights and speculars glow softly into their surroundings like halation on film. Both are restrained enough that skin texture, text and edges remain crisp — the image just feels more photographic.

### F08 Film Grain & Bloom

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Layers fine moving film grain over the graded image and lets only the brightest highlights bloom into a soft glow, while faces and detail stay sharp.
- **Parameters:** Grain amount: 30%; Grain size: Medium (16mm); Bloom threshold: 85%; Bloom radius: 24 px; Bloom strength: 35%; Red halation: off
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Film Grain & Bloom texture as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation.
  2. Place this pass AFTER the base grade and BEFORE captions/graphics. Bloom: split the graded image, keep only luma above 85% (curves=all='0/0 0.85/0 1/1'), blur it with gblur=sigma=12 (≈ 24px radius at 1080px width), then screen it back at 35%: [g]split[a][b];[b]curves=all='0/0 0.85/0 1/1',gblur=sigma=12[bl];[a][bl]blend=all_mode=screen:all_opacity=0.35.
  3. Grain: medium (16mm-like, ~1.5px structure) at 30% — generate it fresh every frame (temporal, never a frozen overlay): noise=c0s=11:c0f=t+u (luma only; leave chroma clean) and soften the grain structure for the larger size by applying it to a separate noise plate: color=gray:s=1080x1920,noise=c0s=11:c0f=t+u,gblur=sigma=0.6 blended with blend=all_mode=overlay:all_opacity=0.4. Grain must be strongest in midtones and nearly invisible in pure blacks and clipped whites (overlay blending does this).
  4. Full FFmpeg graph (after the base grade): -filter_complex "[0:v]split[a][b];[b]curves=all='0/0 0.85/0 1/1',gblur=sigma=12[bl];[a][bl]blend=all_mode=screen:all_opacity=0.35,noise=c0s=11:c0f=t+u[out]" -map "[out]".
  5. Remotion approximation: render a duplicate of the footage layer with style={{ filter: "brightness(0.59) contrast(8) blur(12px)", mixBlendMode: "screen", opacity: 0.35 }} (threshold → blur order matters: wrap the thresholded element in a parent that applies the blur), plus an SVG <feTurbulence type="fractalNoise" baseFrequency="0.8" seed={frame}> layer in mixBlendMode "overlay" at 0.18 opacity — change the seed every frame (or every 2 frames for a 12fps grain cadence).
  6. Restraint: blooms must not swallow faces — skin must stay below the threshold (if a bright forehead blooms, raise the threshold by 3–5%). Text, UI recordings and screenshots composited later must stay grain-free and sharp.
  7. Verify on sampled frames at 100% zoom: highlight edges glow by ≈ 24px without haloing dark outlines; grain is visible in midtone areas but does not cause flicker in flat walls; encode at a bitrate high enough (≥ 12 Mbps for 1080×1920) that the encoder does not smear the grain into blocks.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING)
- **Fallback:** If the final delivery platform recompresses heavily, reduce grain to 15–20% and use the fine size, otherwise it turns into blocky noise. Without visible highlights (flat overcast footage), skip the bloom and keep only grain.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.

9. Analog Tape Texture# CES style instruction — F09 Analog Tape Texture

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F09 Analog Tape Texture** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — Analog Tape Texture simulates the character of consumer videotape without destroying the image: chroma channels sit a few pixels out of register, faint scanlines and tape noise add texture, colours fade slightly toward a warm cast, and a soft tracking band occasionally rolls through the frame with a tiny horizontal timebase wobble. Each artifact is dialled low so faces and captions stay readable — it reads as nostalgic, not broken.

### F09 Analog Tape Texture

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Adds restrained VHS-style artifacts: a few pixels of red/blue channel offset, faint scanlines, tape noise, slight colour fade and an occasional rolling tracking band.
- **Parameters:** Chroma offset: 4 px; Scanline opacity: 18%; Tape noise: 20%; Tape colour (saturation): 85%; Rolling tracking band: on; Timebase wobble: 2 px; VCR on-screen display: off
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Analog Tape Texture texture as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation.
  2. Tape colour first (after the base grade): saturation 85%, a slight warm fade and softened chroma — eq=saturation=0.85:contrast=0.94:brightness=0.01,colorbalance=rs=0.02:bs=-0.03,curves=master='0/0.04 1/0.95', then blur chroma only horizontally to mimic tape bandwidth: format=yuv444p,split[y][c];[c]boxblur=luma_radius=0:chroma_radius=2:chroma_power=1[cb];[y][cb]mergeplanes=0x001112:yuv444p (or simply chromashift + a mild 'gblur=sigma=0.6:planes=6').
  3. Chroma misregistration: rgbashift=rh=4:bh=-4:edge=smear (red 4px right, blue 4px left at 1080px width). Keep it ≤ 4px — larger offsets make faces look broken. Timebase wobble: while the tracking band passes, shift the full frame horizontally by up to 2px for 3–5 frames (Remotion: translateX with a decaying random offset; FFmpeg: a short rgbashift=rh=6:gh=2:bh=-2 segment with enable='between(t,…)').
  4. Scanlines at 18%: every 4px row darkened — overlay a generated plate: color=black:s=1080x1920,format=rgba,geq=r=0:g=0:b=0:a='if(lt(mod(Y,4),1),46,0)'[sl];[v][sl]overlay. Tape noise at 20%: noise=c0s=8:c0f=t+u plus faint horizontal streaks (noise on a 1px-tall plate scaled to full width).
  5. Tracking band: a soft horizontal band (~5% of frame height, white at 8–12% with noise inside) that rolls from top to bottom once every 3–5 seconds: color=c=white@0.10:s=1080x96,format=rgba,noise=alls=40:allf=t[band];[v][band]overlay=x=0:y='mod(t*480\,H+h)-h'. Never park the band over a face for more than 6 frames.
  6. No on-screen VCR text — the texture alone signals the analog look.
  7. Remotion approximation: render three copies of the footage, each multiplied by pure #FF0000, #00FF00 and #0000FF inside an isolated group, combine with mixBlendMode "screen" and offset the red/blue copies by ±4px; add a repeating-linear-gradient scanline layer at 0.18 opacity and a translateY-animated band. Pipeline order: source footage → primary balance → this texture → captions, titles, logos and UI graphics composited on top. Do not run brand graphics through the texture; #D7FF5F and the caption whites must render exactly as specified. Screen recordings and supplied screenshots get at most 30% of the texture strength so text and interface colours stay faithful. Captions may carry a 1px chroma offset for consistency, but must stay sharp. Verify by sampling frames: faces readable, no channel offset larger than 4px, band timing never collides with key words on screen.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING)
- **Fallback:** If the footage is already soft or low-resolution, halve the chroma offset and scanline opacity so the image does not degrade further. For platforms that recompress hard, prefer offsets + colour fade and skip the noise.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
10. Premium Commercial Finish# CES style instruction — F10 Premium Commercial Finish

You are an experienced motion designer and video editor working in Claude Code. Apply the **F10 Premium Commercial Finish** technique to the supplied footage as specified below.

**B. Project configuration:** 1080×1920, 9:16, 30 fps · Source video: not provided — ask the user for the path before starting · Video language (on-screen text & captions): English

**Colour & Finish** — The look of a high-end product commercial: whites are truly neutral and bright without clipping, blacks are dense but detailed, micro-contrast makes materials and edges crisp, and product colours are matched exactly to the real item across every shot. Specular highlights are kept glossy, and an optional, single diagonal light sweep on hero product shots adds that premium 'gleam'. It reads as expensive, controlled and trustworthy.

### F10 Premium Commercial Finish

- **When:** Applies to the whole video
- **Layer / track:** Whole composition
- **What it does:** Delivers clean neutral whites, crisp micro-contrast, polished speculars and exact, repeatable colour — product-ad polish with a subtle specular sweep on hero shots.
- **Parameters:** Grade strength (mix): 90%; Contrast: 16%; Saturation: 108%; White point target: 98%; Micro-contrast / sharpening: 30%; Specular sweep on hero shots: on; Protect skin tones: on; Look LUT (.cube, optional): (not set)
- **Steps:**
  1. Apply the Premium Commercial grade as one global adjustment across the entire 1080×1920 @ 30fps timeline — every camera shot and b-roll clip gets identical settings; no shot is graded in isolation.
  2. Neutralise every shot to a measured reference: white balance on a white/grey card or the brightest neutral surface until R=G=B within 1%; set whites to 98 IRE exactly (gain), blacks at 0.5–1 IRE (dense but with shadow detail), skin 62–72 IRE. If a product image or the real product colour (hex) is available, match the product's on-screen colour to it within ΔE < 3 on every shot.
  3. Look (strength 90%): contrast +14% with a clean S-curve curves=master='0/0.005 0.2/0.17 0.5/0.5 0.8/0.84 1/0.98', saturation 107% (huesaturation=saturation=0.07:colors=r+g+c+b+m — skip y so skin stays put), micro-contrast 30%: unsharp=luma_msize_x=5:luma_msize_y=5:luma_amount=0.36 (luma only, no chroma sharpening, no halos). FFmpeg chain: -vf "eq=contrast=1.144,curves=master='0/0.005 0.2/0.17 0.5/0.5 0.8/0.84 1/0.98',huesaturation=saturation=0.07:colors=r+g+c+b+m,unsharp=5:5:0.36:5:5:0".
  4. Protect skin: sample the median skin colour from a well-exposed cheek/forehead area (hex, e.g. 0xC08A6E — use the real sample) and build a hue-qualified matte around the ~20–30° skin hue range: [pre]colorkey=color=<skinHex>:similarity=0.16:blend=0.10,alphaextract,negate,gblur=sigma=4[skinMatte]; [balanced][skinMatte]alphamerge,colorchannelmixer=aa=0.8[skin]; [graded][skin]overlay=format=auto. This keeps faces on the vectorscope skin line (≈123°) while the look shifts everything else. Check the matte is soft-edged and does not catch wood, sand or orange props; tighten similarity if it does.
  5. Specular sweep: only on the product's hero shot(s) — not on talking-head shots — animate one diagonal (105°) soft white band (12–18% of frame width, peak 35% opacity, mixBlendMode "soft-light" or FFmpeg blend=all_mode=softlight) across the product over 24 frames with an ease-in-out curve, masked to the product silhouette when a product cutout/mask exists. Once per hero shot, never looping.
  6. No LUT supplied — build the look from the numeric controls below (they are the complete recipe). Never download or embed third-party LUTs; if the user later supplies a .cube file, apply it with lut3d=file=…:interp=tetrahedral before these trims. Remotion approximation (motion-graphics plates, previews, or when FFmpeg is not in the pipeline): wrap each footage <OffthreadVideo> in an <AbsoluteFill style={{ filter: "contrast(1.144) saturate(1.072) brightness(1.03)" }}>. CSS filters run in 8-bit display space, so for the final master prefer rendering the FFmpeg-graded footage and using that file in Remotion. Pipeline order: source footage → primary balance → this grade → captions, titles, logos and UI graphics composited on top. Do not run brand graphics through the grade; #D7FF5F and the caption whites must render exactly as specified. Screen recordings and supplied screenshots get at most 30% of the grade strength so text and interface colours stay faithful.
  7. Verify before export: grab one frame per shot (ffmpeg -ss <t> -i graded.mp4 -frames:v 1 shotN.png) and inspect ffmpeg -i shotN.png -vf "split[a][b];[a]waveform=mode=column:graticule=green:flags=numbers[w];[b]vectorscope=mode=color3:graticule=green[v];[w][v]hstack" scope.png. Whites at 98 IRE on neutral surfaces with R=G=B on the parade, product colour within ΔE < 3 of the reference, no sharpening halos at 200% zoom. Shots must match each other within ±2 IRE in skin exposure and ±3° in skin hue; fix outliers with a per-shot exposure/white-balance trim before the look, never by changing the look itself. Export at 1080×1920, 30fps, 10-bit or high-bitrate 8-bit with -pix_fmt yuv420p to avoid banding in gradients and skies.
- **Assets:** Source video (⚠ MISSING) · optional: LUT file, Product image
- **Fallback:** Without a product reference, match product colour across shots to the best-exposed shot rather than to an absolute value, and say so. If the footage is noisy, keep sharpening ≤ 15% so noise is not amplified.

**G. Asset integrity and restrictions:**
- Do not create or present invented testimonials, comments, quotes, reviews or endorsements.
- Do not fabricate numbers, metrics, charts or results. Data visualisations use only the supplied data.
- Do not create fake screenshots or UI and present them as real. Illustrative graphics must be clearly illustrative.
- Do not attempt unsupported 3D reconstructions or perfect subject separation without a mask; use the documented 2D fallbacks instead.

**I. Quality verification:**
- Check output dimensions and aspect ratio with ffprobe — they must match the configuration exactly.
- Check the output frame rate and that the duration is within ±0.5 s of the target.
- Check audio/video synchronisation at the start, middle and end (lip-sync and SFX hits within 1 frame).
- Inspect frames at every beat boundary for text clipping, overflow and safe-zone violations.
- Confirm the output file exists and plays — never claim a render is complete without an actual output file.
- Report any missing asset or unimplemented effect explicitly.
